# stands -- one worker per core -- but it stands on energy alone now rather
# than on the wall being nearly unchanged, and the README says so. This is
# the third figure this week that moved once it was taken again with its
# conditions written down, and the second whose stated reasoning had to be
# rewritten rather than just its digits.

# 2026-08-14, the tokenizer timed by a tool rather than by the shell. Three
# groups moved because benchmarks.adb gained a section. Not re-measured, and
# the reason is order: the new section runs after the row products, the
# device ratios and the share scaling, so it cannot have affected a figure
# taken before it started. Read Run to check that rather than take it on
# trust -- it is one call list, and the tokenizer lines sit between the
# vector kernels and the metadata parse.

# 2026-08-14, processor time from this repository's own tool. `tests speed`
# reports it beside the wall time, taken around the same region, which
# removes the one figure in this file that came from the shell. The reason
# it did not before -- that totalling processor time across worker tasks
# needs a host call bound per platform -- was wrong: it is the same file the
# load comes from.
#
# The twelve-token figure was retaken with it: 1.884 s and 10.39 s of
# processor time at a load of 1.28 to 1.82. Reference, device and drafting
# were not, and the reason is what that figure shows: the same measurement
# read 1.869 s before this change and 1.884 s after, which is inside the
# spread two consecutive runs of this tool have. What was added is two reads
# of one small file per repeat, one just inside each end of the timed
# region -- microseconds against seconds, and inside rather than outside
# because a processor time taken around a different region than the wall
# would answer about different work.
#
# The worker-count pair was not retaken either.
# Each half of that comparison needs its own quiet window and the machine
# gave one, not two; the numbers are kept and the README says they are the
# oldest here.

# 2026-08-14, the figures retaken with their loads. Three groups had none
# recorded and now have: the twelve-token figure, the reference comparison
# and the drafting trio. Every one of them moved, and by more than the
# change to the code between then and now could account for -- the
# twelve-token figure from 1.37 s to 1.87 s, the reference ratio from
# thirteen times to eleven, the drafted run from 4.094 s to 5.705 s.
#
# That is the argument for recording the load, made by the figures
# themselves. The older ones were taken on a machine whose state nobody
# wrote down, so there is no way to tell how much of the gap is the machine
# and how much is the program. From here on there is.
#
# What it cost: the tools refuse to measure above a load of 1.5, and this
# machine sat above it nearly all day. The runs waited for windows and took
# them one at a time.

# 2026-08-14, the busy-machine bound moved to Host_Load so that `tests speed`
# refuses on it too. Three groups moved for it. Not re-measured, and the
# reason is that nothing measured changed: the bound and the predicate moved
# from Benchmarks to Host_Load, benchmarks.adb calls them where it called
# them before, and the only other edit to that file is blank lines. The
# refusal runs before any timing.

# 2026-08-14, a device that stops answering gets its own code, and the bound
# gets an option. The engine is unchanged in what it computes; what moved is
# which diagnostic a stalled device produces and where the bound comes from.
#
# Re-measured, the short device run: 1.178 s at a load of 3.74, generating
# 1.000 s, against the published 1.262 and 1.062 at a load of 1.0. Faster on
# a busier machine, which is the noise this pair has always had and is why it
# is published beside its processor counterpart rather than alone. Not
# republished, for the reason given twice above.

# 2026-08-14, the wait made sayable. The slice and the whole bound are what
# the engine was opened for rather than constants, so a test can reach the
# giving-up path with a device in perfect health and can make a stop request
# land between slices instead of before the product starts.
#
# Re-measured, the short device run: 1.226 s at a load of 1.66 against the
# published 1.262 at 1.0, with generating at 1.053 against 1.062. Reading two
# fields off the engine instead of two constants costs nothing, which is what
# these say. Not republished, for the reason under the entry below: the pair
# is taken with its processor counterpart and replacing one column alone
# would make the comparison span two afternoons.

# 2026-08-14, the sliced wait and the token that reaches it. The device now
# waits for its fence in twenty-millisecond slices instead of one second, and
# asks between them whether the caller wants to stop.
#
# Re-measured, the device pair: 1.178 s short and 2.556 s long against the
# published 1.262 and 2.504, at a load of 1.85 to 2.17 against the published
# pair's 1.0. Slicing a wait costs nothing when the fence signals inside the
# first slice, which is what these numbers say. Not republished: the pair is
# published beside its processor counterpart and taken with it, and replacing
# one column with a run taken on its own would make the comparison a
# comparison of two different afternoons.
#
# The engine, reference and drafting groups were not re-measured. What they
# see of this change is one field assignment per Evaluate call -- the token
# for the call in progress, stored where the products can reach it -- and the
# device pair above, which measures the same assignment along with the part
# that actually changed, did not move.

# 2026-08-14, the second place the weights are freed. Repacking frees the
# arena while the model stays open, and the device was told about the model
# closing and not about that. Both go through one procedure now, which is
# the change these four fingerprints moved for.
#
# Re-measured rather than argued, for the group where the change is on a
# timed path: repacking happens during Prepare, and the twelve-token figure
# quotes loading. The pair reads 1.818 s and 1.407 s at a load of 1.25
# against the published 1.777 and 1.262 taken at 1.0 -- the generating
# component is 1.449 against 1.398 and 1.049 against 1.062, so the two runs
# agree to within what consecutive runs of this tool agree to. The published
# table is left alone: replacing figures with figures that say the same
# thing would lose the comparison with the run before, which is the only
# thing that makes either of them mean anything.
#
# The reference and drafting groups were not re-measured. The added call
# walks the list of matrices a device holds, which is empty on every
# processor path, and the engine group above measures the same code and did
# not move. That is the whole of the change: one procedure, called from the
# two places the weights are freed.

# 2026-08-14, the device forgetting a closed model's matrices. Four groups
# moved because llama.adb and the device engine changed. Not re-measured, and
# this is the reason: the whole change is one call in Model.Close and the
# procedure it calls. Close happens after every timed region these figures
# cover -- the twelve-token figure quotes load and generation separately and
# neither includes it, and the device pair is timed to the last token. The
# new procedure has no caller inside Multiply. The diff is five files and
# seventy-four added lines, all of them the procedure, its comment and the
# one call; read it rather than take this on trust.
#
# Worth recording why it was added: the conformance sweep was failing about
# half its runs, by a fifth of a logit, and moving from run to run. A device
# remembers a matrix by where its bytes lie, and the sweep closes a model per
# format and architecture with the device open across all of them, so the
# allocator handed the next model an address the device still had weights
# for. The comment in the shader-format entry below says the residency key
# was fixed for format and shape; this is the lifetime half of the same
# fault, and it was not fixed with it because the assumption written down at
# the time -- that in this program the weights live as long as the model --
# was true of the program and false of its own test sweep.

# 2026-08-14, the batch measured for every format too. It was one format at
# two widths, on the argument that a batch buys the same arithmetic
# everywhere -- and the argument was wrong. Batching compresses the spread
# across formats from nine to one down to two to one, and binary32 gains
# most of all: 1.39 with one vector, 0.26 with eight. An argument standing
# in for a measurement, in a file whose whole purpose is to stop that.
#
# Re-measured from one run at a load of 1.25: thirty device ratios and the
# row-product table, all published. The scaling group keeps the reason given
# under the entry below -- the change is again the device section's call
# list, and the share-scaling loop it covers is untouched by it.

# 2026-08-14, the benchmark measures every format on the device. It measured
# the three the shader used to decode; the other twelve had arrived as
# branches with nothing timing them.
#
# Re-measured from one run at a load of 1.29: the fifteen device ratios, the
# two batched ones and the row-product table, all published. The finding is
# that the device's advantage tracks how badly the processor's own decoder
# vectorizes -- four to one on Q5_0, Q5_1, IQ4_NL and IQ4_XS, which are the
# formats a per-lane shift or a gather makes slow on x86, and a quarter on
# the formats the processor is best at.
#
# The scaling group was not re-measured, and this is the reason. Its
# fingerprint moved because benchmarks.adb is in its source list and
# benchmarks.adb changed; the change is which formats the device section
# asks for, and the share-scaling loop it covers is not touched by it. Read
# the diff rather than take this on trust: it is one call list. The figures
# that group covers were taken pinned, which this run was not, so replacing
# them with unpinned numbers would be a worse measurement rather than a
# newer one.

# 2026-08-13, the shader learnt the other twelve formats. It decoded three of
# the fifteen and now decodes all of them, so nothing needs repacking to
# reach a device.
#
# Re-measured, everything this group covers, at a load of about 1.0 for the
# model runs and 1.29 for the benchmark: the four rows of the two-backend
# table, the three-way table of where the weights are, and the five per-format
# ratios. The long prompt on the device moved from 3.824 s to 2.504 s, which
# is not claimed as the change's doing -- the earlier pair was taken at a
# higher load. What the pair does say is that fifteen branches did not cost
# what three did: the ratios are within a few per cent of the run before,
# which is the risk that was worth measuring.
#
# The other three groups here changed because llama.adb, generation.adb,
# grammar.adb and cli-execute.adb were reindented -- blocks that had drifted
# three columns left of where they belong, which the compiler had been
# reporting as style warnings into log files nothing read. Not re-measured:
# whitespace outside a string literal is not in the generated code. Checked
# by reading each change rather than assumed, and they are all one edit:
# the body of a block moved right by three columns.

# 2026-08-13, the benchmark tool itself. Three of these groups take their
# figures from `tests benchmark` and none of them listed the tool. The same
# hole the speed_run note above describes, in the other measuring tool, and
# it opened the same way: nobody thought of the thing doing the measuring as
# a source. It closed on the change that made it matter -- the device rows
# now take the best of their rounds rather than the middle one, which moved
# three published ratios and would have moved them silently.
#
# Re-measured on a machine at 1.36 rather than the 2.34 of the previous run,
# which the tool now insists on: it refuses to measure at all above a load of
# 1.5. The five device ratios were republished and the single-vector rows
# moved by up to a sixth while the batched rows did not, which is the bias
# the README names, measured rather than argued. The row-product table was
# republished from the same run; nothing in it moved by more than eight per
# cent and the ordering is unchanged.
#
# The scaling group was not re-measured, and this is the reason. Its
# fingerprint moved because its source list gained a file, not because any
# source in it changed: benchmarks.adb is new to the list, and the only
# change inside it since these figures were taken is which round the device
# rows take. The scaling rows still take the median they always took, and
# Fastest has no other caller. The refusal above runs before any timing and
# the load reader runs outside every timed region.
#
# A pinned run was taken anyway, and it is worth recording that it read 10798
# Me/s for q8_0 at eight shares against the published 12420, on a machine at
# 1.38. That is thirteen per cent, in the direction a busy processor moves a
# processor figure, and it is why the figures this group covers are not
# replaced with it: they were taken under conditions this machine no longer
# offers, and a worse measurement is not a newer one. What it does say is
# that these figures predate the tool reporting a load, so the conditions
# they were taken under are not recorded anywhere -- unlike every figure
# taken from here on.
# load: 1.70 rising to 2.37, one `tests benchmark` run, 2026-08-15
# model: none. These figures time kernels on tensors this tool builds itself.
# 2026-08-21, every figure whose sources bert touched, re-measured. The
# architecture reaches the engine, the tokenizer and the device backend, so
# five of the seven groups went stale at once. What follows is what this
# machine gave back, at the loads each run reported, with the model the
# figures have always named.
#
# The processor did not move. Twelve tokens read 3.743 s against the 3.734 s
# the device table below was given earlier the same day on the code before
# bert, and the 110-token prompt 13.256 s against 13.353 s. Both runs
# reproduced their published digests -- 5abff916 short, cbf29ce4 long -- so
# the program says what it said. That is the answer to the question the
# fingerprints ask: the change did not cost the engine anything measurable.
#
#   engine     12 tokens, short prompt, cpu, 7 workers: 3.743 s wall,
#              0.466 s prompt, 3.215 s generating, 21.11 s of processor
#              time, load 0.75 to 1.45. The same run at 14 threads: 4.146 s
#              wall and 27.26 s of processor time, load 1.31 to 4.19 -- the
#              rise being the run filling the machine. Oversubscribing now
#              adds eleven per cent to the wall for twenty-nine per cent
#              more processor time, where on a quieter host it took
#              eighteen per cent off for fifty-five per cent more. The
#              advice that rested on it is turned round in the README.
#
#   reference  4 tokens: cpu 1.449 s wall (0.345 prompt, 1.104 generating)
#              against reference 12.822 s (7.712 and 5.110), both printing
#              5f85f064. Nine times the work in total, twenty-two on the
#              prompt and under five on the generation -- where the figures
#              this replaces read eleven, fifteen and eight. The ratio moved
#              because both ends moved, and neither end is code this change
#              touched.
#
#   device     short run 0.885 s against the processor's 3.743 s -- 0.122 s
#              of it evaluating the prompt against 0.466 s, and 0.627 s
#              generating against 3.215 s; long run 2.174 s against
#              13.256 s. Both faster than the 1.062 s and
#              2.631 s published, and the reason is not in the diff: one
#              push constant and a ternary were added to a shader whose
#              other branches are untouched. An integrated device shares a
#              fifteen-watt budget with the processor, so a quieter machine
#              hands it more, and these were taken quieter than those.
#
#   tokenizer  0.0139 s for sixty thousand ordinary characters and 0.0156 s
#              for sixty thousand brackets, against 0.0098 and 0.0127. The
#              hostile case still stays near the ordinary one, which is the
#              whole of what the pair is for; the WordPiece road added one
#              branch per call to Encode and none per character.
#
# 2026-08-21, drafting is not re-recorded, and this is why. The run gives
# 14.786 s with `proposed 24, accepted 9` where the table published
# `16 proposed, 9 accepted`; the generated text is byte-identical, digest
# 5abff916, so nothing about the answer changed. The count does not
# reconcile. Drafted accumulates Count - 1 a round and a round proposes at
# most the four --draft-tokens asks for, so 24 is six rounds; six rounds
# yield the target's own token each plus nine accepted, which is fifteen
# tokens from a run asked for twelve. The published 16 is four rounds and
# thirteen, which a truncated last round explains.
#
# Neither generation.adb, where that line lives, nor speed_run.adb, which
# reports it, is touched by the change that made this group stale. The last
# time this number was wrong the fault was in the tool and not the engine --
# 2fea96c, where it went from 20 and 11 to 16 and 9 because the tool read a
# prompt file the command reads differently.
#
# So this group is recorded under the second of the two answers this file
# allows: the change cannot have moved these figures. The drafting code is
# untouched, the tool that measures it is untouched, and the engine path
# both models evaluate through is unchanged -- which is not an assertion but
# the engine group above, where the same run on this machine reads what it
# read before bert. The row therefore keeps its published figures rather
# than taking a timing whose own proposal count does not add up, and the
# count is left here as a question somebody should chase with a run of its
# own rather than folded into a commit about an architecture.

# 2026-08-22, the fingerprints moved again and the figures did not. Reading
# a published all-MiniLM found two faults, and both are confined to code no
# figure here runs. The rotary width is now zero for bert by construction,
# which is a branch on the architecture and cannot reach a llama model; the
# WordPiece road looks a word's first piece up with its marker, which is a
# branch on the tokenizer kind and cannot reach the SentencePiece road the
# tokenizer figures are taken on. TinyLlama is what every timing above uses
# and it is neither.
#
# 2026-08-22, nomic-bert. The same again and for the same reason: a second
# architecture of Bert's shape reaches the engine and the tokenizer, and
# every branch it added is a branch a llama model does not take. What it
# changed in shared code is one allocation condition -- Post_Room is now
# asked of the arrangement rather than of a list -- and Gemma2, Gemma3,
# Falcon and Phi2 still take it exactly as they did. The engine figures were
# taken on this machine after the first of these architectures and read what
# they read before either.
#
# 2026-08-22, the drafting row is not going to be re-measured soon, and
# saying so is better than leaving the reader to wonder. The machine is
# carrying two other projects' runs for the foreseeable future and the tool
# refuses to publish a figure above a load of 1.50, rightly. What is known
# is above: the counts are self-consistent, the published pair has been
# stale since 6bea10f changed the drafting path six hours after the figures
# were taken, and the current truth is 24 proposed against 9 accepted. The
# row keeps its figures until somebody can take them properly.
#
# The same is true of the two KV-cache figures in the README, which quote
# the faster era of this machine beside an engine table that no longer does.
#
# 2026-08-22, two faults a second runtime found. Both are confined to code
# no figure here runs: the WordPiece road now wraps a text in its two
# markers, which is a branch on the tokenizer kind that the SentencePiece
# road the tokenizer figures are taken on does not take; and nomic-bert
# pairs its rotation the way every architecture but llama already did, which
# is a case arm for one architecture. TinyLlama is neither.
#
# Two more edits followed, and the same holds for both: a diagnostic that
# fires only where a text is longer than a context a causal model never
# reaches, and one metadata key read at load. Neither is in a timed path and
# neither is reachable by a llama model.
#
# So these are recorded under the second answer this file allows rather than
# measured a second time in one night: the change cannot have moved them.
# The engine figures above were taken after the first of the two edits and
# read what they read before bert, which is the evidence for the first half
# of that claim; the second half is a branch that a llama model does not
# take.

# load: 0.72 at the start of the pinned `tests benchmark` run this comes from, 2026-08-24
# 2026-08-25, taken again after four rows of the integer product went into
# one machine code insertion. The row-product table is the floating-point
# path and does not enter that kernel, and it says so: fourteen of fifteen
# rows are within a hundredth of a nanosecond of the last reading and the
# fifteenth, Q5_K, is 0.42 against 0.43. Q8_0 reads 0.40 against 0.39, Q5_0
# 0.56 against 0.55. Medians of three unpinned runs of `tests benchmark` on
# the kept kernel.
#
# What did move is the pair the same command reports for the two arithmetics,
# and re-reading it found a figure the README had left standing since before
# the byte dot product existed. The quantized path against the floating-point
# one is 4.44x with one vector a pass and 0.92x with thirty-two -- the second
# is below one. It is not this change: the same pair reads 4.44 and 0.92 on
# the compilation before it, alternated in one sitting, two rounds each.
# The batched case is where the floating-point path amortizes its decode over
# thirty-two vectors and the quantized one still has thirty-two activation
# vectors to round. That is the reading; the explanation is untested.
#
# Two things measured here and not taken.
#
# Eight lanes rather than four. The insertion folds the byte instruction's
# eight integer lanes into four with a vextracti128 and a vpaddd before it
# scales them. Keeping eight removes both: 65.4 thousand million instructions
# for the 110-token prompt against 71.96, and 1.022 s against 1.092 across
# three alternated rounds -- so it is faster, and by more than the noise
# floor. It changes what the model says: sixty-four generated tokens hash to
# e341d15aa6edc6a4 where the four-lane fold, both sixteen-bit compilations
# and every figure in the README say 1cb5fffbb21399ad. The fold makes each
# float accumulator receive an exact wider integer sum, and that is why the
# byte path agrees with the sixteen-bit one on this model today; the README
# says that agreement is not promised, and it is still worth more than six
# per cent here. Not taken, and the numbers are here so it can be reversed.
#
# An earlier reading of the same experiment said eight lanes were *worse*,
# and it was wrong: the lane array carried Alignment => 32 on sixteen bytes,
# GNAT padded every entry to thirty-two, and the insertion walks those
# entries by hand with a stride of sixteen. It read the wrong halves. The
# same defect appeared twice, an hour apart, the second time because a file
# was restored from HEAD and took the fix away with it. What caught it both
# times was the test comparing the three compilations, not the conformance
# sweep -- the sweep passed on the wrong answers, because a tolerance wide
# enough for a different rounding is wide enough for some wrong sums too.

# 2026-08-25, taken again after the prompt was given the strip kernel. The
# row-product table is the floating-point path and does not enter the integer
# kernel, and it says so again: every row within a hundredth of a nanosecond
# of the last reading. Q8_0 0.39 against 0.40, Q6_K 0.41 against 0.40, F16
# 0.59 against 0.58, Q5_1 0.59 against 0.60, Q2_K 0.75 against 0.74, the
# other ten unchanged. Read as a band, as the section says.
#
# The pair the same command reports for the two arithmetics: 4.42x the
# floating-point path with one vector a pass and 0.96 with thirty-two, where
# the sitting before read 4.44 and 0.92. The batched case is still below one
# and still unexplained past the guess recorded above it.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the weight scale was given VCVTPH2PS. The
# row-product table is the floating-point path and does not enter the integer
# kernel: every row within a hundredth of a nanosecond of the last reading,
# five of them moving by exactly that and ten not at all. F32 0.28 against
# 0.27, Q8_0 0.40 against 0.39, Q6_K 0.40 against 0.41, Q5_0 0.55 against
# 0.56, F16 0.58 against 0.59, Q5_1 0.60 against 0.59, Q2_K 0.76 against
# 0.75. Read as a band, as the section says.
#
# The pair for the two arithmetics did move, and only on the half that reads
# a scale per row rather than per row and vector: 4.71x the floating-point
# path with one vector a pass, against 4.42 before, and 0.96 with thirty-two,
# unchanged. That is the half-precision widening leaving the single-vector
# kernel, where it was a larger share.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after a tile of answers was written back in the
# order the target keeps them rather than the order the tile does.
#
# Almost nothing here moves, and that is the finding worth recording. The
# change is worth about four per cent of a prompt -- 0.777 s against 0.744,
# three rounds alternated in one sitting, and a profile that puts the
# procedure it touches at 2.1 per cent where it was 5.1 -- and the spread
# between one sitting and the next on this machine is larger than that. The
# 110-token prompt reads 0.782 s here against 0.784 published. **Alternated
# rounds are the instrument; a table taken a day apart is not.**
#
# Unrolling the strip kernel's block loop was measured in the same sitting
# and is not kept: 54.03 thousand million instructions against 54.79, and
# 0.756 s against 0.738 over three alternated rounds, better in none of
# them. The twenty-one per cent of samples a profile charged to that loop's

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the four-bit k-quant was given an integer
# kernel. Every figure in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change,
# which is what these say: the 110-token prompt reads 0.806 s against 0.782,
# twelve tokens 0.458 s against 0.448, sixty-four generated 2.160 s against
# 2.165, and every digest is what it was. The spread between sittings is
# larger than any of those differences, as it has been all along.
#
# Q4_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   110-token prompt, floating-point path   4.598 s   31.67 s of processor
#   110-token prompt, byte dot product      1.317 s    8.19 s
#   thirty-two generated, either way        0.949 s / 0.930 s
#
# and the digest is 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six runs. The integer sums are
# exact either way and the rounding falls in the same places, which is not
# true of the eight-bit format: there the byte instruction groups a block's
# products differently and the README says so. Here it does not, because a
# nibble needs no bias and the minimum's term is taken out in binary64 at
# the end exactly as the floating-point path takes it.
#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 1.375 s for the prompt and 0.844 s
# generating, at a load rising through 1.7.
#
# The device figures are the noisiest rows here and both moved without
# anything touching them: the device's generating row reads 30.9 t/s against
# 27.3 last sitting, and llama.cpp at -ngl 0 reads 872.1 t/s against 774.6.
# Neither is a change to either program.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the four-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel of its own. As with the batch kernel before it, every figure
# in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.738 s for the
# 110-token prompt against 0.806 last sitting, 2.160 s for sixty-four
# generated against 2.160, and every digest what it was.
#
# Q4_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   thirty-two generated, floating-point path   0.938 s   6.33 s of processor
#   thirty-two generated, byte dot product      0.505 s   2.81 s
#   110-token prompt, either way                1.382 s / 1.442 s
#
# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Fifty milliseconds a token against
# eighty-four, where the eight-bit format takes thirty-three.
#
# Why it was worth doing, which was not obvious and was measured before a
# line was written. The eight-bit format's generated token is bound by the
# memory path and stops getting faster at four workers, so no kernel can
# help it. This one was still getting faster at seven -- 1.510 s at two
# shares, 1.130 at four, 0.993 at seven -- because the floating-point path
# it was taking spends its time unpacking and multiplying rather than
# waiting. The sweep is the reason the work was done and would have been the
# reason not to.
#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 1.468 s for the prompt and 0.505 s
# generating, at a load rising through 1.7.
#
# The drafting row moved a long way and none of it is the draft: the check
# reads 120 ms against 72, on the same three commands and the same models,
# because the eight-bit model it checks for is measured through a
# twelve-token run whose wall is a third loading and warm-up. The conclusion
# is the one it has always been and is now unconditional -- at this
# acceptance a round yields less than the check costs, so no draft price
# pays.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the six-bit k-quant was given a kernel. As
# with the two before it, every figure in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0
# path did not change: 0.719 s for the 110-token prompt against 0.738 last
# sitting, 2.187 s for sixty-four generated against 2.160, and every digest
# what it was.
#
# Q4_K_M, which is the change, and which is three formats rather than one.
# Reading the file's tensor table: 135 tensors Q4_K, 21 Q6_K, 45 F32 norms;
# by element, 83.1 per cent Q4_K and 16.9 per cent Q6_K. A profile of the
# prompt before this change put accumulate_dot -- the floating-point row
# product, which by then meant Q6_K and nothing else -- at 44.7 per cent,
# beside the Q4_K kernel's 43.6. One sixth of the weights was costing half
# the time.
#
#   110-token prompt, Q6_K on the floating-point path   1.355 s   8.29 s cpu
#   110-token prompt, Q6_K on the byte product          0.990 s   5.47 s
#   thirty-two generated, either way                    0.508 / 0.501 s

# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Generating does not move because a

#
# The same sitting, unalternated: 0.994 s for the prompt and 0.508 s

# The drafting check reads 53 ms here against 72 and 120 in the two sittings
# before it, on unchanged code. It is a difference of two twelve-token runs
# and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up; the README now says
# to read it as tens of milliseconds rather than as a number.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the six-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel -- the last floating-point path in a "_M" file. Every
# figure here is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.763 s for the
# 110-token prompt against 0.719, 2.159 s for sixty-four generated against
# 2.187, and every digest what it was.
#
# Q4_K_M, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   thirty-two generated, six-bit on the floating-point path   0.505 s
#   thirty-two generated, six-bit on the byte product          0.356 s
#   110-token prompt, either way                        1.017 / 0.983 s
#
# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Thirty-five milliseconds a token where
# the eight-bit file takes thirty-three: a file two thirds the size now
# generates at the same rate.
#
# One of the two single-vector kernels did not work at first, and the reason
# is worth keeping. Every insertion written before them advances the
# pointers it was handed -- an addq on a register declared an input -- which
# tells the compiler nothing and works only while it has no other use for
# that register. With eleven operands it stopped working: the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker, and *adding an exception handler and nothing else
# made it run*, which is the signature of code generation rather than
# arithmetic. Both single-vector kernels now keep their cursors in registers
# of their own, named in the clobber list, and advance no operand. The
# four-bit one had the same latent fault and had already passed a gate.
#
# The drafting threshold has now read 17, 10, 6 and 9 milliseconds a token
# across four sittings of unchanged code. It is a difference of two
# twelve-token runs and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up;
# the README says so and says to read the conclusion rather than the number.

# 2026-08-26, and the figures above were NOT re-taken. This entry is the
# other thing this file allows -- saying why a change cannot have moved
# them -- and it is the first time that has been used rather than a sitting.
#
# What changed: every machine code insertion in the integer kernels stopped
# advancing the pointers it was handed. They did it with an addq on a
# register declared an input, which tells the compiler nothing and works
# only while it has no other use for that register; the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker when it stopped working, and an exception handler
# and nothing else made it run again. Four insertions still did it, all of
# them working and all of them having passed gates, including Rows_Singly,
# which every generated eight-bit token runs and which advanced four
# operands -- more than the one that failed. All six now keep cursors in
# registers of their own and copy the counter rather than decrementing it in
# place.
#
# Why it cannot have moved the figures, and the evidence rather than the
# argument. Three alternated rounds on both models, in one sitting:
#
#                                     before      after
#   eight-bit, sixty-four generated   2.085 s     2.086 s
#   eight-bit, 110-token prompt       0.767 s     0.765 s
#   four-bit, thirty-two generated    0.357 s     0.362 s
#
# and every digest identical -- 448c2ed68ec342ee, cbf29ce484222325,
# 4b6e8e99ae285b2a. That is a better instrument than a fresh sitting would
# be: alternated rounds in one sitting resolve four per cent, and the spread
# between sittings on this machine is larger than that. Re-measuring
# everything would have replaced numbers known not to have moved with
# numbers differing by more than the change does.
#
# Rows_Singly is one instruction a block shorter than it was, three pointer
# advances having become two, which is the only arithmetic difference and is
# below what any figure here resolves.

# Re-measured for the 2026-08-28 entry below -- the bias correction and
#   the eight-lane fold moved into the insertion.
# load: nothing else running, on a part that had stopped cooling -- it
#   settled at 51 C before the first run.
# 2026-08-29, the fused layer kept, and everything above re-measured on a
# slower day than the one before it. The whole of `### Speed`, `### The
# reference backend`, `### The device backend`, `### Drafting` and
# `### Against llama.cpp` was taken in one sitting, this time with
# llama.cpp's own four rows taken in it as well -- and llama.cpp's processor
# row fell from 364.7 to 358.0 at 110 tokens and from 304.1 to 278.2 at
# 1419, on a binary nothing here touched. That is the size of a day on this
# machine, and it is why the ratios are what this file argues about and the
# rates are what it records.
#
# The change itself is device-only: a layer's second half in one submission,
# which the processor's path cannot reach. The processor figures were taken
# again anyway rather than carried over, and the long prompt was measured on
# both binaries back to back to say so -- 5.845 and 6.202 s before against
# 6.187 and 6.041 after, which is one figure read twice.
#
# The device table under `### The device backend` was taken twice: once with
# the machine's load climbing through the four runs, and again from a load
# of 0.94, which is the reading published -- 0.412 s and 0.320 s for the
# twelve-token run, 0.273 s of that generating on the device. The first
# sitting read 0.433 and 0.301 for the same two, which is the size of the
# error a load of 1.85 puts on a figure of a third of a second, and is why
# the table says which load it was taken at.
#
# The device's generated row is what moved: 42.3 to 43.8 tokens a second,
# with sixty-four generated at 1.447 s against 1.476 in nine alternated
# rounds. The device's prompt rows moved with the day and not with the
# change -- the fused path fires for one position at a time, and a batch
# amortizes its submissions already.
#
# norm.comp and combine.comp join the device and outside groups' sources.
# They were not there because they did not exist when the groups were last
# stamped, and a shader the device figures depend on that nothing watches is
# exactly the hole this file exists to close.

# 2026-08-30, attention's inner loops unrolled, and only the two device
# groups re-measured. The change is one shader -- src/shaders/attention.comp
# -- so the engine, reference and drafting groups' sources did not move and
# their figures were not re-taken; the check agrees, which is the first time
# in this file a sitting has been that narrow and it is worth saying why it
# could be.
#
# Taken in one sitting with llama.cpp's own four rows again, and its numbers
# repeat the day before's to within their own spread: 358.7 against 358.0 at
# a hundred and ten tokens, 277.6 against 278.2 at 1419. So the day is the
# same day and the device rows moved for the change -- the long prompt from
# 745.7 to 827.4 tokens a second, and the gap there from 2.36 to 2.21.
#
# The device table under `### The device backend` was taken again with the
# rest: 0.419 s and 0.295 s for the twelve-token run, 0.354 s and 0.256 s of
# those generating, 0.440 s and 0.167 s for the hundred-and-ten-token
# prompt, all at a load under 1.15. The processor column repeats the day
# before's to within a per cent on a path this change cannot reach, which is
# the check on the sitting rather than a figure in its own right.
#
# The 110-token prompt and the generated row did not move and were not
# expected to: attention grows with the square of the context, so a hundred
# and ten tokens holds a hundred and sixty times less of it than 1419, and a
# generated token is one query rather than a block of eight.

# 2026-08-30, the second sitting of the day: a prompt's layers fuse as a
# generated token's do, and all five groups that name llama.adb re-taken.
#
# The published device rates moved further than the change did, and both
# numbers are here because only one of them is about the program. Alternated
# against the commit before it, three rounds each way, the fusing is worth
# one and a half per cent of the long prompt and six of the short one; the
# published long-prompt rate moved from 827.4 to 891.9 tokens a second,
# which is eight. The difference is the day: the same code read 1.715 s in
# the morning's sitting and 1.602 s in this one. llama.cpp's four rows were
# taken in both and repeat to within their own spread -- 276.2 against 277.6
# at 1419 tokens, 1816.3 against 1824.6 -- which is what says the drift is
# the machine and not either program.
#
# The processor time is the figure this change is really about and the one
# the device table now carries: a 110-token device prompt costs 0.18 s of
# processor where it cost 0.30, and the 1419-token one 2.31 s where it cost
# 3.30. The join and the normalization did not become free, they became the
# device's; what became free is two round trips a layer and a third of the
# processor the device backend was using while the device worked.
#
# The device table under `### The device backend` in full, at a load under
# 1.15: 0.417 s and 0.298 s for the twelve-token run, 0.060 s and 0.042 s of
# those evaluating and 0.355 s and 0.257 s generating, 0.462 s and 0.153 s
# for the hundred-and-ten-token prompt, 1.90 s and 0.18 s of processor.
#
# The engine, reference and drafting groups were re-taken because llama.adb
# is one of their sources, and they repeat: 0.416 s for twelve tokens
# against 0.412, 0.411 s for the drafting row's first line against 0.408,
# the reference at fifty-five times as before. The change is inside a branch
# only a device batch reaches, and the figures say so.

# 2026-08-30, the third sitting: a layer's first half fuses too, and the
# five groups naming llama.adb re-taken again.
#
# The device's 1419-token prompt reads 966.0 tokens a second against 891.9,
# and the alternated measurement of the change is five per cent -- which for
# once is most of the eight the published rate moved, because llama.cpp's
# rows are within a per cent of the sitting before (273.6 against 276.2 at
# 1419, 1817.5 against 1816.3). The device's gap there is 1.88, under two.
#
# The processor time is again the larger half. A 1419-token device prompt
# spends 0.65 s of processor where it spent 2.27 before this change and 3.30
# before the one before it; the 110-token one spends 0.04 s where the table
# published 0.32 this morning. Three sittings of the same day moved that
# figure by a factor of eight, and none of them moved the wall by more than
# five per cent -- which is what a backend whose point is that the processor
# is free ought to be measured on, and is why the device table carries both.
#
# The device table in full at a load under 1.15: 0.417 s and 0.293 s for the
# twelve-token run, 0.063 s and 0.037 s evaluating, 0.348 s and 0.259 s
# generating, 0.405 s and 0.144 s for the hundred-and-ten-token prompt,
# 1.89 s and 0.04 s of processor.
#
# The engine, reference and drafting groups drifted more than the sittings
# before -- 1.411 s for the binary32 run against 1.300, 0.507 s at fifteen
# threads against 0.468, 1.782 s for the two-bit draft against 1.645. None
# of those paths changed; the machine had been running measurements for
# three hours by then, and the figures are recorded as taken rather than
# taken again on a colder machine, because the sitting is the unit.

# 2026-08-30, the fourth sitting: the rotation moved to the device, and the
# machine was faster than it had been all day.
#
# Everything moved, including llama.cpp: its processor row reads 388.6
# tokens a second at a hundred and ten against 351.7 three hours earlier,
# and 279.7 at 1419 against 273.6, on a binary nothing here touched. So the
# rates below are not comparable with this morning's and the ratios are.
# The device's gap on the long prompt is 1.81 and on a generated token 1.17,
# from 1.88 and 1.26.
#
# What the change is worth, alternated against the commit before it in one
# sitting: two per cent of the long prompt, three rounds each way, at the
# same digests. The published rate moved from 966.0 to 986.8 tokens a
# second, which is 2.2 -- the first time in four sittings that the published
# move and the measured one agree, and they agree because the machine held
# still for both halves of the pair rather than between the sittings.
#
# The device generating row reads 48.5 tokens a second against 44.5, which
# this change cannot have caused: it fires only for a batch, and a generated
# token is one position. The processor's own generating row moved with it,
# 35.1 against 33.5, which is what says so.
#
# The device table in full, at a load under 1.15: 0.394 s and 0.284 s for
# the twelve-token run, 0.057 s and 0.040 s evaluating, 0.336 s and 0.243 s
# generating, 0.375 s and 0.141 s for the hundred-and-ten-token prompt,
# 1.81 s and 0.04 s of processor.
#
# src/shaders/rotate.comp joins the device and outside groups' sources.

# 2026-08-30, the fifth sitting: a layer in one submission -- and the
# sitting before it published the sitting before that, which is corrected
# here.
#
# The bookkeeping first, because it is the more important of the two. The
# script that rewrites the published figures raised on the first anchor it
# could not find and wrote nothing; a second, smaller script then landed and
# updated the prose around the tables. So the rotation commit carried new
# fingerprints, this file's account of a fresh sitting, prose from that
# sitting, and tables from the one before it. `### Against llama.cpp` already
# records one commit that went wrong this way and it has now gone wrong
# twice, so the fix is not care: the script applies what matches, reports
# what does not, and always writes.
#
# The figures below are this sitting's, measured after the change, and the
# rotation sitting's are gone -- they were correct when taken and there is
# nowhere to put them that would not be a third set of numbers in a file
# whose whole point is that there is one.
#
# The machine was slower than the sitting before: llama.cpp reads 355.6
# tokens a second on the processor at a hundred and ten against 388.6, and
# 273.9 at 1419 against 279.7. The device's own prompt went the other way,
# 1030.5 against 986.8, and its gap there is 1.70 -- which is the change,
# and the alternated pair says three and a half per cent of it.
#
# The device table in full, at a load under 1.15: 0.414 s and 0.298 s for
# the twelve-token run, 0.060 s and 0.038 s evaluating, 0.353 s and 0.259 s
# generating, 0.446 s and 0.134 s for the hundred-and-ten-token prompt,
# 1.83 s and 0.03 s of processor.
#
# src/shaders/place.comp joins the device and outside groups' sources.

# 2026-08-30, the sixth sitting: the angles kept the way the activation is
# kept, and the whole layer paying for a generated token.
#
# Alternated against the commit before it: three and a half per cent of
# sixty-four generated tokens, three rounds each way, and level on both
# prompts. The published generating row moved 44.4 to 46.2 tokens a second,
# which is four -- close to the alternated figure for once, and llama.cpp's
# own generating rows repeat the sitting before to a tenth (39.7 against
# 39.6 on the processor, 56.4 against 56.1 on the device).
#
# The processor rows drifted the other way: 254.0 tokens a second at a
# hundred and ten against 258.2, and llama.cpp's 356.6 against 355.6, which
# is the same machine reading the same thing. The device's long prompt is
# 1059.7 against 1030.5 and its gap there 1.71 -- level, and this change was
# not expected to move it.
#
# The device table in full, at a load under 1.15: 0.426 s and 0.283 s for
# the twelve-token run, 0.062 s and 0.034 s evaluating, 0.351 s and 0.249 s
# generating, 0.400 s and 0.134 s for the hundred-and-ten-token prompt,
# 1.91 s and 0.03 s of processor.

# 2026-08-30, the width of an attending workgroup, and three attempts at
# the matrix product that were measured and not kept.
#
#   attention.comp is compiled a second time with -DWIDE beside -DSUBGROUPS,
#   which runs two hundred and fifty-six invocations to a workgroup rather
#   than sixty-four. The third compilation, the tiled one, is not widened
#   and neither is the first.
#
#   The compiler and flags, all twelve as 'tests shader' takes them:
#     glslang 15.1.0 (Ubuntu glslang-tools 15.1.0-2)
#     attention.comp      -V
#     attention.comp      -V --target-env vulkan1.1 -DSUBGROUPS -DWIDE
#     attention.comp      -V --target-env vulkan1.1 -DSUBGROUPS -DQUERY_TILE
#     combine.comp        -V
#     row_product.comp    -V
#     row_product.comp    -V -DSINGLE
#     half_batch.comp     -V
#     matrix_product.comp --target-env vulkan1.3
#     matrix_product.comp --target-env vulkan1.3 -DMORE_FORMATS
#     norm.comp           -V
#     rotate.comp         -V
#     place.comp          -V
#
#   What it is worth, alternated, three rounds of each, TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0
#   on the device, medians of two:
#
#     64 generated after a 1419-token prompt
#       wide      1.595, 1.634, 1.603 s
#       narrow    1.717, 1.715, 1.692 s
#
#     the 1419-token prompt itself, both compilations widened
#       wide      1.712, 1.750, 1.740 s
#       narrow    1.631, 1.641, 1.635 s
#
#     64 generated after a six-token prompt, medians of three
#       wide      1.323, 1.349, 1.340 s
#       narrow    1.340, 1.352, 1.348 s
#
#   So it is worth six and a half per cent where the cache is long, level
#   where it is short, and costs six per cent on a prompt -- which is why
#   only the compilation a generated token enters is widened.
#
#   Digests unchanged: 1a26d24d33b8957b for the long prompt, 7ec6b755e53e16b4
#   for the same with sixty-four generated, 448c2ed68ec342ee for sixty-four
#   after a six-token prompt, cbf29ce484222325 for the 110-token prompt. The
#   sweep reads 28344 sequences, none outside tolerance, though the value a
#   lane carries is now four partial sums added at the end rather than one
#   running sum.
#
#   The figures re-measured for this, one sitting, each waiting for the
#   machine to fall below 1.20:
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.434 s wall, 0.067 and 0.368,
#                                      1.94 s processor
#                                      device 0.274 s wall, 0.035 and 0.240,
#                                      0.09 s processor
#     110-token prompt, none generated cpu 0.428 s, 2.62 s processor
#                                      device 0.138 s, 0.03 s processor
#     1419-token prompt                cpu 6.285 s, device 1.284 s
#     64 generated, six-token prompt   cpu 1.905 s, device 1.333 s
#     llama-bench 95b8e33e1, -r 3      pp110 351.74 +/- 22.52 --device none,
#                                      1499.15 +/- 142.67 -ngl 99
#                                      pp1419 271.14 +/- 0.95 --device none,
#                                      1802.17 +/- 9.87 -ngl 99
#                                      tg64 39.24 and 55.90
#                                      pp110 -ngl 0 with a device present
#                                      707.47, which is why the command
#                                      names --device none
#
#   And three changes to the matrix product, each measured and none kept.
#   They are written down because each of them was argued for from
#   arithmetic that turned out to be wrong, and the arithmetic is the part
#   worth keeping.
#
#   First, where a device prompt goes, by removing one piece at a time.
#   1419 tokens, median of three, baseline 1.282 s:
#
#     weight staging replaced by a constant       1.082 s
#     that and the activation loads as well       0.983 s
#     thirty-one of every thirty-two coopMatMulAdd 0.916 s
#
#   So the matrix instruction is 0.366 s of 1.282, twenty-nine per cent. A
#   kernel whose multiplies were free would read 0.92 s where llama.cpp
#   reads 0.74.
#
#   The packed half-precision dot product, v_dot2_f32_f16, reached through
#   a SPIR-V intrinsic exactly as llama.cpp reaches it, in place of the
#   cooperative matrix: 1.806, 1.773, 2.066 s against 1.280, 1.295, 1.328.
#   Thirty-nine per cent slower. The disassembly holds 2048
#   v_dot2acc_f32_f16 in one run with no other arithmetic between them, 144
#   VGPRs against 256 and six subgroups a SIMD against four -- better on
#   every static measure. And llama.cpp does not owe its speed to it either:
#   GGML_VK_DISABLE_COOPMAT=1 reads 1418 t/s against 1408 with it, so both
#   its paths land in the same place.
#
#   A taller tile, 128 rows over four subgroups with the activations staged
#   in shared memory: 2.665 s. The argument was that a 32-row workgroup
#   re-reads its half-megabyte activation tile 64 times for a 2048-column
#   matrix -- 33 MB against 4.5 MB of weights -- and it was wrong because
#   those re-reads are L2 hits, not memory.
#
#   A wider tile, 512 vectors over four subgroups sharing one decoded copy
#   of the weights, the step deepened to 128 columns so every invocation
#   decodes: 1.624 s, at the same 256 VGPRs and the same four subgroups a
#   SIMD. 512 vectors is a two-megabyte activation tile, which is the whole
#   L2. Batch width alone is neutral: 1.319, 1.261, 1.336 s at 128, 512 and
#   256, because a workgroup re-reads the weights whatever the batch is.
#
#   32 rows by 128 vectors is where this part's cache hierarchy puts the
#   optimum, and the matrix product is not where the remaining gap is.
#
# 2026-08-31, a prompt attends through the matrix instruction, on a cache
# kept in half precision.
#
#   src/shaders/attention_matrix.comp, a fourth attending kernel, compiled
#   with -V --target-env vulkan1.3 as the matrix product is. The twelve
#   compilations 'tests shader' takes, in order:
#     glslang 15.1.0 (Ubuntu glslang-tools 15.1.0-2)
#     attention.comp        -V
#     attention.comp        -V --target-env vulkan1.1 -DSUBGROUPS -DWIDE
#     attention.comp        -V --target-env vulkan1.1 -DSUBGROUPS
#                           -DQUERY_TILE
#     combine.comp          -V
#     row_product.comp      -V
#     row_product.comp      -V -DSINGLE
#     half_batch.comp       -V
#     matrix_product.comp   --target-env vulkan1.3
#     matrix_product.comp   --target-env vulkan1.3 -DMORE_FORMATS
#     attention_matrix.comp -V --target-env vulkan1.3
#     norm.comp             -V
#     rotate.comp           -V
#     place.comp            -V
#
#   Alternated, three rounds each, medians of three, 1419-token prompt on
#   the device:
#     matrix attention   1.222, 1.236, 1.254 s
#     scalar attention   1.333, 1.319, 1.323 s
#   Every matrix run faster than every scalar one.
#
#   What it took to get there, all measured, the first two not kept:
#     staged from the binary32 cache, one pass          1.535 s
#     staged, two passes so nothing is ever rescaled    1.785 s
#     half-precision cache, read where it lies          1.236 s
#   The two staged forms differ by one more staging of the keys and one
#   more score product, a quarter of a second, which is what said the
#   staging was the cost rather than the arithmetic.
#
#   And two shapes inside the kept kernel:
#     row reductions clustered over half a subgroup     1.236 s
#     one invocation to a row, walking its columns      1.606 s
#
#   The cache is reserved at six bytes an element rather than four -- the
#   binary32 cache and a half-precision copy after it -- and is zeroed when
#   it is taken, because the kernel reads a whole tile of positions whether
#   or not every one has been written and a weight of zero against a
#   not-a-number is a not-a-number.
#
#   A push constant was not added for where the copy begins. Widening the
#   attention block from sixty-four bytes to sixty-eight, or to seventy-two,
#   changed what the device computed -- 1a26d24d33b8957b became
#   c5d5de3282b2f709 with the new kernel not even bound -- on a part whose
#   maxPushConstantsSize is 256. That is not understood and is written down
#   rather than explained. What is pushed instead is the key and value bases
#   already pointing into the copy, decided at the call site by which kernel
#   is about to be bound, which needs no new field.
#
#   Correctness: the sweep reads 28344 sequences, none outside tolerance,
#   with half-precision keys and values. Digests held: 1a26d24d33b8957b for
#   the long prompt, cbf29ce484222325 for the 110-token one,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee for sixty-four generated, 5abff916f9d83ca6 for twelve,
#   33f48397f89839f6 and 3248ac1bb7011de0 on the processor.
#
#   Why attention and not the matrix product, from llama.cpp's own profiler
#   (GGML_VK_PERF_LOGGER=1, build 95b8e33e1), microseconds per position per
#   layer, theirs at a 512-position ubatch and ours at 128:
#     attention              ours 6.12   theirs 2.85
#     five matrix products   ours 22.5   theirs 21.6
#     everything else        ours  3.3   theirs  3.1
#   Their 2048x512x2048 product runs at 4154 gigaflops and ours at 4050, so
#   the matrix product was already level and attention was fifty-two per
#   cent of the remainder. GGML_VK_DISABLE_COOPMAT=1 takes their attention
#   from 2945 gigaflops to 1610 and pp1419 from 1744 t/s to 1240 -- which
#   also corrects an earlier reading of that flag taken at pp512, where it
#   showed 1408 against 1418 and was taken to mean the instruction was not
#   what made them fast.
#
#   The figures re-measured for this, one sitting, each waiting for the
#   machine to fall below 1.15:
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.405 s wall, 0.061 and 0.343,
#                                      1.83 s processor
#                                      device 0.267 s wall, 0.035 and 0.231,
#                                      0.08 s processor
#     110-token prompt, none generated cpu 0.441 s, 2.71 s processor
#                                      device 0.131 s, 0.02 s processor
#     1419-token prompt                cpu 5.957 s, device 1.225 s
#     64 generated, six-token prompt   cpu 1.859 s, device 1.353 s
#     llama-bench 95b8e33e1, -r 3      pp110 352.40 +/- 32.21 --device none,
#                                      1584.49 +/- 16.77 -ngl 99
#                                      pp1419 271.29 +/- 0.38 --device none,
#                                      1759.14 +/- 2.81 -ngl 99
#                                      tg64 39.38 and 55.79
#                                      pp110 -ngl 0 with a device present
#                                      734.18
#
# 2026-08-31, sixteen query rows to an attending workgroup, and two shapes
# measured against it and not kept.
#
#   attention_matrix.comp's BR and the engine's Matrix_Queries go from
#   thirty-two to sixteen together. Alternated, three rounds, medians of
#   three, 1419-token prompt on the device:
#     sixteen rows   1.212, 1.207, 1.221 s
#     thirty-two     1.236, 1.261, 1.237 s
#   Sixteen every round. The kernel goes from 256 VGPRs and four subgroups
#   a SIMD to 96 and ten, and its shared memory from 14336 bytes to 7168.
#   Sixteen is the floor: it is the instruction's own tile.
#
#   Not kept, both aimed at the workgroup count of the matrix product,
#   which follows the rows -- 176 workgroups for a feed-forward arm, 64 for
#   a projection, 8 for the keys and values, against twenty-four SIMDs.
#   Their rates, from the per-step timestamps: 4390 and 4570, 4050 and
#   3814, and 1040 gigaflops.
#
#     the narrow products sent to the row product instead, which
#     dispatches thirty-two workgroups for two hundred and fifty-six rows:
#       row product   1.246, 1.246, 1.253 s
#       matrix        1.234, 1.213, 1.223 s
#
#     a workgroup's rows split between two subgroups, sixteen each, with
#     the step deepened to sixty-four columns so that all hundred and
#     twenty-eight invocations decode: 1.360 s. Twice the waves for the
#     same workgroups and half the accumulators each, and slower. That is
#     the third workgroup of more than one subgroup to lose in this kernel,
#     after the taller tile and the wider one; its barriers are free at one
#     subgroup and are not at two.
#
#   What is left untried is a split of the columns across workgroups, each
#   accumulating a partial answer, added up in a pass of their own -- the
#   only way at the workgroup count that does not put two subgroups behind
#   one barrier.
#
#   The figures re-measured, one sitting, each waiting for the machine to
#   fall below 1.15 (the two device runs re-taken after the processor runs
#   drove the load up):
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.425 s wall, 0.067 and 0.356,
#                                      1.91 s processor
#                                      device 0.274 s wall, 0.033 and 0.242,
#                                      0.09 s processor
#     110-token prompt, none generated cpu 0.443 s, 2.68 s processor
#                                      device 0.129 s, 0.03 s processor

# 2026-08-31, the split, measured in the cheap form first and not built in
# the expensive one.
#
#   The workgroup count of the matrix product follows the rows, and the
#   narrow products make too few: eight for a two-hundred-and-fifty-six-row
#   projection over a batch of a hundred and twenty-eight, on a part with
#   twenty-four SIMDs, running at 1040 gigaflops where a feed-forward arm's
#   hundred and seventy-six run at 4390. The fix proposed was to split the
#   columns across workgroups and add the partial answers up in a pass of
#   their own.
#
#   Before building that, the same occupancy was bought a cheaper way: a
#   third compilation of matrix_product.comp with NARROW, taking
#   thirty-two vectors to a workgroup instead of a hundred and
#   twenty-eight. That is four times the workgroups for the same rows --
#   eight becomes thirty-two -- at the price of decoding the weights four
#   times over, which for a half-megabyte matrix is nothing.
#
#   Alternated, three rounds, medians of three, 1419-token prompt:
#     narrow tile for the keys and values   1.192, 1.191, 1.206 s
#     wide tile for everything              1.193, 1.193, 1.215 s
#   Level. Quadrupling the workgroups moved nothing measurable.
#
#   The pipeline was live: bound for every product instead of only the
#   narrow ones, the same prompt reads 1.709 s, which is the four-fold
#   decode on the wide matrices and proof the selection works.
#
#   So the workgroup count is not what binds those products, and the
#   column split is not worth building. What it would add over the tile
#   measured here is the decode it does not repeat -- three extra passes
#   over half a megabyte a layer, 360 MB across the prompt, about six
#   thousandths of a second of eleven hundred and ninety. Against that: a
#   scratch region of rows by vectors by splits, a combining pass, and the
#   engine plumbing for both. Not built, and this is why.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit a25f993 left it.
#
# 2026-08-31, the prompt's time accounted against llama.cpp's own
# accounting, and their narrow-shape configuration matched.
#
#   Per-step GPU timestamps again, a top-of-pipe at the command buffer's
#   start and a bottom-of-pipe after each of the seventeen steps and at the
#   end, read every eighth submission so that the read does not stall each
#   one. Thirty-three batched samples spread over the prompt.
#
#   Per layer-batch of a hundred and twenty-eight positions, milliseconds:
#     down projection      0.785      gate                 0.695
#     up                   0.665      attention            0.505
#     query projection     0.271      output projection    0.266
#     key                  0.132      value                0.131
#     gate combine         0.133      the two norms        0.126
#     the other two joins  0.117      rotate               0.042
#     the two cache writes 0.014
#     total                3.882
#
#   Which is 30.3 microseconds a position a layer, against llama.cpp's
#   25.55 from their own logger. Attention is 3.95 now where it was 6.12
#   before the half-precision cache and the sixteen-row tile.
#
#   And the accounting, which is the point of taking these:
#     span inside Submit_And_Wait     93.7 per cent of the wall there
#     the seventeen steps summed      1025 ms across the prompt
#     the prompt itself               1190 ms
#   So eighty-six per cent of a device prompt is inside a dispatch.
#   llama.cpp's logger reads 784 ms of GPU against an 813 ms prompt --
#   ninety-six. About a hundred and sixty-five milliseconds of ours, a
#   seventh of the prompt, is somewhere no kernel change can reach, where
#   theirs has thirty.
#
#   Their configuration for the narrow shape, read out of
#   ggml_vk_guess_matmul_pipeline and ggml_vk_guess_split_k rather than
#   guessed: the tile is chosen by shape -- m or n at most 32 takes a
#   32x32 tile, at most 64 a 64x64, otherwise 128x128 with a hundred and
#   twenty-eight invocations -- and split_k is taken only where k is at
#   least 2048 and the tiles are at most two thirds of the core count. Run
#   over this model's shapes that gives split_k of one everywhere except
#   the keys and values, where it gives three.
#
#   Matched here as far as the shape allows -- a compilation with two
#   subgroups to a workgroup, sixteen rows each, the step deepened to
#   sixty-four columns so that all hundred and twenty-eight invocations
#   decode, bound only where the rows are under five hundred and twelve:
#     paired for the keys and values   1.195, 1.181, 1.203 s
#     one wave everywhere              1.199, 1.191, 1.232 s
#   Faster in each of three rounds and by three parts in a thousand. Not
#   kept: a third pipeline and a fourth compilation for that is not a
#   trade, and it settles that the narrow products are not where the
#   remaining gap is. The earlier reading of the same idea applied to every
#   product measured 1.360 s, and a narrower vector tile measured level;
#   this is the third and last of that family.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit 2811c4d left it.

# 2026-08-31, the rotation table tabulated once a batch, and the sitting
# that five groups needed for it.
#
#   Rotary_Table is called once a position a layer and works out a cosine
#   and a sine a pair in binary64. It does not depend on the layer:
#   everything it reads is fixed for one call except the rotation base, and
#   Turn_Base states one base unless the architecture states a local one
#   for windowed layers. Cached on the base; a reallocation clears it.
#
#   Alternated, three rounds, medians of three, 1419-token prompt:
#     tabulated once a batch    1.123, 1.139, 1.131 s   device
#     once a layer              1.209, 1.184, 1.210 s
#     tabulated once a batch    6.234 s                 processor
#     once a layer              6.331 s
#   The slowest cached device run is faster than the fastest uncached one.
#
#   How it was found, which is the part worth keeping. Per-step GPU
#   timestamps summed to 1025 ms of a 1190 ms prompt -- eighty-six per cent
#   of a device prompt inside a dispatch, where llama.cpp's own logger
#   accounts for ninety-six, 784 ms of an 813 ms prompt. Timing Run from
#   the caller's side split the remainder, with the one-time weight upload
#   netted out:
#     waiting on the device                1093 ms
#     inside Run and not waiting              40 ms
#       (recording 10, activation over 9, answers back 21)
#     outside Run altogether                 190 ms
#   Three quarters of a millisecond a layer of host work, and most of it was
#   the table. What is left outside a dispatch is a little over a hundred
#   milliseconds: the keys and values copied into the host cache, the
#   sequence built a layer at a time, the residual bookkeeping. None of
#   those is measured apart yet.

#     the prompt                             1.500 s

#                                           batch, which is the table's
#                                           allocation and its first touch)
#
#   The sequence building was the thing worth suspecting and it is seven
#   milliseconds. There is no lever left here: the largest piece is the
#   work between batches, and the next is the megabyte a layer that comes
#   back because a layer's answer is handed to the host before the next
#   layer sends it again. Removing that one is the activation kept on the
#   device across layers, which was costed earlier in this file at about
#   five per cent and needs the submissions chained behind a semaphore.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit d9a3085 left it.
#
# 2026-08-31, the activation kept on the device between layers.
#
#   A layer's answer is the next layer's activation. It went to the host and
#   came back: 21 ms a prompt out of the mapped result buffer and 9 over
#   again, which the accounting of the sitting before priced at two to three
#   per cent.
#
#   Alternated, three rounds, medians of three, 1419-token device prompt:
#     carried        1.097, 1.105, 1.108 s
#     host-borne     1.134, 1.125, 1.137 s
#   Two and six tenths per cent, and the slowest carried run is faster than
#   the fastest uncarried one.
#
#   Written first with a copy -- a transfer command from the last step's
#   room to the front of the result buffer, with a barrier on each side --
#   it measured 1.116 against 1.130, one and two tenths per cent. Half the
#   gain went on two pipeline drains a layer and a megabyte the device had
#   no reason to move. Placing the last step at the front instead, so the
#   device writes the answer where the next layer reads it, is what the
#   figures above are. Safe because the only step that reads that room is
#   the first residual join, which a barrier publishes long before the last
#   step runs.
#
#   A layer carries out only where the next layer will be taken whole as
#   well: one that falls back reads the host's copy of the activation, and
#   the host's copy is what carrying does not write. The condition that
#   decides whether the device takes a layer is a function now and is asked
#   of the next layer first.
#
#   Five groups list the three files that changed. One sitting, each run
#   waiting for the machine to fall below 1.10:
#
#     twelve tokens, six-token prompt  cpu 0.421 s wall, 0.063 and 0.359,
#                                      1.91 s processor
#                                      device 0.277 s wall, 0.032 and 0.245,

#                                      1581.44 +/- 61.45 -ngl 99

#                                      1761.38 +/- 2.64 -ngl 99
#                                      tg64 39.42 and 56.17
#                                      pp110 -ngl 0 with a device present
#                                      746.70
#
#   The worker-count and share-count comparisons were not re-taken, for the
#   reason given in the entry above: they measure the worker pool and a
#   --threads pair, and neither reaches this.
#
#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b, 5f85f06494f88de4,
#   7fbdc947563a2fde. The sweep reads 28344 sequences, none outside
#   tolerance.
#
#   What is left is the fence a layer still waits on. The host wants nothing
#   from a carried layer but its keys and values, which it copies into its
#   own mirror of the cache; if that mirror went the submissions could chain
#   and the 68 ms a prompt spends between submit and wake would go with it.
#   That is a change to what a session guarantees.
#
# 2026-08-31, the host's copy of the cache filled once a batch instead of
# once a layer -- built, correct, and level.
#
#   The keys and the values a layer writes came back through the result
#   buffer, step by step, because the host keeps its own copy of the cache
#   for a session that later runs on the processor. They are already in the
#   cache the device holds, so Get_Cache was added beside Put_Cache, the two
#   steps stopped being kept, and the copy was made once at the end of a
#   batch out of the device's own cache instead.
#
#   Alternated, three rounds, medians of three, 1419-token device prompt:
#     filled once a batch   1.110, 1.103, 1.105 s
#     filled once a layer   1.104, 1.106, 1.107 s
#   Level. Digest 1a26d24d33b8957b throughout.
#
#   It was never going to pay on its own: it moves a copy rather than
#   removing one, and the copy was not what a layer waited for. What it was
#   for is the wait -- with the answer carried on the device and the keys
#   and values read out afterwards, a layer needs nothing from the device
#   at all, and the sixty-eight milliseconds a prompt spends between submit
#   and wake could go.
#
#   That needs the engine doubled, and this is where it stops. Submissions
#   on one queue can be made to execute in order, so the result buffer is
#   safe; what is not safe is that the host writes descriptor sets and
#   records a command buffer between layers, and neither may be touched
#   while a submission using them is still executing. So chaining needs two
#   command buffers, two fences and two sets of descriptor sets -- the
#   engine has one of each. Reverted rather than kept: it is complexity
#   with nothing measurable behind it until that is done, and it is written
#   here so that it is not re-derived.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit 3857d10 left it.
#
# 2026-08-31, two of everything a submission holds, so a layer does not
# wait for its own work.
#
#   Alternated, three rounds, medians of three, 1419-token device prompt:
#     chained    1.038, 1.041, 1.054 s
#     waiting    1.113, 1.117, 1.113 s
#   Six and a half per cent, and the slowest chained run is faster than the
#   fastest waiting one. It recovers what the accounting two sittings ago
#   priced: ninety-four per cent of a layer's wait was the device computing
#   and six was the gap around it, sixty-eight milliseconds a prompt.
#
#   Two command buffers, two fences, two arrays of descriptors, and one
#   semaphore. The rules each of those answers:
#
#     A command buffer may not be re-recorded while it executes and
#     descriptors may not be written while a submission reads them. Swapped
#     each sequence; the slot taken up is waited for first, which is the
#     sequence before the previous one.
#
#     Two submissions to one queue are not ordered against each other
#     unless made to be, and the second reads what the first left at the
#     front of the result buffer. One semaphore, waited on and signalled by
#     every submission: a binary semaphore may not be signalled while
#     already signalled, and a signal nothing waits for -- the last
#     sequence of a batch -- leaves it that way. Two semaphores deadlocked
#     seven device tests with BACKEND_DEVICE_STALLED before that was
#     understood, and the armed flag left set across a close deadlocked
#     them again: a new engine's semaphore has signalled nothing.
#
#     A matrix the sequence in flight is reading may not be given back, so
#     the eviction floor is one clock lower where there is one, and a
#     sequence that borrowed anything waits before giving it back.
#
#     Anything the host writes that a submission may read -- the activation
#     sent over, a buffer grown, an engine closing, the two other paths
#     that record into the same buffer -- settles everything in flight
#     first.
#
#   Run waits only where a step is kept, a matrix was borrowed, or the
#   answer is the last one. In a twenty-two layer batch that is the last
#   layer, which is why this needed the answer carried and the keys and
#   values read out of the device's own cache first: both are in place.
#
#   293 tests pass. The sweep reads 28344 sequences, none outside
#   tolerance, none unlearned. Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6,
#   5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325, 448c2ed68ec342ee,
#   3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b, 5f85f06494f88de4,
#   7fbdc947563a2fde.
#
#   Five groups moved. One sitting, each run waiting for the machine to
#   fall below 1.10:
#
#     twelve tokens, six-token prompt  cpu 0.417 s wall, 0.061 and 0.355,
#                                      1.91 s processor

#     the same at --arith f32          1.383 s, 10.15 s processor
