# 2026-09-01, both of attention's loops moved into the kernel that scores.
#
#   A profile of the long prompt on the processor puts head_scores at 6.7 per
#   cent of it. Forty-six per cent of that kernel's samples were not in the
#   run: ten arguments, six reach comparisons, an index check on each of
#   three arrays every time an address was taken, and a frame -- in front of
#   sixty-four multiply-adds, because the caller walks a block of eight keys
#   and one head at a time and that is one turn of the loop inside.
#
#   Head_Scores_Across takes the head range and the whole key run, proves the
#   reach once, and issues the run in place. The run is a constant both
#   procedures share, so it is the same instructions in the same order, and
#   the scores are bit for bit what they were. Head_Scores still does one
#   head and takes the tail and every host without the lanes.
#
#   Counted rather than timed, two alternated rounds:
#
#     a head at a time    1032457207147 and 1032422518763 instructions
#                          380124653266 and  378714944135 cycles
#     the range at once    991366405630 and  991291133539 instructions
#                          375974170333 and  370745329887 cycles
#
#   Four per cent of the instructions and 1.6 per cent of the cycles, both
#   reproducing to five figures. Nine wall-clock runs alternated over three
#   rounds read 6.057 s against 5.952 -- 1.7 per cent, and inside the spread
#   on its own. The counters are what settle this one.
#
#   Inside the kernel the multiply-adds went from 39 per cent of the samples
#   to 51 and the prologue from 46 to 30. The run is at both walls at once,
#   sixty-four loads and sixty-four multiply-adds to a block on a part that
#   does two of each a cycle, so there is nothing left to trade.
#
#   src/library/model_runner-kernels.ads was in no group's source list, and a
#   change to it would have passed the gate in silence. It is in the five
#   groups that name the body now, which is why their stamps move further
#   than the change alone would move them.

#   faster; only the settled sitting may be published as a figure.

#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b, 5f85f06494f88de4,
#   7fbdc947563a2fde, 4b6e8e99ae285b2a.

#   llama.cpp's 270.0, which is a gap of 1.04 -- the closest either side of
#   this comparison has been at either length.

#   model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, and Q4_K_M and Q2_K beside it
#
# 2026-09-01, four measurements of the generating row kernel, none kept, and
# a diagnosis corrected.
#
#   The list this came from said the generating kernel copies and biases
#   every weight before multiplying it. It does not. The staging copy is in
#   the four-row insertion, and a generated token does not go there: deleting
#   that loop outright moved the instruction count of a sixty-four token run
#   by four hundredths of a per cent -- 81216 against 81181 million -- which
#   is how a path is proved cold. What a token runs is Rows_Singly, and that
#   one already reads the weights where the file holds them, keeps the
#   accumulator in a register from a row's first block to its last, and
#   issues eleven instructions for every thirty-two multiply-adds.
#
#   What the counters do say is that the generating side is not waiting for
#   the bus. Sixty-four tokens: 81.2 billion instructions in 35.5 billion
#   cycles, which is 2.29 a cycle. Reading a gigabyte of weights a token at
#   about 31 GB/s on a part that does far more, at that rate of issue, is a
#   program limited by what it executes rather than by what it fetches. This
#   file has said the other thing for several sittings.
#
#   Four things were then measured. All four are refused.
#
#   The block scale, decoded by the hardware rather than by Ada. Scale_At
#   builds a sixteen-bit number out of two byte loads, a shift and an or
#   before vcvtph2ps can start -- six instructions in a function every block
#   of every row calls. vpinsrw takes the two bytes at the address directly,
#   which is three:
#
#     a head at a time     81220, 81274, 81204 million instructions
#     the pair loaded      74404, 74411, 74396
#
#   Eight and four tenths per cent of every instruction a generated token
#   executes, reproducing to five figures, and the tokens are identical
#   because it is the same convert of the same two bytes. It is not faster.
#   Q8_0 cycles read 35.50 against 34.77 in one sitting and 35.61 against
#   35.85 in the next, which is noise around zero, and Q4_K generation is
#   clearly worse: 9.86 billion cycles against 14.90, and 0.40 s generating
#   against 0.69. The two byte loads issue in parallel with everything
#   around them; vpinsrw is a load and an insert into one register, and the
#   convert waits behind both. Fewer instructions on a longer chain.
#
#   The four kilobytes of `rep stosq` at the top of that kernel. Row_Scale
#   has room for a thousand blocks and is zeroed on entry to set the
#   hundred and seventy-six a row will overwrite; the instruction was among
#   the ten hottest in a profile of a generated token. Alternated, three
#   rounds: 1.893 s generating against 1.870, and Q4_K level. One and two
#   tenths per cent, under the floor.
#
#   The bias correction as an integer rather than a sum. The insertion's
#   unsigned operand is the weight byte with its sign bit flipped, and what
#   that adds comes back out as a running binary64 sum over every block of
#   every row -- seven instructions a block, and removing it outright takes
#   twelve per cent off the whole run. It need not be a sum at all: the
#   correction is an integer smaller than a million, it depends on the
#   activation block and not on the row, and the lane the multiply-add
#   accumulates into can start at it instead of at zero, which is a load
#   where the insertion had a xor.
#
#     base                 81220, 81274, 81204 million instructions
#                          35499, 36155, 35264 million cycles
#     the pair loaded       and the correction folded in
#                          66191, 66226, 66165 million instructions
#                          35432, 35420, 34861 million cycles
#
#   Eighteen and a half per cent of the instructions and nothing at all on
#   the clock -- the cycles read one and nine tenths per cent worse than the
#   pair-loaded arm alone. The xor it replaced is a zeroing idiom, which
#   costs no execution at all, and the load it becomes goes into the chain
#   the multiply-add is already waiting on. It moves the answers as well
#   (3740ed87be385f2d against 3248ac1bb7011de0, and the suite passes at 294
#   of 294): the correction becomes exact where it was a rounded sum. Being
#   more nearly right for nothing is not a reason to change published
#   digests.
#
#   Nothing is kept and no figure moves. What is worth keeping is the shape
#   of the answer: three of the four cut instructions by four to eighteen
#   per cent and none of them cut cycles, on a kernel issuing 2.29
#   instructions a cycle. There is no front-end slack left in it. The next
#   thing to try on this side has to shorten a dependency chain or move
#   fewer bytes, not execute fewer instructions -- and the entry two above
#   this one, which cut four per cent of the instructions and 1.6 per cent
#   of the cycles, is the same lesson read from the other end.
#
#   load:  under 1.05 before every reading
#   model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 and Q4_K_M
#
kernels D86518E765C42652 src/library/model_runner-quantization.adb src/library/model_runner-numerics.adb src/library/model_runner-tensors.adb tests/src/benchmarks.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-decoders.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-wide.ads model_runner.gpr src/library/model_runner-byte_sources-files.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-kernels.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-kernels.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-plain.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-wide.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-deep.ads src/library/model_runner-platform-instructions.ads src/platform/linux/model_runner-platform-instructions.adb

# 2026-08-24, four rows against one reading of the activation. The quantized
# product multiplied one row at a time and read the activation block once for
# every row of the matrix; it multiplies four rows against one reading of it
# now, which changes no value -- the generated text's digest is unchanged --
# and moves the prompt alone, because only a prompt has rows enough at once
# for the reuse to be worth anything.
#
# The tile is four because four measured fastest: 2.120 s for the 110-token
# prompt at two rows, 1.948 s at four, 2.122 s at eight. Eight spills the
# accumulators it was meant to keep.
#
# Everything below was taken again for it in one sitting at loads of 0.87 to
# 2.6:
#
#   twelve tokens              0.600 s wall, 0.107 evaluating and 0.502
#                              generating, 3.48 s processor
#   the same at --arith f32    1.317 s wall, 8.60 s processor, back to back
#   fourteen threads           0.535 s wall, 6.12 s processor
#   110-token prompt           2.056 s wall, 9.84 s processor
#   64 tokens generated        2.928 s wall, 17.08 s processor
#   110-token prompt, device   1.937 s wall, 1.07 s processor
#   64 generated, device       4.554 s wall, 1.14 s processor
#   reference, four tokens     cpu 0.101 s and 0.154 s against 5.704 s and
#                              3.826 s
#   drafting, the three runs   0.608 s, 1.578 s and 3.834 s, 24 proposed and
#                              7 accepted
#   batch sizes 1 to 128       4.807, 3.239, 2.459, 2.255, 2.313, 1.899,

# cpu 0.609 s against device 0.992 s; evaluating the prompt 0.119 s against

# One reading here is a finding rather than a number. Fourteen threads beat
# seven for the first time in a year -- 0.535 s against 0.600 s for seventy-
# six per cent more processor time -- and the reason is the arithmetic: a
# product that multiplies bytes waits on memory less and on execution units
# more, so a second thread on a core has something to do again. The bargain
# has now been eighteen per cent better, eleven worse, seven worse, nothing,
# and eleven better, on one machine, and every one of those readings was
# right when it was taken.
#
# The row-product and share-scaling tables come from a pinned `tests
# benchmark` run of its own, started at a load of 0.49. Both measure the
# floating-point product -- the tool never asks for the other arithmetic --
# so what they say about this change is that it did not touch what they
# measure, which is the useful thing for them to say. The row product reads
# f32 0.25, q4_0 0.31, bf16 0.32, q8_0 0.38, q4_k 0.39, q6_k 0.40, q5_k 0.41,
# q4_1 0.49, q3_k 0.49, iq4xs 0.50, q5_0 0.54, f16 0.56, q5_1 0.58, iq4nl
# 0.63 and q2_k 0.72 nanoseconds an element. Pinned, q8_0 with one vector a
# pass reads 2501 Me/s serially and 14233 at eight shares against 12773 at
# seven; batched thirty-two a pass, 4690 serially and 26088 at eight against
# 24462 at seven. Four bits against eight: 13668 against 14233 at eight
# shares with one vector, 25911 against 26088 with thirty-two, 2544 against
# 2501 serially.
#
# The pinned run taken inside the sitting above started at a load of 2.98 --
# twenty measurements had just raised it -- and was discarded rather than
# published, which is what that bound is for.

# 2026-08-24, the batch a prompt is read in, and what a device call costs.
#
# B1 ruled out the shader's memory pattern and its byte-at-a-time decode as
# explanations for the device being thirty times behind. What was left was
# how much it costs to tell the device to do something, and the cheapest way
# to weigh that is a control this program already has: --batch-size changes
# how many calls a prompt makes while leaving the number of passes over the
# weights alone. A 110-token prompt on the device:
#
#   batch   8   2.767 s        batch  32   1.973 s        batch 128   1.054 s
#
# Sixteen passes over the model either way, and a factor of 2.6 between them.
# That is 0.6 to 2.3 milliseconds a call, and a prompt at a batch of
# thirty-two makes about five hundred of them.
#
# So the default batch is the engine's cap now rather than thirty-two. What
# it costs is how often a run can be cancelled or report progress while
# reading a prompt, which is written down where the default is.
#
# Everything was taken again for it in one sitting at loads of 0.82 to 2.4:
#
#   twelve tokens              0.589 s wall, 0.095 evaluating and 0.494
#                              generating, 3.47 s processor
#   the same at --arith f32    1.280 s wall, 8.63 s processor, back to back
#   fourteen threads           0.534 s wall, 6.13 s processor
#   110-token prompt           1.907 s wall, 9.09 s processor
#   64 tokens generated        2.872 s wall, 16.75 s processor
#   110-token prompt, device   1.054 s wall, 0.12 s processor
#   64 generated, device       4.541 s wall, 1.14 s processor
#   reference, four tokens     cpu 0.098 s and 0.158 s against 5.739 s and
#                              3.835 s
#   drafting, the three runs   0.608 s, 1.673 s and 3.584 s, 24 proposed and
#                              7 accepted
#   batch sizes 1 to 128       5.315, 3.248, 2.556, 2.138, 2.022, 2.125,
#                              1.855 and 1.738 s on the processor
#   llama.cpp on the same file 351.86 and 39.75 tokens a second on the
#                              processor, 1642.15 and 56.58 on the device,
#                              766.45 and 39.96 with -ngl 0
#
# The device table, cell for cell: six-token prompt with twelve generated,
# cpu 0.565 s against device 0.987 s; evaluating the prompt 0.109 s against
# 0.177 s; generating 0.476 s against 0.809 s; processor time 3.43 s against
# 0.22 s; 110-token prompt cpu 1.713 s against device 1.054 s, processor time
# 8.79 s against 0.12 s.
#
# Five groups gained two sources with this entry -- model_runner-generation.ads
# and model_runner-cli-options.ads -- and the reason is a hole this change
# walked through. The default batch decides every prompt figure published
# here, and it lives in a file no group named, so raising it moved six
# published numbers with no fingerprint firing and nothing asking whether
# they were still true. A figure covered by a group that does not name what
# decides it is a figure covered by nothing, which this file has said once
# before about the drafting group and its own loop.
#
# What was tried and abandoned before the batch was measured: keeping the two
# buffers a product uses mapped from one call to the next instead of mapping
# and unmapping them per call. It is the right change and the file's own
# cache comment argues for it, but this attempt got it wrong twice -- a
# mapping sized by the call rather than by the allocation, and a Write_Into
# that unmaps a buffer another pointer is still held into -- and the device
# tests caught both. It is not in the tree. What is in the tree is the
# measurement that says how much such a change could be worth: at most the
# 0.6 to 2.3 ms a call above, of which the batch has already taken three
# quarters by making four times fewer calls.

# 2026-08-24, the results a product reads back are no longer mapped and
# unmapped once a call.
#
# What B1 to B3 ruled out left one candidate: the cost of a device call
# itself, which the batch measurement put at 0.6 to 2.3 ms. A Vulkan submit
# and fence should be a fraction of that, and the difference was this
# program's own doing -- every product mapped the result memory, copied out
# of it and unmapped it again, and the cache beside it had already written
# down that a map and an unmap pair costs about a millisecond and a half.
#
# The mapping stands now. The 110-token prompt on the device goes from
# 1.054 s to 0.660 s and its processor time from 0.12 s to 0.14 s; generating
# sixty-four tokens goes from 4.541 s to 4.292 s, which is inside that row's
# own spread and is not claimed as anything.
#
# Everything was taken again in one sitting at loads of 0.56 to 2.4:
#
#   twelve tokens              0.536 s wall, 0.092 evaluating and 0.447
#                              generating, 3.36 s processor
#   the same at --arith f32    1.265 s wall, 8.45 s processor, back to back
#   fourteen threads           0.524 s wall, 6.09 s processor
#   110-token prompt           1.687 s wall, 8.68 s processor
#   64 tokens generated        2.522 s wall, 15.57 s processor
#   110-token prompt, device   0.660 s wall, 0.14 s processor
#   64 generated, device       4.292 s wall, 1.11 s processor
#   reference, four tokens     cpu 0.090 s and 0.162 s against 5.576 s and
#                              3.710 s
#   drafting, the three runs   0.537 s, 1.478 s and 3.539 s, 24 proposed and
#                              7 accepted
#   batch sizes 1 to 128       4.889, 3.367, 2.512, 2.098, 2.166, 2.064,
#                              1.944 and 1.729 s on the processor
#   llama.cpp on the same file 376.56 and 40.04 tokens a second on the
#                              processor, 1679.39 and 56.32 on the device,
#                              775.45 and 40.00 with -ngl 0
#
# The device table, cell for cell: six-token prompt with twelve generated,
# cpu 0.534 s against device 1.044 s; evaluating the prompt 0.087 s against
# 0.192 s; generating 0.449 s against 0.851 s; processor time 3.36 s against
# 0.26 s; 110-token prompt cpu 1.615 s against device 0.660 s, processor time
# 8.55 s against 0.14 s.
#
# The processor rows moved too -- 1.907 s to 1.687 on the prompt and 2.872 to
# 2.522 generating -- and nothing in this change is on the processor path.
# That sitting was the quietest of the run, and the figures are published
# from it because a table has to come from one sitting; the honest reading of
# those two is the machine and not the program, and the README says so.
#
# What was tried and is not here: the same treatment for the activation a
# product writes in. It is the other pair of map and unmap per call and worth
# roughly as much again, and it produced wrong answers -- the drafted device
# test, which runs a batched evaluator and a single-token one over the same
# weights, reported them disagreeing. Three explanations were tried and
# measured out: a mapping sized by the call rather than by the allocation,
# another path unmapping the same memory underneath it, and a megabyte of
# batch copied through a constant on the stack. None of them was it. It is a
# separate question with the same shape as the one above and a different
# answer, and the comment where the write is says so.

# 2026-08-24, the activation a product writes is out of a standing mapping
# too, and why it took three attempts.
#
# The entry above kept the read-back's mapping and left this one, saying the
# cause of its wrong answers had not been found. It has. The two products
# that write an activation are textually different -- one names Columns and
# the other Steps.Items (1).Columns -- so a search and replace over the
# obvious form converted one of them and left the other calling a writer
# that unmaps when it is done. One writer left on a buffer pulls the mapping
# out from under every other writer of it. Three explanations had been tried
# and measured out before that one was looked for, and none of them was
# wrong about anything except being the cause.
#
# The lesson is the one this file already wrote after the same afternoon:
# the unit that can be converted safely is every user of one buffer, not one
# call site. It is now written where the code is as well.
#
# With both mappings standing, and Write_Into gone because nothing called it:
#
#   110-token prompt, device   1.054 s -> 0.660 s -> 0.608 s
#   64 generated, device       4.541 s -> 4.292 s -> 3.626 s
#
# The generating row is the noisiest figure in this repository and 3.626 s is
# its best reading in eight sittings; the middle column of that line is the
# read-back alone. What can be said is that both steps moved it in the same
# direction and that the spread is wide enough that no stronger claim is
# available.
#
# Everything taken again in one sitting at loads of 0.54 to 2.3:
#
#   twelve tokens              0.609 s wall, 0.106 evaluating and 0.501
#                              generating, 3.52 s processor
#   the same at --arith f32    1.330 s wall, 8.61 s processor, back to back
#   fourteen threads           0.569 s wall, 6.53 s processor
#   110-token prompt           1.767 s wall, 8.75 s processor
#   64 tokens generated        2.830 s wall, 16.92 s processor
#   110-token prompt, device   0.608 s wall, 0.11 s processor
#   64 generated, device       3.626 s wall, 0.94 s processor
#   reference, four tokens     cpu 0.102 s and 0.161 s against 5.751 s and
#                              3.823 s
#   drafting, the three runs   0.604 s, 1.666 s and 3.682 s, 24 proposed and
#                              7 accepted
#   batch sizes 1 to 128       4.700, 3.175, 2.553, 2.359, 2.175, 2.021,
#                              1.889 and 1.732 s on the processor
#   llama.cpp on the same file 355.41 and 40.01 tokens a second on the
#                              processor, 1671.64 and 56.40 on the device,
#                              749.97 and 39.93 with -ngl 0
#
# The device table, cell for cell: six-token prompt with twelve generated,
# cpu 0.592 s against device 1.047 s; evaluating the prompt 0.102 s against
# 0.187 s; generating 0.506 s against 0.842 s; processor time 3.50 s against
# 0.27 s; 110-token prompt cpu 1.717 s against device 0.608 s, processor time
# 8.74 s against 0.11 s.
#
# The processor prompt has now read 1.687, 1.732 and 1.767 s across three
# sittings with nothing between them touching the processor path. That is
# what this figure repeats to, and the README quotes it loosely for that
# reason rather than moving a published number by two per cent every time
# the device changes.

# 2026-08-24, attention in shares of the heads, and a device figure with an
# open question against it.
#
# The budget entry above said attention was the largest thing left on the
# processor and the one part of a forward pass still running on the calling
# task. It runs on the pool now, a share of the heads to a worker, which
# needed one thing first: the scores are a row a head rather than one row
# shared, because a head writes its scores, softmaxes them and reads them
# back inside its own iteration.
#
#   1419-token prompt   83.757 s -> 31.043 s   (16.9 -> 45.7 tokens a second)
#   110-token prompt     1.767 s ->  1.377 s
#   64 tokens generated  2.830 s ->  2.778 s
#
# And a gate that was wrong differently from the thing it reproduces. With
# the sharing in place the gate reported four logits outside tolerance --
# all gemma3, generating a token at a time, one apart by 0.5131 against a
# bound of 0.5 -- while the same sweep run on its own was clean. The sharing
# was innocent: the gate runs the unit suite first, one of its cases runs the
# program in the same process, and the program's default arithmetic is the
# quantized one, so the flag was still set when the sweep began and the whole
# cross product ran in an arithmetic nobody asked for against the tight
# bound. The sweep sets its own arithmetic now, from the command line and
# from nothing else.
#
# Two point seven times on a long prompt and a fifth on a short one, which is
# the shape to expect: attention is quadratic in the context and the rest of
# a forward pass is linear. tests/fixtures/speed-prompt-long.txt exists so
# that the first line can be taken again; it is the 110-token prompt
# thirteen times over, which the model reads as 1419 tokens.
#
# Every digest is unchanged. A head's arithmetic does not depend on which
# heads are computed beside it, so this is the same values in a different
# order of execution rather than a change the tolerance had to allow.
#
# The batched evaluator shares the heads and loops the positions inside each
# share rather than the other way round: the same work, one hand-off a layer
# instead of one a position, which for a hundred and twenty-eight positions
# over twenty-two layers would be nearly three thousand for one prompt.
#
# Everything taken again in one sitting at loads of 0.90 to 2.6:
#
#   twelve tokens              0.589 s wall, 0.097 evaluating and 0.486
#                              generating, 3.51 s processor
#   the same at --arith f32    1.296 s wall, 8.68 s processor, back to back
#   fourteen threads           0.533 s wall, 6.17 s processor
#   110-token prompt           1.377 s wall
#   64 tokens generated        2.778 s wall
#   1419-token prompt         31.043 s wall
#   110-token prompt, device   1.696 s wall, 1.04 s processor
#   64 generated, device       4.032 s wall, 1.09 s processor
#   reference, four tokens     cpu 0.109 s and 0.176 s against 5.748 s and
#                              3.803 s
#   llama.cpp on the same file 355.39 and 39.99 tokens a second on the
#                              processor, 1680.84 and 56.32 on the device
#
# The device table, cell for cell: six-token prompt with twelve generated,
# cpu 0.597 s against device 1.035 s; evaluating the prompt 0.120 s against
# 0.193 s; generating 0.493 s against 0.841 s; processor time 3.56 s against
# 0.26 s; 110-token prompt cpu 1.445 s against device 1.696 s, processor time
# 8.87 s against 1.04 s.
#
# And the open question. The device's 110-token prompt read 0.608 s an hour
# before this sitting and reads 1.696 s in it, with the extra time appearing
# as processor time -- 1.04 s against 0.11 s. That is attention falling back
# off the device: the batched evaluator attends there only while the cache is
# resident, and something is refusing it.
#
# What it is not is the change that landed between the two readings. It was
# bisected out twice: once by running the head shares on the calling task,
# which left the figure at 1.694 s, and once by putting the scores back in a
# single row, which left it at 1.683 s. A larger --device-memory does not
# recover it either -- 1.891 s against 1.987 s at the default, which is
# noise. The weights are still resident and the run says so: 155 matrices,
# 1099071488 bytes, none given back.
#
# It is written down rather than quietly re-measured because a figure that
# moves by a factor of three with no cause found is exactly what this file is
# for, and because the --device-memory case in the suite has been
# intermittent all day with a message about a budget that did not reach the
# device. The engine reports whether the weights are resident and does not
# report whether the cache is, which is the diagnostic this wants and the
# reason the question is still open.

# 2026-08-25, later still. A session can be asked where its time went, so
# llama.adb changed and the five groups that name it moved with it. Every
# figure was taken again in one sitting rather than argued about, because the
# accounting is inside the timed region -- one branch at each phase boundary
# when nobody asked -- and the precedent this file sets for re-fingerprinting
# without re-measuring is a change outside it.
#
# Nothing moved beyond the spread these rows already have:
#
#   twelve tokens, seven workers    0.556 s wall, 3.10 s processor
#   the same at fourteen threads    0.535 s wall, 5.52 s processor
#   four tokens, reference          9.697 s wall against 0.537 s
#   six-token run                   cpu 0.537 s   device 0.576 s
#     evaluating the prompt         cpu 0.084 s   device 0.073 s
#     generating                    cpu 0.460 s   device 0.504 s
#     processor time                cpu 3.09 s    device 0.04 s
#   110-token prompt                cpu 1.240 s   device 0.549 s
#     processor time                cpu 7.10 s    device 0.11 s
#   sixty-four generated            cpu 2.551 s   device 2.908 s
#   batch sweep, 110 tokens         4.371 s at one, 1.294 s at 128
#   drafted, q2_k drafting q8_0     3.874 s, 24 proposed, 7 accepted
#   row dot, q8_0                   0.40 ns an element
#   device ratios                   q8_0 0.55 one vector, 0.14 at eight,
#                                   0.043 at thirty-two; load 1.16 to 2.09
#   llama.cpp, processor            381.9 t/s prompt, 40.2 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device              1678.9 t/s prompt, 57.6 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0             776.9 t/s
#
# The budget the accounting produces is published under "Where a prompt's
# time goes" and is not covered by any group here, on purpose: it is a
# description of one run rather than a figure to be compared across sittings,
# and the command that prints it is in the README beside it. What would make
# it a figure is somebody quoting a share of it as a fact about the program,
# and the shares are stated with the run's own wall time beside them so that
# a reader can see which it is.
#
# 2026-08-25, the budget covers a generated token as well as a batch, so
# llama.adb changed and the groups naming it were taken again. Nothing moved
# beyond the spread these rows already have:
#
#   twelve tokens, seven workers    0.480 s wall, 2.71 s processor
#   the same at fourteen threads    0.459 s wall, 4.74 s processor
#   four tokens, reference          9.711 s wall against 0.481 s
#   six-token run                   cpu 0.481 s   device 0.426 s
#     evaluating the prompt         cpu 0.086 s   device 0.052 s
#     generating                    cpu 0.394 s   device 0.375 s
#     processor time                cpu 2.73 s    device 0.04 s
#   110-token prompt                cpu 1.265 s   device 0.535 s
#     processor time                cpu 7.46 s    device 0.11 s
#   sixty-four generated            cpu 2.175 s   device 2.010 s
#   batch sweep, 110 tokens         3.821 s at one, 1.333 s at 128
#   drafted, q2_k drafting q8_0     3.911 s, 24 proposed, 7 accepted
#   llama.cpp, processor            385.1 t/s prompt, 40.0 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device              1688.4 t/s prompt, 57.7 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0             776.0 t/s
#
# The budget it now takes is published under "And where a generated token's
# goes" and is covered by no group here, for the reason the prompt's budget
# is not: it describes one run rather than being a figure to compare across
# sittings, and it is printed with that run's own wall time beside it.
#
# What it says, because it decided what not to do next. Sixty-four tokens on
# the device: feeding 1.495 s, projecting 0.369, attending 0.285, reading out
# 0.110, everything else 0.047, accounted for 2.304 of a 2.390 s run.
#
# The products are eighty-one per cent and they run at about 33 to 36
# gigabytes a second against a part that can do something near ninety. A
# layer's feed-forward is 1062 microseconds a token for 34.6 million weights
# and its projections 262 for 5.2 million -- within a tenth of each other per
# weight, and their four-to-one ratio is their weight ratio. There is no
# outlier to attack.
#
# Attending reads 12.4 per cent against a prompt's 6.2 and that is not what
# it looks like either: Attend_And_Project sends attention and the matrix
# reading its blend as one submission, so most of that is a four-megabyte
# product. What is left is about a hundred microseconds a submission and
# three submissions a layer, and collapsing those is the change recorded
# above as slower.
#
# So a generated token on the device is where a prompt on the processor is:
# the arithmetic is arranged about as well as this program knows how, and
# what remains is that the products do not reach the speed the memory could
# feed them. That is the same shape as 0.7 instructions a cycle against
# another runtime's 2.5, and on the device there is even less to see it with.
#
# covers: the twelve-token figure, the worker-count and share-count
# comparisons, and the batch-size table under "Speed". Re-measure all four
# with `tests speed --model PATH`, which reports the median of three runs of
# exactly the published run and prints a digest of what was generated; the
# batch-size table is the same command with --prompt-file and --batch-size,
# and both commands are printed beside their figures in the README. The
# processor figures come from the operating system's timing tool, as the
# README says.
# 2026-08-11, sliding-window attention. The engine and reference groups both
# list model_runner-llama.adb, which changed: the two attention loops now
# start at the earliest position the window allows instead of at zero.
#
# The figures were NOT re-measured, and the reason is that the change cannot
# have moved them. Every model the published figures were taken on -- and
# every model without an attention.sliding_window key -- has Window = 0, for
# which Earliest returns zero, so both loops run over exactly the positions
# they ran over before and the arithmetic is unchanged. What is added is one
# comparison per head per layer per token: for TinyLlama, 32 heads times 22
# layers, against something over a billion multiply-adds for the same token.
#
# Not re-measuring is a judgement, so here is what it rests on and what would
# overturn it: the model those figures need is not on this machine, and
# nothing in this repository may require one. If a windowed model is ever
# measured, it needs its own figures rather than these -- a narrower window
# does less arithmetic per token, so it would be faster, and none of the
# numbers below would describe it.
# 2026-08-11, mixture of experts. The same two groups again, and the same
# judgement, for the same reason: a model without expert metadata has
# Experts = 0, and every one of the additions is inside a branch on that.
# The dense path runs the products it ran before, in the order it ran them,
# with the loop bounds it had. What is added to it is one test of an integer
# per layer per token. The figures were NOT re-measured and the model they
# need is still not on this machine.
#
# A mixture model would need its own figures and could not use these: it
# runs its feed-forward block a position at a time rather than a batch at a
# time, and it runs several experts where a dense model runs one block, so
# neither the twelve-token figure nor the batch-size table would describe it.
# 2026-08-11, rotary scaling. This one touches code every model runs, not a
# branch a model opts into, so the reasoning has to be arithmetic rather than
# "it is behind an if".
#
# Per rotated pair the rotation now does: one more multiply to form the
# stretched angle, a mix of two angles whose weight is loop-invariant and zero
# for an unscaled model, and two multiplies applying a magnitude that is one.
# Call it six floating-point operations a pair, against the power, the cosine
# and the sine that pair already costs -- on the order of a hundred cycles.
# For TinyLlama that is 36 heads by 32 pairs by 22 layers, about 25 thousand
# pairs a token, so the rotation is under one percent of a token's work and it
# grew by a few percent of that. The published figure is a median of three
# wall-clock runs; this is two orders of magnitude below what that can see.
#
# The figures were NOT re-measured and the model they need is still not on
# this machine. This is the third such note in a row and they are cumulative:
# taken together the three changes add, to a model with no window, no experts
# and no scaling, one integer test per layer per token, one more per layer,
# and the six operations above per rotated pair. Still nothing a stopwatch
# reaches. If that stops being true, the note has to become a measurement.
# 2026-08-11, separate key and value head widths. The cache is now two
# widths rather than one and the attention loops index it with two strides.
# For a model where the two widths are equal -- every model these figures
# were taken on, and every model that states neither width -- the strides are
# the strides that were there, the loop bounds are the bounds that were
# there, and the value cache is the size it was. What is added is one more
# multiply per layer per token to form the second base offset.
#
# The figures were NOT re-measured, and the model they need is not on this
# machine. A model whose value heads are wider would hold a larger cache and
# do more work per position; these figures would not describe it, and it
# would need its own.
# 2026-08-11, qwen3 and qwen3moe. Two architectures added to an enumeration
# and one new thing computed: a normalization of each query head and each key
# head before the rotation. That runs only for a model whose architecture
# has it -- the gain is null for llama and qwen2, and the call is behind a
# test of that -- so a llama model, which is what these figures were taken
# on, does one null pointer comparison more per layer per token.
#
# The figures were NOT re-measured; the model they need is not on this
# machine. A qwen3 model would need its own: normalizing every head is a pass
# over the query and key projections that a llama model does not make.
# The engine and reference groups list quantization.adb, which gained two
# formats as two branches of a case that dispatches by format. A model in
# any other format never enters them. Not re-measured, and the model those
# figures need is still not on this machine.
# The engine and reference groups list llama.adb, where the attention of one
# position moved out of both evaluation paths into a procedure of its own and
# a second was written beside it for the halved cache. The exact path does
# what it did -- same loops, same bounds, same arithmetic -- now behind one
# call per position per layer, which is a call against a head's worth of
# multiply-accumulates. The default is unchanged and the model these figures
# need is still not on this machine.
# 2026-08-11, the embedding command. llama.adb gained an accessor that copies
# the final hidden state out of a session; nothing on the evaluation path
# calls it and nothing else in the file changed. Not re-measured, and the
# model those figures need is not on this machine.
# 2026-08-11, adapters and the grammar. llama.adb gained a merge that runs
# once at load and touches no evaluation path, and one field recording how
# the weights were packed. The generation loop gained a test of a null
# reference per token, and the whole grammar filter is behind it. Neither
# figure is taken with an adapter or a grammar. Not re-measured, and the
# model those figures need is not on this machine.
# 2026-08-12, batched embedding. llama.adb's batched evaluation gained an
# optional buffer of every position's hidden state, written only when a
# caller passes one; generation passes none. Nothing else on either path
# changed. Not re-measured, and the model those figures need is not on this
# machine.
# 2026-08-12, saved contexts. llama.adb gained a snapshot of the committed
# cache and a reader for one back. Neither is on any evaluation path: one
# runs when a caller asks for the bytes and the other before a prompt is
# read. Not re-measured, and the model those figures need is not on this
# machine.
# 2026-08-12, what a merge does to a model's identity. llama.adb records a
# digest of each adapter merged and the fingerprint mixes it in. Both run at
# load and neither is on an evaluation path. Not re-measured, and the model
# those figures need is not on this machine.
# 2026-08-12, the device backend. Two changes to llama.adb reach these
# groups. Llama.Resolve gained two parameters and now asks the backend about
# the format it will read rather than the one the file holds; that runs once
# per tensor during Prepare, which both figures quote separately from what
# they measure -- the same argument the entry above makes, and the same one
# the file's own rule allows. Llama.Product_Batch gained a `when
# Backend_Device` alternative to a case statement that already had one for
# every other backend; the alternative these figures take is unchanged, and
# an alternative not taken is not work done.
#
# Re-measured anyway, because the model is on this machine now and the entries
# above could not say that: `tests speed` reports 1.72, 2.11 and 1.78 s
# against the published 1.28. That is not republished and the reason is the
# machine rather than the code -- three other agents were running on it, at a
# load average of 2.4 on eight cores, and the published figure was taken on a
# quiet one. A figure taken under contention would replace a measurement with
# a coincidence. What the run does say is that the generated text is
# unchanged: the same output digest, 7784f071e5740782, in all three.
# 2026-08-12, the shared matrix walk and the backend memory check. Both are
# in Prepare, which both figures quote separately from what they measure --
# the same argument as the two entries above and the same one the file's rule
# allows. Re-measured anyway: the twelve-token run reads 1.723 s against the
# published 1.28, on a machine running three other agents at a load average
# of 2.5 on eight cores. Not republished for that reason, and the generated
# text is unchanged: the same output digest, 7784f071e5740782.
# 2026-08-15, falcon. The four groups that carry llama.adb moved with it and
# are not re-measured, for two reasons and both are worth stating.
#
# What was added to a loop: the normalization sites now call a Normalize that
# asks whether the architecture centres its normalization and whether a bias
# is present, instead of calling K.RMS_Norm directly. That is two tests and a
# call at each normalization -- twice a layer a token, and once more at the
# end -- beside a full pass over the same vector, and the vector is the one
# the matrix products behind it read hundreds of times over. It cannot show at
# the resolution these figures have: two runs of the twelve-token figure an
# hour apart on this machine differ by fifteen per cent, which is recorded a
# few entries above. The feed-forward branch was rearranged so that the
# projection down is written once rather than once an arrangement; it does the
# same work. Layer_Norm is new and no architecture but falcon reaches it.
#
# And what stopped a measurement being taken anyway: this machine has no model
# file on it, so `tests speed` has nothing to run. The earlier entries in this
# file that say "re-measured anyway" were written when one was here. That is
# recorded rather than left to be inferred from a missing figure.

# 2026-08-15, phi2. The same four groups moved with llama.adb again. What
# was added to a loop this time is an addition: a bias is added to what a
# projection produced, at four places a layer and once at the end, and each
# addition is one pass over a vector the projection had just written
# hundreds of products into. Every architecture but this one has a null
# there and tests it once. Not re-measured, and this machine still has no
# model file to measure with, which is the same reason recorded under the
# engine group above rather than a second one.

# 2026-08-16, the byte cache. The four groups carrying llama.adb moved with
# it. What was added to a loop is a third storage: a session that asks for it
# packs a row on the way in and unpacks an element on the way out, both in
# the attention loop, and a session that does not ask for it tests one
# enumeration value more per position than it did. The published figures are
# taken at the default precision, which allocates and touches none of it.
# Not re-measured, and this machine still has no model file to measure with,
# which is the reason recorded under the engine group above.

# 2026-08-16, --kv-cache in the timing tool. All four groups list
# speed_run.adb, which gained a parameter naming the storage a session keeps
# its context in. No engine source moved with it.
#
# And a model file exists on this machine at last, so the reason recorded
# three times above -- that there was nothing to measure with -- no longer
# holds. The engine group's twelve-token figure was taken again on
# TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, the model every one of these figures describes:
# 1.871 s wall against the published 1.88, 10.76 s of processor time, at a
# load of 1.44 rising to 2.12, and the generated text is the same digest the
# README publishes -- 5abff916f9d83ca6. The figure holds.
#
# The other three groups are not re-measured. What moved is the tool's
# parameter list and nothing they time, and the run above is the evidence
# that the tool still measures what it measured.

# load: the twelve-token figure at 1.28 rising to 1.82, 2026-08-14, and it
#       now carries its processor time from this repository's own tool
#       rather than from the shell. The worker-count and batch-size tables
#       beside it was retaken too: fourteen threads and seven, both at
#       about 1.25, 2026-08-14. The batch-size table is the last part of
#       this group with no load recorded.
# 2026-08-17, gpt2. The four groups carrying llama.adb moved with it. What
# changed in a loop is one test a token: a model with a table of positions
# adds a row to the embedding, and a model without one asks whether it has a
# table and moves on. Everything else gpt2 needed was already there. Not
# re-measured on that account, and the twelve-token figure was taken again
# on this model two days ago at 1.871 s against the published 1.88.

# 2026-08-18, gpt2's output bias. The four groups carry llama.adb, which
# stopped asking gpt2 for an output.bias -- one enumeration value removed
# from one test at load time, in a branch no published figure's model takes.
# Not re-measured on that account. The twelve-token figure was taken on this
# model two days ago at 1.871 s against the published 1.88.

# 2026-08-18, gpt2's feed-forward normalization. The groups carrying
# llama.adb moved with it. What changed in a loop is that the feed-forward
# normalization now goes through the same call the attention normalization
# does, which decides between a centred normalization and a root-mean-square
# one -- one test a layer a token, and for every architecture but gpt2 it
# lands where it landed before. Re-measured rather than reasoned about,
# because a branch in the innermost loop is exactly the kind of change that
# is argued to be free and is not: twelve tokens on TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0,
# median of three, 1.682 s wall against the published 1.88, 9.56 s of
# processor time, at a load of 1.47 rising to 2.15. The figure holds.

# 2026-08-20, attention on the device from both evaluators. The four groups
# carry llama.adb, which gained two subprograms and three call sites: a
# position's keys and values are written to a cache a device holds, and a
# position attends to that cache there rather than here. This was already
# said while generating; saying it while a batch is evaluated is what this
# change adds, and the reason it had to be added is that a drafted run
# checks its proposals through the batched evaluator while it generates
# through the other, so a device wired into one and not the other made a
# drafted run disagree with an undrafted one.
#
# The device group is re-measured; this group is not, and the reason is
# that the code cannot run here. Put_Position returns on its first
# statement unless the session's backend is the device one, and
# Attend_There is reached only when Put_Position has come back saying the
# device took the position, which off the device it never does. What these
# figures pay for the change is one enumeration comparison per position per
# layer, on a path that reads a matrix per position per layer.
#
# That is an arithmetic argument rather than a measurement, so it was worth
# checking that the arithmetic is the whole story: this group's twelve-token
# figure was taken again on the model above and read 4.016 s against the
# published 1.869, at a load of 1.44 rising to 2.51 on a machine with a
# virtual machine of somebody else's on it at two thirds of a processor.
# That reading is not published and does not overturn anything -- it is a
# busy machine, in the direction and roughly the size a busy machine moves
# this figure -- but it is recorded here because a figure not taken is worth
# less than a figure taken badly and said to be bad.

# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat v1.0 Q8_0, as TheBloke published it -- tinyllama-1.1b-chat-v1.0.Q8_0.gguf, 1170781568 bytes.

# Worker counts, same sitting: eight shares 0.470 s for 2.63 s of processor

# wake, and waking only the team is a change to the coordinator rather than

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the six-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel -- the last floating-point path in a "_M" file. Every
# figure here is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.763 s for the
# 110-token prompt against 0.719, 2.159 s for sixty-four generated against
# 2.187, and every digest what it was.
#
# Q4_K_M, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:
#
#   thirty-two generated, six-bit on the floating-point path   0.505 s
#   thirty-two generated, six-bit on the byte product          0.356 s
#   110-token prompt, either way                        1.017 / 0.983 s
