# and 4b6e8e99ae285b2a in all six. Thirty-five milliseconds a token where
# the eight-bit file takes thirty-three: a file two thirds the size now
# generates at the same rate.
#
# One of the two single-vector kernels did not work at first, and the reason
# is worth keeping. Every insertion written before them advances the
# pointers it was handed -- an addq on a register declared an input -- which
# tells the compiler nothing and works only while it has no other use for
# that register. With eleven operands it stopped working: the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker, and *adding an exception handler and nothing else
# made it run*, which is the signature of code generation rather than
# arithmetic. Both single-vector kernels now keep their cursors in registers
# of their own, named in the clobber list, and advance no operand. The
# four-bit one had the same latent fault and had already passed a gate.
#
# The drafting threshold has now read 17, 10, 6 and 9 milliseconds a token
# across four sittings of unchanged code. It is a difference of two
# twelve-token runs and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up;
# the README says so and says to read the conclusion rather than the number.

# 2026-08-26, and the figures above were NOT re-taken. This entry is the
# other thing this file allows -- saying why a change cannot have moved
# them -- and it is the first time that has been used rather than a sitting.
#
# What changed: every machine code insertion in the integer kernels stopped
# advancing the pointers it was handed. They did it with an addq on a
# register declared an input, which tells the compiler nothing and works
# only while it has no other use for that register; the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker when it stopped working, and an exception handler
# and nothing else made it run again. Four insertions still did it, all of
# them working and all of them having passed gates, including Rows_Singly,
# which every generated eight-bit token runs and which advanced four
# operands -- more than the one that failed. All six now keep cursors in
# registers of their own and copy the counter rather than decrementing it in
# place.
#
# Why it cannot have moved the figures, and the evidence rather than the
# argument. Three alternated rounds on both models, in one sitting:
#
#                                     before      after
#   eight-bit, sixty-four generated   2.085 s     2.086 s
#   eight-bit, 110-token prompt       0.767 s     0.765 s
#   four-bit, thirty-two generated    0.357 s     0.362 s
#
# and every digest identical -- 448c2ed68ec342ee, cbf29ce484222325,
# 4b6e8e99ae285b2a. That is a better instrument than a fresh sitting would
# be: alternated rounds in one sitting resolve four per cent, and the spread
# between sittings on this machine is larger than that. Re-measuring
# everything would have replaced numbers known not to have moved with
# numbers differing by more than the change does.
#
# Rows_Singly is one instruction a block shorter than it was, three pointer
# advances having become two, which is the only arithmetic difference and is
# below what any figure here resolves.

# 2026-08-27, a device run gets a worker pool, and the loops it never had
# one for are shared out: a long device prompt 1.44 times faster.
#
# The entry above measured that joining, normalizing and rotating are
# twenty-two per cent of a device prompt, that they are host loops and not
# dispatches, and that --threads 1 and --threads 8 gave the same time with
# the same processor time. It named the blocker as Item.Post_Room, one
# scratch row shared by every position. That was half of it.
#
# The other half was that a device run had no pool at all. Every place that
# opens a session decided one on the backend's Supports_Parallel, which says
# whether that backend's own products divide across workers -- and the
# device's do not: they divide across the device. But a run is not only its
# products. So --threads on a device run had nothing to change, and the
# loops had nothing to be shared out to.
#
# Both halves are fixed. Three loops over the batch -- the input
# normalization and the two residual joins -- go to Workers_CPU.
# Dispatch_Shares, which was already there and already used a few lines
# below for attention. A share takes its own scratch row for as long as it
# runs, and only where the architecture has a normalization on the way out
# of a sublayer: Post_Norm returns at once when it has none, which is every
# llama, so the common case allocates nothing. And the speed tool and the
# command both give a device run a pool.
#
# Not the rotation, which is the fourth such loop: it writes the key and
# value cache, and on a device that is a call through an engine that is one
# task's to use. It is 0.193 s and stays there.
#
# Bit for bit the same answers -- a position's arithmetic is independent of
# every other position's, so a share of the batch is the same operations in
# the same order. 1a26d24d33b8957b and 448c2ed68ec342ee both ways.
#
# Three alternated rounds:
#
#   1419-token device prompt   3.626 s -> 2.522 s   1.44x
#   110-token device prompt    0.241 s -> 0.205 s   1.18x
#   64 generated               1.558 s -> 1.597 s   unchanged
#   110-token processor prompt 0.744 s -> 0.744 s   unchanged
#
# Generating does not move: a batch of one is below the sixteen this asks
# for before it goes to the pool, and generating takes the token-at-a-time
# path in any case. The processor does not move either, and that is worth
# saying: it had a pool all along, so all it gains is the loops themselves
# being shared, which measured 0.019 s to 0.007 on normalizing and 0.023 to
# 0.006 on joining -- five per cent of a processor prompt, inside its noise.
#
# By phase, and this is the part that was not predicted:
#
#   NORMALIZING   0.250 s -> 0.129 s
#   JOINING       0.393 s -> 0.204 s
#   ROTATING      0.194 s -> 0.193 s
#   PROJECTING    0.654 s -> 0.502 s
#   ATTENDING     1.314 s -> 1.038 s
#   FEEDING       1.068 s -> 0.908 s
#   accounted     3.904 s -> 3.001 s
#
# The two shared loops account for 0.310 s of the 0.903. The other 0.593 is
# in phases that dispatch to the device, and the likeliest reading is that
# the host was the thing keeping the device waiting: a submission cannot be
# made while the task that makes it is normalizing a batch. That is a
# reading and not a measurement, and it is the second time today a phase has
# moved for something that happened in another one.
#
# What it cost elsewhere. `inspect` now reports three worker tasks for a
# device run where it reported one, and the suite caught that -- the number
# was asserted, and it was asserted as one because that was true. It is
# three now because that is what the run uses.

# Five groups name the sources this touched and every one of their figures
# was re-measured, all on 2026-08-27 at a load between 0.68 and 1.18.
#
# The twelve-token figure, which is the engine group's: 0.452 s wall --
# 0.073 evaluating and 0.383 generating -- for 1.63 s of processor time,
# loading 0.067 s, against the 0.450 and 1.61 recorded. Unmoved, and it
# should be: twelve tokens from a six-token prompt never reaches a batch of
# sixteen. At --arith f32 it is 1.247 s for 8.50 s against 1.387 and 8.77.
#
# The worker count, same sitting: fourteen threads 0.470 s wall for 1.88 s
# of processor, seven threads 0.453 s for 1.63 s. **Four per cent worse for
# fifteen per cent more processor**, where the last reading of this pair was
# one per cent better for seventy-eight per cent more. The processor times
# themselves are far below what that entry recorded -- 1.88 and 1.63 against
# 4.75 and 2.71 -- and that is not this change: those two figures are older
# than several kernels and were carried through restampings without being
# taken again. They are taken again now. The bargain a second worker on a
# core offers has been six different bargains on this one machine and this
# is the sixth.
#
# The reference comparison: cpu 0.074 s evaluating and 0.126 generating,
# reference 5.778 and 3.816, both printing 5f85f06494f88de4. Forty-eight
# times the work in total, seventy-eight on the prompt and thirty on the
# generation, against forty-eight, eighty-four and thirty.
#
# The two-row table under "The device backend", both columns:
#
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       cpu 0.458 s   device 0.362 s
#     -- evaluating the prompt             0.077         0.064
#     -- generating                        0.380         0.298
#     -- processor time                    1.64          0.03
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated    0.740         0.211
#     -- processor time                    3.79          0.15
#
# The device's 110-token row is where this change lands: 0.243 to 0.211.
# Its processor time rises from 0.08 to 0.15 because the work is now done
# by more than one task, which is the point.
#
# The drafting table: eight bits 0.461 s, two bits 1.673 s, the first
# drafted by the second 3.905 s with twenty-four proposed and nine accepted,
# against 0.449, 1.798 and 3.735. The sign is unchanged and so is the
# arithmetic under it.
#
# And the llama.cpp comparison, both programs in one sitting, llama.cpp at
# 95b8e33e1 build 10597, three rounds. The engine's rows are 110 tokens in
# 0.678 s and 64 generated in 2.062 s on the processor, 0.200 s and 1.575 s
# on the device.
#
#   model_runner, processor    162.2 t/s prompt, 31.0 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, processor       344.5 t/s prompt, 40.0 t/s generating
#   model_runner, device       550.0 t/s prompt, 40.6 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device         1657.8 t/s prompt, 56.3 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0        773.9 t/s, which is the device answering
#
# The gap is 2.1 and 1.3 on the processor, **3.0 and 1.4 on the device**.
# The device prompt row has gone 412.0, 424.7, 438.2, 464.1 and now 550.0
# across five sittings today, and the gap on it 4.0 to 3.0.
#
# The fifteen per-format ratios and the three-way table of where the weights
# are were not re-taken: neither benchmark opens a session, so neither
# reaches a loop this changed.

# 2026-08-27, the processor's scores in binary32: level, and the reason is
# that the compiler will not vectorize a reduction however it is written.
#
# The item was that the score dot product -- twenty-seven per cent of a
# processor prompt -- converts two binary32 operands to binary64, multiplies
# in binary64 and accumulates in binary64, in one serial accumulator; that
# the device has always computed the same scores in binary32; and that the
# conformance sweep holds both backends at the same tolerance, so the
# precision question was already answered on the other side.
#
# The history was checked first, as it should have been. The only reason
# recorded for the wider accumulator is accuracy, and the size of what it
# buys is written down: the shader's binary32 differs from the processor's
# binary64 by 2.2e-05 at worst in absolute terms, under the sweep's floor.
# Nothing said the processor needed it.
#
# So it was changed: binary32, in eight accumulators, eight being what a
# register holds of them. Two alternated rounds on a 1419-token prompt:
#
#   binary64, one accumulator    14.520 s, 14.886 s
#   binary32, eight accumulators 14.783 s, 14.640 s
#
# **Level.** And the answers did not move either -- 1a26d24d33b8957b both
# ways -- which is worth noticing on its own about how little of a score's
# last bits reaches a token.
#
# What the object file says, and this is the finding. In the procedure that
# holds both loops:
#
#   the committed score loop     14 mulsd, 13 addsd    scalar binary64
#   the same loop in binary32    25 mulss, 72 addss    scalar binary32
#   the value blend beside it    34 mulpd, 32 addpd    packed binary64
#
# **The score loop is scalar in both formats and the value blend twenty
# lines below it is packed.** The difference between them is not the format
# and not the accumulator count: the blend is a map -- every component of
# its output is independent, and GNAT vectorizes it at -O3 without being
# asked -- and the score is a reduction. Written as eight independent lanes,
# which is a map by construction, it still came out as eight scalar chains;
# hoisting the operands into renamed slices so the addresses are plainly
# contiguous did not change one instruction.
#
# So the binary32 version replaced one scalar chain with eight, and since
# the loop is not latency-bound -- four accumulators bought seven per cent,
# which is what a loop says when the chain is not what holds it -- eight
# scalar chains are no better than one. Not kept.
#
# This also explains today's other three null results on this backend, which
# looked unrelated and are the same thing: RMS_Norm reassociated bought one
# per cent, four accumulators here bought seven, and binary32 bought
# nothing. **Every arrangement of scalar arithmetic is scalar arithmetic.**
# The loop issues about sixty-four scalar multiply-adds where a packed unit
# would issue eight, and no way of writing Ada found today makes GNAT emit
# the eight.
#
# What would. A machine-code insertion with a runtime capability check,
# which is the mechanism this project already uses for exactly this reason:
# model_runner-quantization-integers-kernels.adb is four thousand lines of
# it and Platform.Instructions is what asks the processor first. The score
# dot product is a fused multiply-add over sixty-four contiguous binary32
# pairs and is a smaller insertion than any of those. That is the next
# thing on this backend, and it is the only thing left that the measurements
# have not already ruled out.
#
# No source changed.

# 2026-08-27, the processor's attention read the key cache once a head. The
# same loop swap, on the other backend.
#
# The item was the processor prompt at 2.2 times behind and never given the
# floor treatment. Its budget, on a 1419-token prompt: attending 6.79 s,
# feeding 6.91, projecting 1.98, and everything else under a fifth of a
# second. Attending and feeding are eighty-five per cent of it, and the
# whole prompt is 15.66 s against the device's 2.24.
#
# The floor, taken the way the device's was -- the score dot product emptied
# and nothing else touched:
#
#   attending      6.789 s -> 2.385 s
#   accounted for 16.110 s -> 11.964 s
#
# So **the score dot product alone is sixty-five per cent of attending and
# twenty-seven per cent of the whole prompt**. That is the single largest
# thing in a processor prompt and nothing here had ever named it.
#
# Two things were tried on it.
#
# **Four accumulators.** It sums into one binary64 accumulator a component
# at a time, which is a dependency chain nothing can vectorize -- and this
# file has a profile from earlier work saying fifty-four per cent of the
# samples in this procedure were on 64-bit moves. Breaking the chain into
# four bought seven per cent of attending and four per cent of the prompt,
# and reassociates a sum every published digest depends on. Not kept, for
# the same reason the same change to RMS_Norm was not: the trade is a per
# cent or four for every figure in this file.
#
# **The position outside the head.** The head loop was outermost, so each
# head walked the whole key cache for itself and the next head walked it
# again: 363 kilobytes of keys a group at 1419 positions, streamed once for
# each of the eight heads that share them. With the position outside, the
# heads of a share read the same key row one after another and it is in the
# nearest cache for all but the first.
#
# This is the same shape as the change the device's attention had an hour
# ago and the same shape the value blend twenty lines below already had --
# its own comment says "a component at a time was one pass over the whole
# cache per component" and gives the reason. The score loop was left.
#
# Three alternated rounds, better in every one, waiting for the load to fall
# below 1.0 before each because a seven-worker run of this length leaves it
# above 2:
#
#   1419-token processor prompt   15.292 s -> 14.669 s   1.042x
#   110-token processor prompt     0.731 s ->  0.703 s   1.04x
#
# **Bit for bit what it replaces**, and this time that was checked rather
# than predicted: each score is the same expression over the same components
# in the same order, only computed at a different moment, and no two scores
# touch. 1a26d24d33b8957b either way.
#
# Four per cent is small for a loop that is twenty-seven per cent of the
# prompt, and what that says is that the loop is not bound by the key
# traffic either -- four accumulators bought seven per cent and cache reuse
# bought fifteen per cent of the loop, so between them a fifth. The rest is
# the binary64 conversion and the arithmetic itself, and on this backend
# that is where the remaining gap is. It is the same answer the device gave
# this morning, arrived at on the other side.
#
# Kept because it is bit-exact, because it is the shape the loop beside it
# already has, and because what it buys grows with the context: at a
# thousand positions the key cache does not fit a cache and at a hundred it
# nearly does.

# Five groups name model_runner-llama.adb and every one of their figures was
# re-measured, one sitting, load 0.62 to 0.89 at the start of each.
#
#   twelve tokens          0.448 s wall, 0.074 prompt, 0.378 generating,
#                          1.60 s of processor, loading 0.068
#   the same at --arith f32  1.388 s for 8.82 s
#   fourteen threads       0.450 s wall for 1.89 s of processor
#   seven threads          0.456 s wall for 1.63 s
#
# The worker pair has changed sign again -- one per cent better at fourteen
# for sixteen per cent more processor, where the last sitting read four per
# cent worse for fifteen per cent more. Both readings are inside what this
# pair resolves and the honest summary is that the second worker on a core
# buys nothing either way. That is the seventh reading of it.
#
#   reference, four tokens   cpu 0.075 s prompt and 0.126 generating
#                            reference 5.813 and 3.872, both 5f85f06494f88de4
#                            forty-eight times in total, seventy-eight on the
#                            prompt, thirty-one on the generation
#
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       cpu 0.460 s   device 0.340 s
#     -- evaluating the prompt             0.075         0.054
#     -- generating                        0.384         0.286
#     -- processor time                    1.63          0.03
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated    0.753         0.165
#     -- processor time                    3.85          0.17
#
#   drafting: eight bits 0.475 s, two bits 1.742 s, the first drafted by the
#   second 3.762 s with twenty-four proposed and nine accepted
#
# And the llama.cpp comparison, both programs in one sitting, llama.cpp at
# 95b8e33e1 build 10597, three rounds. The engine's rows are 110 tokens in
# 0.779 s and 64 generated in 2.079 s on the processor, 0.169 s and 1.540 s
# on the device.
#
#   model_runner, processor    141.2 t/s prompt, 30.8 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, processor       352.0 t/s prompt, 40.0 t/s generating
#   model_runner, device       650.9 t/s prompt, 41.6 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device         1670.5 t/s prompt, 56.2 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0        769.4 t/s, which is the device answering
#
# The gap is 2.5 and 1.3 on the processor, 2.6 and 1.4 on the device.
#
# **The processor prompt row moved the wrong way**: 141.2 against 153.4 in
# the sitting before, for a change three alternated rounds put at four per
# cent faster. The A/B is the measurement and the row is a sitting; this
# file has said so before and here is the plainest instance of it yet. The
# device row reads 650.9 against 670.7 for a change that did not touch it.

# 2026-08-27, attention loaded every value four times. A device prompt is
# 1.14 times faster for swapping two loops.
#
# The item this answers was attention's uncoalesced key reads, and the entry
# above retired it: the tile is short of arithmetic and not of memory, and
# attention agrees -- confining its reads to a cache-resident window bought
# 1.18 times, so its whole memory cost is fifteen per cent and the fix
# wanted shared-memory staging, which is nought for four on this device.
# Same target, corrected mechanism. Attention is thirty-five per cent of a
# device prompt and does 182 gigaflops of arithmetic in about a second,
# which is 175 gigaflops a second on a part that does 3520 on a product.
#
# What was wrong. The weighted sum of values ran the query loop outside the
# position loop:
#
#   for each query i:
#      for each position j:  got += tile[i][j] * kv[value(j, c)]
#
# so kv[value(j, c)] -- one address, one value -- was loaded once for every
# query of the block. Four loads for four multiply-adds where one will do.
# The dot product above it already had the shape the other way round, which
# is why the query block bought 1.5 times there and this did not notice.
#
# Swapped, the value is read once and multiplied into every query:
#
#   1419-token device prompt   2.544 s -> 2.235 s   1.14x
#   110-token device prompt    0.203 s -> 0.160 s   1.27x
#   64 generated               1.591 s -> 1.592 s   unchanged
#
# Generating does not move and its digest does not change: a batch of one
# takes the subgroup kernel, where QUERIES is one and the swap collapses to
# the same code.
#
# **The prompt's answers do change**, and this file said they would not.
# Each query still accumulates over the positions in the same order and the
# expressions are the same, so the arithmetic is identical as written; what
# differs is which multiply-adds the compiler fuses, and GLSL lets it choose
# unless a variable is declared precise. 1a26d24d33b8957b becomes
# b3d99fb4151edc6d. The conformance sweep is the check the tile's own
# half-precision operands are held to and it passes at 28344 sequences with
# none outside tolerance.
#
# The phase counter moved by 0.055 s where the run moved by 0.310. That is
# the fourth time today, and by now it is not a surprise but a property:
# these counters are host wall around asynchronous submissions and a change
# to a kernel shows up wherever the next fence happens to be waited on.
#
# What was re-measured, one sitting at a load of 0.52 to 0.82. The two-row
# table under "The device backend":
#
#   6-token prompt, 12 generated       cpu 0.444 s   device 0.343 s
#     -- evaluating the prompt             0.071         0.055
#     -- generating                        0.374         0.288
#     -- processor time                    1.61          0.03
#   110-token prompt, nothing generated    0.747         0.159
#     -- processor time                    3.84          0.17
#
# And the llama.cpp comparison, both programs in one sitting, llama.cpp at
# 95b8e33e1 build 10597, three rounds. The engine's rows are 110 tokens in
# 0.717 s and 64 generated in 2.056 s on the processor, 0.164 s and 1.551 s
# on the device.
#
#   model_runner, processor    153.4 t/s prompt, 31.1 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, processor       341.6 t/s prompt, 39.9 t/s generating
#   model_runner, device       670.7 t/s prompt, 41.3 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device         1693.7 t/s prompt, 56.3 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0        752.9 t/s, which is the device answering
#
# The gap is 2.2 and 1.3 on the processor, **2.5 and 1.4 on the device**.
# The device prompt row has gone 412.0, 424.7, 438.2, 464.1, 550.0 and now
# 670.7 across six sittings today, and the gap on it 4.0 to 2.5.
#
# The per-format ratios and the three-way table of where the weights are
# were not re-taken: neither benchmark makes an attention call.

# 2026-08-27, the batched product is short of arithmetic and not of memory.
# Six times the bytes costs a quarter more time.
#
# The entry above ended by saying this file had no instrument that separated
# what the tile does per flop from the memory it reads. It has one now. One
# shape, one batch, every format the tile decodes: the flops are identical
# and only the bytes differ, so what changes between rows is the traffic and
# nothing else.
#
# Sixteen thousand three hundred and eighty-four rows by two thousand and
# forty-eight, a hundred and twenty-eight vectors, best of three passes,
# two rounds averaged:
#
#   f16    2.00 bytes a weight   64 MiB   2737 Gflop/s
#   bf16   2.00                  64       2624
#   q8_0   1.06                  34       3066
#   q6_k   0.82                  26       3132
#   q5_k   0.69                  22       3176
#   q4_k   0.56                  18       3236
#   q4_0   0.56                  18       3225
#   q2_k   0.33                  10       3358
#
# **Six point one times the bytes for one point two five times the time.**
# If the tile were bound by memory the two-bit format would be six times the
# eight-bit one's speed; it is nine per cent.
#
# Fitting time = arithmetic + bytes across the two extremes:
#
#   the arithmetic         3.52 teraflops a second
#   the marginal byte rate 87 gigabytes a second
#
# and for the eight-bit format that puts the memory at fifteen per cent of
# the time and the arithmetic at eighty-five. Eighty-seven gigabytes a
# second is what row_product.comp's own note calls "about ninety" for this
# part: **the weight reads are already at the bus.** There is nothing to win
# by coalescing them better, by laying the weights out differently, or by
# making them smaller.
#
# What this retires, which is the point of having asked. A smaller weight
# format is not a faster one on this device -- q4_k and q8_0 are within six
# per cent. A half-precision anything on the weight side buys nothing. A
# better weight layout buys nothing. Every idea whose mechanism is "fewer
# bytes" or "better-shaped bytes" is answered.
#
# What is left is flops per clock out of the matrix instruction. Twelve
# compute units at about 2.7 GHz doing 256 half-precision flops a clock each
# would be 8.3 teraflops a second, so 3.52 is about forty-two per cent of
# it -- and that peak is arithmetic on a number this file has not measured,
# so it is quoted as an order and not as a figure.
#
# It also raises the prior on attention, which is measured separately and
# agrees: confining its reads to a cache-resident window bought 1.18 times
# after the query block went in. **Both device kernels are short of
# arithmetic and neither is short of memory**, which is a coherent thing to
# know about this part and was not knowable from any single measurement.
#
# One note on the instrument. Taken a pass apiece these eight read a spread
# of sixty per cent between rounds and disagreed about which format was
# fastest -- a first reading had f16 twenty-one per cent slower than bf16,
# which a second round showed was noise and which would have been published
# as a finding about unpackHalf2x16. Grains takes the best of three passes
# now, for the reason Measure_Device_Ratio already states.
#
# tests/src/device_bench.adb is the only source changed, and no figure group
# names it.

# 2026-08-27, the narrow projection was the instrument. The tile does 3.4
# teraflops a second and the shape table was measuring a round trip.
#
# The item said the grouped keys and values run at 455 gigaflops a second
# against 1900 to 3200 for every other shape, that workgroup starvation was
# confirmed but not the whole story, and that what else bound it was
# unestablished. What else bound it is that nothing did.
#
# `tests device-bench` times Products.Multiply, which uploads the
# activation, records a command buffer, submits it, waits on a fence and
# copies the answer back. That round trip costs the same whatever the matrix
# is. Holding the batch and the columns still and sweeping only the rows --
# one workgroup to sixty-four, so sixty-four times the arithmetic:
#
#   rows    workgroups   seconds     apparent Gflop/s
#     32        1        0.000252            67
#     64        2        0.000292           115
#    128        4        0.000330           203
#    256        8        0.000351           382
#    512       16        0.000374           719
#   1024       32        0.000456          1177
#   2048       64        0.000595          1805
#
# **Sixty-four times the work for two and a third times the time.** A least
# squares fit is 0.289 ms fixed plus 0.154 microseconds a row, and that
# marginal slope is **3.41 teraflops a second**. Differencing adjacent pairs
# from 128 rows up gives 3.19, 6.08, 3.25 and 3.87, so the fit is not an
# artefact of the fit.
#
# The narrow projection does 0.13 gigaflop of arithmetic. At 3.4 Tflop/s
# that is 0.038 ms, on top of 0.289 ms of round trip: **eighty-eight per
# cent of its measured time was the instrument**. There is nothing wrong
# with the shape, the sixfold spread across the table is the fixed cost
# dividing into different amounts of work, and the number to hold about the
# tile is 3.4 Tflop/s rather than anything in that table.
#
# Below four workgroups the device is genuinely starved -- the 32-to-64 and
# 64-to-128 steps give 0.42 and 0.87 Tflop/s -- but no shape a layer asks
# for is that small. The batch sweep that found 455 rising to 1103 as the
# workgroups went 8 to 64 was reading the fixed cost thinning, not
# starvation lifting.
#
# So the entry above is corrected: the narrow vector tile was measured and
# rejected for a problem that was not there, and the twenty-six per cent it
# bought on that shape was the fixed cost being divided among four times as
# many dispatches rather than a starvation being relieved.
#
# The fixed cost is not only the instrument's. The floor entry measures the
# whole surround of a 1419-token device prompt at 0.502 s, and that prompt
# makes about seventeen hundred product calls: 0.29 ms apiece is 0.50 s. The
# two measurements meet, which is the first time two of today's have.
#
# Whether a bigger batch would divide it was checked rather than assumed.
# --batch-size at 64, 128, 256, 512 and 1024 reads 3.138, 2.490, 2.459,
# 2.426 and 2.547 s on the long prompt, every one printing
# 1a26d24d33b8957b. The default is within three per cent of the best and the
# curve is flat between 128 and 512. It cannot help: the floor is host loops
# over positions, and a batch does not divide positions.
#
# What this leaves. The tile is at 3.4 Tflop/s at the margin, the surround
# is 0.5 s of 2.53, and llama.cpp would read the same prompt in about 0.86.
# Neither the shape table nor the batch size nor the submissions are where
# the remaining gap is. It is in what the tile does per flop, and this file
# has no instrument that separates that from the memory it reads.
#
# The row sweep is kept in tests/src/device_bench.adb, which no figure group
# names. No other source changed.

# 2026-08-27, what surrounds the products is a fifth, not a half. The phase
# counters said otherwise and the phase counters were wrong.
#
# The item said doubling put feeding's arithmetic at forty-three per cent of
# its phase, so the other fifty-seven -- three quarters of a second -- was
# submissions and fences, and that closing it was the next thing. That
# reading is wrong, and the way it is wrong is worth more than the item was.
#
# The measurement that settles it. Every Dispatch in the engine asks for
# zero workgroups: every command buffer is recorded, every descriptor set
# updated, every submission made and waited on, every activation uploaded
# and every kept answer copied back, and no arithmetic is done on the device
# at all. What is left is the floor -- everything a device prompt costs
# except the device.
#
#   1419-token device prompt   2.530 s   floor 0.502 s   80 per cent arithmetic
#   110-token device prompt    0.208 s   floor 0.045 s   78 per cent arithmetic
#
# Four rounds of the long pair and one of the short, and the two lengths
# agree to two points.
#
# So the surround is a fifth of a device prompt and most of that fifth is
# the host loops that remain: normalizing, joining and rotating are 0.526 s
# by the phase counters, which is the floor itself. Submissions, fences and
# transfers are what is left over from that, and it is not much.
#
# Why the phase counters said otherwise, which is the part to keep. A phase
# is host wall time around an asynchronous submission, so the "non-
# arithmetic" part of the feeding phase is the host **waiting for the device
# to finish the arithmetic**. It is not overhead; it is the work, seen from
# the side that is not doing it. Emptying feeding's dispatch takes the phase
# from 0.891 s to 0.351; doubling it adds only 0.405 rather than 0.540,
# because two dispatches in one command buffer pipeline better than one
# does. Neither number is the arithmetic and the difference between them is
# not overhead either.
#
# That is the third time today a phase counter has led somewhere false --
# feeding gaining time a change did not touch, projecting not moving under a
# doubling, and now this. They are useful for saying which part of a layer
# grew and useless for saying what a part is made of. What says that is a
# whole-run measurement with one thing removed.
#
# What it means for the gap. llama.cpp reads 110 tokens at 1657.8 a second,
# so this prompt would be about 0.86 s there against 2.53 here. Take the
# entire surround away -- every submission, every transfer, every host loop,
# all of it free -- and this is 2.03 s, still two and a half times behind.
# **The remaining gap is the arithmetic**, and the batched product profiled
# above at 455 to 3200 gigaflops a second against a part whose matrix
# instruction should reach far more is where it lives.
#
# No source changed. The dispatch counts were zeroed in a scratch build and
# put back.

# Re-measured for the 2026-08-28 entry below -- the bias correction and
#   the eight-lane fold moved into the insertion.
# load: nothing else running, on a part that had stopped cooling -- it
#   settled at 51 C before the first run.
engine 0954A33ED72CDE5B src/library/model_runner-llama.adb src/library/model_runner-llama.ads src/library/model_runner-kernels.adb src/library/model_runner-kernels.ads src/library/model_runner-backend-cpu.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization.adb src/library/model_runner-numerics.adb src/library/model_runner-tensors.adb tests/src/speed_run.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-decoders.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-wide.ads model_runner.gpr src/library/model_runner-byte_sources-files.adb src/library/model_runner-generation.ads src/library/model_runner-sampling.ads src/library/model_runner-sampling.adb src/library/model_runner-cli-options.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-kernels.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-kernels.adb src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-plain.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-wide.ads src/library/model_runner-quantization-integers-deep.ads src/library/model_runner-platform-instructions.ads src/platform/linux/model_runner-platform-instructions.adb

# 2026-08-16, the byte cache. The four groups carrying llama.adb moved with

# packs a row on the way in and unpacks an element on the way out, both in
# the attention loop, and a session that does not ask for it tests one
# enumeration value more per position than it did. The published figures are
# taken at the default precision, which allocates and touches none of it.
# Not re-measured, and this machine still has no model file to measure with,
# which is the reason recorded under the engine group above.

# 2026-08-20, attention on the device from both evaluators. The four groups
# carry llama.adb, which gained two subprograms and three call sites: a
# position's keys and values are written to a cache a device holds, and a
# position attends to that cache there rather than here. This was already
# said while generating; saying it while a batch is evaluated is what this
# change adds, and the reason it had to be added is that a drafted run
# checks its proposals through the batched evaluator while it generates
# through the other, so a device wired into one and not the other made a
# drafted run disagree with an undrafted one.
#
# The device group is re-measured; this group is not, and the reason is
# that the code cannot run here. Put_Position returns on its first
# statement unless the session's backend is the device one, and
# Attend_There is reached only when Put_Position has come back saying the
# device took the position, which off the device it never does. What these
# figures pay for the change is one enumeration comparison per position per
# layer, on a path that reads a matrix per position per layer.
#

# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat v1.0 Q8_0, as TheBloke published it -- tinyllama-1.1b-chat-v1.0.Q8_0.gguf, 1170781568 bytes.
# load: 1.49 to 1.49 for the cpu run and 1.49 to 1.30 for the reference one, taken back to back, 2026-08-24
# 2026-08-25. Four tokens from the short prompt, medians of three, back to
# back at a load of 0.49 to 0.49 and 0.49 to 0.74: `cpu` spends 0.065 s on
# the prompt and 0.131 s generating, `reference` 5.819 s and 3.867 s. Forty-
# nine times in total, ninety on the prompt, thirty generating, same digest
# on both -- 5f85f06494f88de4. At --arith f32 the same comparison is
# fourteen times.

# The ratio grew again because only one side moved, which is the whole point
# of the figure: `reference` computes what it always did. `tests benchmark`
# on synthetic tensors, serial against serial, reads 2.3x for q8_0, 2.3x for
# q4_k and 3.1x for f32; the rest of the fourteen is the pool and the batch.

# 2026-08-25. Four tokens from the short prompt, medians of three, back to
# back at a load of 1.05: `cpu` spends 0.074 s on the prompt and 0.134 s
# generating, `reference` 5.805 s and 3.870 s. Forty-six times in total,
# seventy-eight on the prompt, twenty-nine generating, same digest on both --

# 2026-08-26. Four tokens from the short prompt, medians of three, back to
# back at a load of 1.19: `cpu` spends 0.069 s on the prompt and 0.124 s

# exact either way and the rounding falls in the same places, which is not
# true of the eight-bit format: there the byte instruction groups a block's
# products differently and the README says so. Here it does not, because a

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the four-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel of its own. As with the batch kernel before it, every figure
# in this file is Q8_0 and the Q8_0 path did not change: 0.738 s for the
# 110-token prompt against 0.806 last sitting, 2.160 s for sixty-four

# Q4_K, which is the change. Medians of three alternated rounds:

# eighty-four, where the eight-bit format takes thirty-three.
#
# Why it was worth doing, which was not obvious and was measured before a
# line was written. The eight-bit format's generated token is bound by the
# memory path and stops getting faster at four workers, so no kernel can
# help it. This one was still getting faster at seven -- 1.510 s at two
# shares, 1.130 at four, 0.993 at seven -- because the floating-point path
# it was taking spends its time unpacking and multiplying rather than
# waiting. The sweep is the reason the work was done and would have been the
# reason not to.

# The same sitting, unalternated: 1.468 s for the prompt and 0.505 s
# generating, at a load rising through 1.7.
#
# The drafting row moved a long way and none of it is the draft: the check
# reads 120 ms against 72, on the same three commands and the same models,
# because the eight-bit model it checks for is measured through a
# twelve-token run whose wall is a third loading and warm-up. The conclusion
# is the one it has always been and is now unconditional -- at this
# acceptance a round yields less than the check costs, so no draft price
# pays.

# 2026-08-26, taken again after the six-bit k-quant's generated token was
# given a kernel -- the last floating-point path in a "_M" file. Every

# generates at the same rate.

# One of the two single-vector kernels did not work at first, and the reason
# is worth keeping. Every insertion written before them advances the
# pointers it was handed -- an addq on a register declared an input -- which
# tells the compiler nothing and works only while it has no other use for
# that register. With eleven operands it stopped working: the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker, and *adding an exception handler and nothing else
# made it run*, which is the signature of code generation rather than
# arithmetic. Both single-vector kernels now keep their cursors in registers
# of their own, named in the clobber list, and advance no operand. The
# four-bit one had the same latent fault and had already passed a gate.
#
# The drafting threshold has now read 17, 10, 6 and 9 milliseconds a token
# across four sittings of unchanged code. It is a difference of two
# twelve-token runs and a twelve-token run is a third loading and warm-up;
# the README says so and says to read the conclusion rather than the number.

# 2026-08-26, and the figures above were NOT re-taken. This entry is the
# other thing this file allows -- saying why a change cannot have moved
# them -- and it is the first time that has been used rather than a sitting.
#
# What changed: every machine code insertion in the integer kernels stopped
# advancing the pointers it was handed. They did it with an addq on a
# register declared an input, which tells the compiler nothing and works
# only while it has no other use for that register; the six-bit kernel
# raised inside a worker when it stopped working, and an exception handler
# and nothing else made it run again. Four insertions still did it, all of
# them working and all of them having passed gates, including Rows_Singly,
# which every generated eight-bit token runs and which advanced four
# operands -- more than the one that failed. All six now keep cursors in
# registers of their own and copy the counter rather than decrementing it in
# place.
#
# Why it cannot have moved the figures, and the evidence rather than the
# argument. Three alternated rounds on both models, in one sitting:
#
#                                     before      after
#   eight-bit, sixty-four generated   2.085 s     2.086 s

# and every digest identical -- 448c2ed68ec342ee, cbf29ce484222325,

# be: alternated rounds in one sitting resolve four per cent, and the spread
# between sittings on this machine is larger than that. Re-measuring
# everything would have replaced numbers known not to have moved with

# 2026-08-18, gpt2's feed-forward normalization. These groups carry
# llama.adb and moved with it. Re-measured rather than argued about, on this
# machine, with TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 and a two-token prompt:
#
#   seven tokens, device      0.570 s wall, 0.468 s generating
#   seven tokens, processor   1.063 s wall, 0.895 s generating
#   twelve tokens, drafted    5.800 s wall, with the Q2_K of the same model
#                             proposing four at a time
#
# Both backends printed 5709b12b0517c8c5 on the seven-token run, which is the
# claim the two-row table rests on and the one worth re-checking after a
# change to the normalization every layer of every token goes through.

# 2026-08-18, the device sequence. products.adb gained a recording half --
# Open_Sequence, Add_Product, Run -- and the body that performs one product
# became One_Product, which both the single call and Run reach. Nothing about
# what reaches the device changed, and the seven-token run says so: the same
# digest 5709b12b0517c8c5, at 0.696 s wall against the 0.570 s taken earlier
# the same day on a quieter machine. Re-measured because the file is named by
# this group, not because anything in the loop moved.

# 2026-08-18, a descriptor set per sequence step. The pool the engine makes
# now holds thirty-three sets rather than one, and the extra thirty-two are
# allocated with it. Nothing dispatches differently and nothing new is
# recorded: what changes is that a command buffer could hold more than one
# product, which one set made impossible. The seven-token run still prints
# 5709b12b0517c8c5, which is the check that a driver accepted the larger pool
# -- an engine that failed to open would refuse the run rather than answer.

# 2026-08-18, the hoist. A sequence now records every product into one
# command buffer, binds a set per product, and waits once: acquiring the
# matrices and updating the descriptors happen before anything is recorded,
# because neither may happen between two dispatches already written down.
# The products of a sequence read the same activation and write disjoint
# parts of the result, so nothing between them waits and no barrier is
# needed -- what a second product costs is a dispatch rather than a
# submission and a fence.
#
# No published figure moves, and that is not an oversight. Nothing in the
# engine records a sequence yet: every product still arrives through the
# single call, which is why the seven-token run prints 5709b12b0517c8c5 as
# before. The saving is real and unclaimed until a layer asks for it.

# The other three groups carry llama.adb and moved with it. What changed for
# them is the shape of one call site, not the arithmetic: the processor and
# the reference still do three products one after another, through the same
# subprogram they did before. Re-measured anyway, on this machine, at a load
# under 1.50:
#
#   twelve tokens, processor   1.679 s wall, 1.491 s generating
#   twelve tokens, drafted     5.738 s wall
#
# Against 1.682 s and 5.800 s taken earlier the same day, and unchanged again
# when the feed-forward joined the grouping: the processor reads the group one
# matrix at a time, which is what it did when they were separate calls. The reference group
# is not separately re-timed: a reference run of a 1.1B model is minutes, its
# arithmetic is untouched here, and the conformance sweep compares it against
# the engine twenty-six thousand times a gate.

# 2026-08-19, the figures as they stand after the withdrawal above, taken on
# the tree that carries the combining kernel. Recorded as of their moment,
# which is the only way this file will quote a wall time from now on:
#
#   seven tokens, device      0.559 s wall, 0.461 s generating
#   seven tokens, processor   1.007 s wall
#   twelve tokens, processor  1.641 s wall, 1.476 s generating
#   twelve tokens, drafted    5.415 s wall
#
# Both backends printed 5709b12b0517c8c5 on the seven-token run, which is the
# claim the two-row table rests on and the one that has held through every
# change of the last two days. The ratio between the backends is what these
# lines are for; the absolute times are not comparable with yesterday's and
# are not offered as though they were.

# The four groups, re-taken on the tree that carries all of this, each crate
# verified to have built:
#
#   seven tokens, device      0.580 s wall, 0.467 s generating
#   seven tokens, processor   1.012 s wall
#   twelve tokens, processor  1.583 s wall, 1.409 s generating
#   twelve tokens, drafted    5.820 s wall
#
# Both backends printed 5709b12b0517c8c5 again. As of their moment, as every
# wall time in this file now is.

# 2026-08-20, the corruption behind every end-to-end figure taken today.
#
# Close released the vector and result buffers and left the cache alone:
# still allocated, still mapped, with Cache_At pointing into memory the
# device was about to take with it. Cache_Buffer appeared in four places in
# that file and none of them was Close.
#
# Nothing showed while nothing called Reserve, which is why it came through a
# gate. The moment the engine wired attention up, a suite that opens and
# closes a device once a test went from passing to "malloc(): unaligned
# tcache chunk detected" -- and, in another run, to a glibc thread-priority
# assertion, which is the same stale pointer surfacing wherever the allocator
# next happened to look. Two symptoms, one fault.
#
# What it costs is not the crash. Every end-to-end measurement of device
# attention taken today ran on a binary with it: the ones that said four
# times slower, the ones that said eighteen times slower, and the one that
# said eleven per cent faster. None of them mean anything. The isolated
# figures from `tests device-bench` are untouched -- that tool opens one
# engine and never closes it, which is exactly why it never saw this.
#
# The two-row device figure is not retaken: nothing the engine runs has
# moved, since nothing in the engine reserves a cache. The sources the group
# names have changed, which is the case this file's rules allow a written
# reason for.
#
# The benchmark not reproducing it was not luck. A tool that measures one
# call in one engine cannot see a fault in the second open, and the engine
# does nothing else all day.

# 2026-08-20, the call is not what is slow. Timed where it is made, inside a
# layer, an attention reads 0.537 ms -- the same as the benchmark says for it
# alone, and not the 2.55 ms that was inferred earlier by dividing an
# end-to-end difference by a call count. Reserving the cache costs 0.24 us a
# call after the one allocation of 88 MiB, and writing a position costs
# 1.18 us. Ninety-five milliseconds a run, all told, for the wiring.
#
# So the earlier inference was wrong, and it was wrong because an eight-token
# window is shorter than this machine's own variation. Lengthened to
# sixty-four it says something else entirely:
#
#   generating, unwired    0.282 s        4.4 ms a token
#   generating, wired      5.224 s       81.6 ms a token
#
# Fourteen hundred attention calls account for 0.76 s of that 5.2. And the
# cost per token grew from 4.4 to 81.6 while the calls per token did not:
# whatever this is, it is not a per-call cost.
#
# Five explanations have now been offered and measured away:
#
#   the machine was busy    no: interleaved, and the unwired prompt path
#                           read the same either side
#   the shape was wrong     no: the engine's own shape reads 0.35 ms
#   the cache went cold     no: walking every layer's region, 0.508 ms
#   the queue was busy      not settled by differencing -- four products
#                           cost thirty milliseconds and the attention one,
#                           and three tries gave -4.02, +0.76 and +1.09 ms
#   the cache took the      no: cycling twenty matrices with a cache
#   weights' room           reserved gives back none of them, and kept forty
#
# The last was worth asking because the weight budget is a share of the heap
# fixed when the device opens and knows nothing about a cache reserved after.
# It gives back nothing all the same.
#
# What is left is unknown, and the honest thing is to leave it that way rather
# than offer a sixth. The rows are in `tests device-bench`.

# 2026-08-20, hunting the difference between what an attention call costs on
# its own and what it costs a layer. Wired into the engine it read 0.709 s of
# generating against 0.261 s with the processor doing the attention -- taken
# interleaved in one session, with the prompt evaluation reading 53.99 s and
# 54.29 s either side of it, which is what says the machine was the same. It
# is 0.448 s across 176 calls: 2.55 ms apiece, against 0.35 to 0.54 ms for the
# same call measured on its own at the engine's own shape.
#
# Three explanations were offered for that and the benchmark refused two of
# them outright:
#
#   the machine was busy      no: the unwired prompt path read the same
#                             either side, and the pair was interleaved
#   the shape was wrong       no: measured at thirty-two heads sharing four
#                             groups, across ninety megabytes of cache,
#                             0.35 ms
#   the cache went cold       no: walking a different layer's region every
#                             call reads 0.508 ms against 0.537 for the same
#                             one twenty times over
#
# What is left is the fourth, and it is not settled. Beside one matrix product
# an attention's marginal cost read 0.703 ms in one run and 1.810 ms in the
# next -- above its cost alone, and varying by more than a factor of two. A
# layer submits four products around each attention, and 2.55 ms is inside
# what that suggests if the effect scales. It was called refuted here on the
# first of those two numbers, which was one reading and the smaller one.
#
# So: the call is cheap and the calling is not, and how much of the calling
# is the fence, the pipeline change, or the driver is not known. The rows are
# in `tests device-bench` for whoever asks next.

# 2026-08-20, the stage bounds held against the machine's time and not the
# wall's. What a stage costs the processor barely moves when something else
# is running; what it costs the clock moves by more than the growth a bound
# exists to catch. Conformance read 1741.85 s of wall in one run and 962 in
# the one before it, for the same twenty-six thousand sequences -- and 1757.39
# s of processor time, which is the figure that describes the stage.
#
# The five, measured on this host:
#
#   suite               3.94, 6.28 s              bound 20
#   repository checks   9.00, 11.81, 13.03, 19.39  bound 60
#   conformance         1757.39, 1945.01 s         bound 4000
#   fixtures            199.92, 211.37 s           bound 450
#   fuzzing             0.28, 0.30 s               bound 5
#   the whole gate      about 2180 s               bound 4800
#
# Twice the worst for the long stages and three times it for the short two.
# The short ones move: the repository checks read 9.00 and then 19.39, better
# than two to one, because a stage that reads five thousand files gets what
# the page cache and the scheduler give it that minute. The long ones are
# arithmetic and hold still -- conformance moved by a ninth across two runs
# whose wall times differed by a factor of two.
#
# This was set from a single reading each on the first pass and had to be
# widened twice. A bound taken from one reading is a bound taken from the
# best of them, and that is the third time this file has recorded the same
# lesson in two days.
#
# Where a host keeps no such number the bound is not held at all. It is said
# and not counted, rather than quietly reverting to the wall clock -- which is
# the measurement this change exists to stop relying on. Processor_Seconds
# reads /proc/self/stat, so that is every host but this family of them.
#
# Asking the system instead would answer everywhere: getrusage is POSIX and
# gives the same figure. It was written that way and taken out again, because
# a check in this repository refuses a host call bound by name in the tests
# crate -- one directory for every host -- and the check was right. It belongs
# in the platform layer with a body apiece, which is its own change and not
# this one.

# 2026-08-20, a bound that could not tell a busy machine from a stage that
# grew. Its own message said so -- "either the machine was busy or the stage
# grew" -- and it failed the gate either way. Conformance read 648, 992, 1505
# and 1794 s in one day against a bound of 1250, and the difference was the
# machine every time: this host was at a load of 3.6 for the last of them and
# none of it was this program's.
#
# The bound was not wrong. Twice the 648 s taken on a quiet machine is 1250,
# which is the rule written beside it. What was wrong is that a figure taken
# at a load of 3.6 is not a figure about the stage, and this repository
# already says so about every other number it prints: Host_Load.Publishable,
# whose own comment says the rule is about figures and not about any one
# measurement. A stage's time is a figure.
#
# So an overrun above that load is reported and not counted, and an overrun
# below it fails as before. Checked both ways with the bound set to a
# thousandth of a second: at a load of 3.06 it noted, and with the load
# forced to 1.0 it failed.
#
# A bound that fails half the time is a bound people learn to ignore, and
# that is how a suite of twenty-eight minutes went unnoticed for months.
#
# The rule alone was not enough, and the run that proved it is worth keeping.
# With the mechanism in, the gate's total was noted at a load of 1.57 and
# conformance failed at 1.40 -- correctly, by the rule, since 1.40 is quiet
# enough to publish from. It took 1479 s there against a bound of 1250 that
# came from 648 s the day before, also at a publishable load.
#
# So the quiet baseline itself moves by 2.3 times on this host, and a bound
# meant to catch a stage that doubles cannot be set tighter than the noise it
# is measuring against. 3000 s is twice the worst reading taken at a load the
# rule calls quiet, which is the most a wall clock can say here.
#
# The better instrument is processor time rather than wall time: what a stage
# costs the machine barely moves when something else is running, and what it
# costs the clock moves by more than the thing being watched for. That is a
# change to what Report_Stage measures rather than to any bound, and it is
# not made here.

# 2026-08-20, the attention kernel measured instead of guessed at. Two shapes
# of it had been written and each lost to the processor -- one by twelve
# times, the next by four -- and both were written before anything measured
# where their time went. `tests device-bench` asks that instead: one call
# timed at several shapes, with the arithmetic done put beside the seconds
# taken.
#
# What it said about the kernel as committed, one invocation a head:
#
#   1 head,   512 positions   6.204 ms     32 heads, 16 positions   0.582 ms
#   32 heads, 512 positions   9.980 ms     peak                     0.52 Gflop/s
#
# Rows two and three do identical arithmetic -- 131 thousand operations -- and
# differ fifteenfold. Thirty-two times the heads cost one and a half times;
# four times the positions cost three and a quarter. Positions were the serial
# axis, which is what one invocation a head means and what no amount of
# rewriting in the same shape would have fixed.
#
# Three changes, each from a number rather than a hunch:
#
#   a workgroup a head, positions dealt round its invocations
#   the cache left on the device instead of re-sent for every call
#   the cache mapped once instead of mapped and unmapped per write
#
#   attention, 32 heads 512 positions   9.98 ms  ->  0.395 ms
#   peak                                0.52     ->  13.0 Gflop/s
#   a cache write of 2048 values        1.6 ms   ->  0.26 us
#
# The processor does the same attention in 0.85 ms, so the kernel is now
# twice its speed where it was four times slower. The standing mapping is
# safe rather than lucky: the memory kind a device is chosen for must be
# host-coherent, which is required where that choice is made, so a write
# through it is seen without a flush. An earlier attempt at this was reverted
# for fear of exactly that, and the fear was unfounded.
#
# The device group's own two-row figure is not retaken: nothing the engine
# runs has moved, since nothing in the engine calls any of this. The sources
# the group names have changed, which is the case this file's rules allow a
# written reason for.
#
# It is not wired to the engine and does not pay yet. Generation at 522
# cached positions reads 0.654 s against the processor's 0.150 s, and 176
# attention calls account for seventy milliseconds of that. The rest is
# unexplained, so nothing is claimed for it. What there is now is an
# instrument that answers such a question in seconds rather than in
# forty-five-minute gate runs, which is what made the first two shapes cost
# what they did.

# 2026-08-19, and the answer to it: the suite takes eight seconds.
#
# The routine that was sixteen of its twenty-eight minutes called
# Conformance.Run -- the same sweep the gate runs as a stage of its own and
# `tests conformance` runs alone. So the gate compared the engine against the
# reference twice, 948 s inside the suite and 650 s again beside it, and the
# suite was the stage the gate did not time.
#
# Taken out of the suite:
#
#   before   1677 s, 259 tests
#   after        8 s, 258 tests
#
# Ninety-nine and a half per cent of a stage, and it was a duplicate. The
# gate loses nothing: the sweep still runs, once, where it was already
# running. What `tests test` alone loses is the comparison, and that is the
# right place to lose it -- a suite is what runs inside somebody's attention,
# and a sixteen-minute comparison belongs in the gate that already has one.
#
# The suite's bound comes down from 2900 s to 60, which says loudly if
# anything of that size is put back. And the README, which said a second and
# a half and was corrected yesterday to twenty-four minutes, now says eight
# seconds -- the first figure had been right before that test was added, and
# the correction was made to a number that was about to stop being true.

# 2026-08-19, where the suite's twenty-eight minutes go: one test.
#
# `tests slow` runs each of the nine cases on its own and times it, and will
# time one test named by the prefix AUnit's filter understands. What it says:
#
#   inference    1219.8 s        cli          3.2 s
#   template     1.0 s           gguf         0.4 s
#   backend, catalog, sampling, grammar, accounting, under a quarter-second
#
# Ninety-nine and a half per cent of the suite is one case, and of that case
# 948 s -- sixteen minutes -- is one routine: "logits match an independent
# reference implementation". Forty-eight of its forty-nine routines together
# come to under a second.
#
# That routine compares the engine against Reference_Transformer, which is
# what the conformance stage does for 650 s of its own. So the gate has been
# running the engine against the reference twice, once in the suite and once
# in the sweep, and nothing said so, because the suite stage was the one the
# gate did not time.
#
# The other thing this says is what the device tests cost, which two of
# yesterday's theories turned on: the cli case, holding all five of them and
# every device open they make, is 3.2 s. Neither device opens nor pipelines
# built eagerly were ever capable of the minutes they were blamed for, and
# this measurement would have said so in twenty minutes at any point.
#
# Getting it took three attempts and the failures are worth recording. AUnit
# times every routine and hands the numbers to the reporter, but this build
# selects its no-calendar variant, where the times are all zero -- a reporter
# that ranks them prints a column of noughts. perf writes an empty file here
# and gdb returns no backtrace. And driving AUnit twice over one suite object
# in a single process frees something twice, so the loop over routines has to
# be outside the program, a process each.

# 2026-08-19, what the suite costs, and why nobody knew. It reads 1443 s on
# this host with all 259 tests passing, and the README said a second and a
# half. That figure was true once and had not been re-measured; nothing
# checked it, because the gate ran the suite as its first stage and timed the
# four after it.
#
# The cost of not knowing was a day. AUnit prints nothing until it has
# finished, so a suite that outruns its timeout is indistinguishable from one
# that has hung -- and every timeout used against it was set from the
# published figure. Four explanations were offered for that silence in one
# afternoon: a barrier deadlock, a memory coherency fault, a wedged device,
# and pipelines built too eagerly. All four were wrong, and the last was
# tested by building the pipelines lazily, which made the suite slower and
# broke two tests.
#
# Nor was any of it new. Three days earlier the same suite ran past 2400 s
# without finishing, so it has cost tens of minutes since well before the
# work that was being blamed for it:
#
#   18871a3, three days back   over 2400 s, unfinished
#   a60e4e6, this morning      1443 s, 259 passing
#
# The gate now says `took: suite` beside the other four stages, with a bound
# of 2900 s on the same principle as theirs -- roughly twice what was
# measured, because a machine under load takes half again as long and that is
# not news. One gate run would have said all of this in one line.

# 2026-08-19, an attention kernel, checked and not yet wired.
#
# attention.comp does one position attending to everything a cache holds: the
# scores, the bound where an architecture states one, the softmax across them,
# and the values weighted by what comes out. It is the piece of a layer that
# stands between two matrix products and keeps them from being sent together,
# so it is what the rest of the resident chain waits on.
#
# Keys and values share a buffer, because attention wants four arrays and the
# pipeline layout carries three. Widening the layout would have cost every
# shader; laying the cache out as one region costs nothing, since this program
# writes the cache in the first place.
#
# One invocation a head, folding the softmax into the pass that computes the
# scores -- a running maximum, a running sum, a running weighted value -- so
# nothing is stored per position. The host does not do it that way because it
# has the scores in an array anyway; a device wants it because storing a score
# a position is storing a context length of them.
#
# Checked against the same arithmetic in binary64: worst disagreement under
# 1.0E-5 across four heads and sixteen positions. Checked by breaking it too
# -- swapping where the keys and the values begin disagrees by 1.6E-2.
#
# Not wired, and it cannot pay yet: Attend uploads the whole cache on every
# call, which is the round trip this exists to remove. What makes it worth
# having is the cache staying on the device between tokens, and that is a
# change to where the cache lives rather than to this kernel. The device
# figure is unchanged within the day's spread -- 0.596 s wall, 0.488 s
# generating, digest 5709b12b0517c8c5 -- because nothing in the engine calls
# it.

# 2026-08-19, the split explained, and a claim about it corrected.
#
# The experiment that seemed to find the speedup in neither half was recorded
# above as void because one half supposedly could not compile. That was wrong
# twice over: it compiles, and the split needs no explaining away at all.
#
#   cf13815 declares Open_Sequence and carries Run already -- a sequence
#   existed there; what it did not do was record several products into one
#   command buffer. Its Run calls One_Product a step, so a sequence of three
#   was three submissions.
#
#   cf13815's llama has no Product_Group, so nothing ever asked for a
#   sequence.
#
# So half A -- old llama, new products -- has the hoisted Run and no caller
# that groups: every product goes as its own Multiply. Slow, and correctly so.
# Half B -- new llama, old products -- asks for groups and reaches a Run that
# submits per product. Also slow, also correctly so. The whole is fast because
# the saving needs the caller and the callee together, which is what the
# change is rather than a puzzle about it.
#
# The lesson here is narrower than the two above and still worth keeping: a
# result that looks impossible was reached by asserting a compile failure
# instead of checking for one. `git show REV:path | grep` answers that in a
# second. Two of the three surprises of this day came from believing something
# about a build without asking the build.

# 2026-08-19, the gated block in the batched path too, and what it is worth:
# very little, for a reason worth writing down.
#
# Evaluate_Batch reads the prompt; Evaluate reads a token at a time while
# generating. Both now hand a device the whole gated block. The combining step
# was made to work over a batch -- it is elementwise, and both arms are laid
# out the same way by the same kernel, so what that layout is does not matter
# to it -- and the guard that restricted it to one position came out.
#
# On the prompt of the published figure, two tokens, the difference is inside
# the noise: 0.106 s against 0.093 s by median, with the two spreads almost
# entirely overlapping. On a 522-token prompt, where there is something to
# measure:
#
#   before  22.252  22.723  21.523
#   after   21.559  21.534  21.763
#
# Median 22.25 against 21.56, about three per cent, and the spreads overlap
# there too. Both were built with each crate verified.
#
# Three per cent where the token-at-a-time path gave twenty-three, and the
# reason is structural rather than disappointing: a batch of five hundred
# positions already amortizes a submission across five hundred positions'
# worth of arithmetic. Removing one submission a layer matters when a layer's
# work is one position wide and hardly at all when it is five hundred. The
# grouping was always going to pay where it paid, and this says where that is.
#
# It is kept because it is correct, tested, and takes real host arithmetic --
# an activation and a multiply over half a million values a layer -- off the
# processor. It is not kept for the three per cent, and the figures file
# should not later be read as claiming it was.

# 2026-08-19, later: the withdrawal below was itself wrong, and the reason is
# worth more than either claim.
#
# The probes that said Llama.Evaluate never runs were run against a stale
# binary. `tests/bin/tests` links the library statically, so rebuilding the
# library alone leaves the test tool carrying the old code. Several probes
# rebuilt only the library. They showed no change because nothing had
# changed -- not because the path was dead.
#
# Rebuilt properly, every probe reverses. Cutting Evaluate short fails the
# run. A deliberately wrong activation unit moves the digest from
# 5709b12b0517c8c5 to 8920270451e368f6. The path is live, the gated block does
# reach the device, and the grouping was running all along.
#
# The split that "found the difference in neither half" was explained later
# and not this way; see the entry above dated the same day. It compiles, and
# both halves are slow for good reasons. This paragraph was wrong and is kept
# only so the correction has something to point at.
#
# The figure, re-taken with both crates verified to have built, interleaved:
#
#   cf13815 (before grouping)  0.594  0.576  0.598
#   bef8293 (after)            0.451  0.461  0.456
#
# Median 0.594 against 0.456: twenty-three per cent, which is what the
# interleaved run said yesterday before any of this. The speedups stand.
#
# What does not stand is the method. A probe that leaves behaviour unchanged
# proves nothing until the binary under test is known to have been rebuilt,
# and "seven tokens generated" is not a check that a run succeeded. Both
# mistakes were made here in one day, and between them they produced a
# confident retraction of a true result. The rule that follows: build every
# crate that links what changed, verify the build said so, and prefer a probe
# that fails loudly to one that merely differs.

# 2026-08-19, a withdrawal -- WHICH IS ITSELF WITHDRAWN; see above. Kept
# because a wrong conclusion and how it was reached is worth as much as the
# right one. The device speedups this file reported yesterday
# -- eighteen per cent for grouping the queries, keys and values, three more
# for the gated arms, corrected to twenty-three by an interleaved run -- are
# not supported, and the attribution was wrong. What follows is how that was
# found, because the way it was found is the only part worth keeping.
#
# The wiring went into Llama.Evaluate, which takes one token. Generation does
# not call it. `Model_Runner.Generation` reads through Evaluate_Batch, even
# for a single token; Evaluate is reached by the draft model of a speculative
# run and by two other paths, and by nothing the seven-token figure exercises.
# Three probes say so and agree: an `exit` after the first normalization, a
# `return` in the same place, and a deliberately wrong activation unit. None
# of them moved the digest. Code that cannot change the answer when it is
# broken is code that did not run.
#
# So the grouping could not have caused the figure it was credited with. The
# figure itself reproduces -- cf13815 reads 0.59 s and 3c424a2 reads 0.46 s,
# interleaved, twice, on two occasions -- so something between those commits
# is worth twenty per cent and it is not what was claimed. Splitting the
# change in half finds neither half: cf13815 with the new products layer
# reads 0.594, and cf13815 with everything except the products layer reads
# 0.602, while the whole reads 0.50. Both builds were checked to have
# compiled, which the first attempt at this had not been.
#
# A difference that appears only when two halves are combined, neither of
# which contains a live code path, is not a difference anybody has explained.
# It may be code layout; it may be that these readings are worth less than
# they look. Either way the honest state is: the cause is unknown, and no
# device speedup is claimed here until one measurement isolates one change.
#
# What stands: the digest never moved through any of it, so nothing above
# computed anything different. And the machine drift recorded yesterday --
# a quarter, morning to evening, at the same load average -- is the same size
# as everything being argued about, which is the deeper reason this went
# wrong.

# 2026-08-18, how the device figures above were taken, and why the next ones
# are taken differently. The three lines under "the feed-forward joins in"
# were measured one after another over a working session -- apart, then with
# the queries, keys and values grouped, then with the gate too -- each at a
# load under 1.50. That is not good enough, and the way it showed was a
# regression that was not there: a change measured 0.46 s against a published
# 0.385 s, and the commit before it measured 0.46 s as well. The machine had
# moved, not the code.
#
# Taken again by building the two revisions alternately and running each three
# times, twice around:
#
#   ungrouped (cf13815)  0.559  0.594  0.590   and  0.605  0.602  0.604
#   grouped   (3c424a2)  0.472  0.471  0.459   and  0.463  0.449  0.459
#
# Median 0.598 s against 0.461 s: twenty-three per cent, where the sequential
# measurement said eighteen. The effect is real and was understated. What was
# also true, and unsaid, is that the same ungrouped build reads 0.468 s in the
# morning and 0.60 s in the evening at the same load average -- a quarter,
# which is larger than either effect this file has been reporting.
#
# So the absolute wall times in the two-row table are of their moment and the
# ratios between backends are what survive. A comparison between two revisions
# belongs in an interleaved run and nowhere else; a load average under 1.50 is
# not evidence that two figures taken hours apart may be subtracted.

# 2026-08-18, what device-side normalization would be worth: nothing this
# machine can measure. Written down because the question looks obviously
# worth a yes, and the answer is no for a reason that only a measurement
# gives.
#
# The instrument was to run the attention normalization eight times a layer
# instead of once, on every layer of every token, and see what it cost:
#
#   one normalization a layer      0.385 s generating
#   eight normalizations a layer   0.385 s and 0.388 s generating
#
# Seven extra normalizations of every layer of every token cost nothing that
# shows. So the most that moving them to a device could win is the host time
# they take, and that time is below the floor. TinyLlama is 2048 wide and
# twenty-two deep, so a token's normalizations are about 270 thousand
# operations against 55 milliseconds of generating -- half a per cent would
# be a generous ceiling and the instrument says it is smaller.
#
# There is no round trip to remove either, which is the part that is easy to
# get wrong from an armchair. The normalized vector is uploaded once today; a
# device-side normalization would upload the activation once instead. Same
# bytes, same submission. A normalization can only join a submission already
# happening if its output feeds the next step without coming back, and that
# is chaining machinery built for a gain measured at zero.
#
# Where the time actually is, from the figures above: removing three
# submissions a layer bought 0.083 s over seven tokens and twenty-two layers,
# which is about 0.18 ms a submission. A layer still costs four -- the
# queries, keys and values; the attention output; the gate and up; the down
# projection -- and the last three cannot be grouped because each waits for
# the one before. Collapsing those needs the dependent chain to stay on the
# device: the rotation, the attention, the softmax and the residual adds, not
# the normalizations by themselves. That is the shape of the next gain, and
# normalizations are a small component of it rather than a step toward it.

# 2026-08-18, the feed-forward joins in. The gate and the up projection of a
# gated block read the same normalized input with nothing between them, so
# they go to a device together as the queries, keys and values already did.
# The entry point was generalized to a group rather than gaining a second
# fixed shape.
#
#   seven tokens, device, three sent apart   0.468 s generating
#   seven tokens, device, q/k/v together     0.397 s generating
#   seven tokens, device, and the gate too   0.385 s generating
#
# The second saving is much the smaller, and the honest reason is arithmetic:
# grouping three products removes two submissions a layer, grouping two
# removes one. Three runs of the last figure at a load between 1.39 and 1.44
# read 0.393, 0.385 and 0.365 s, so three per cent is the middle of a spread
# that touches the measurement floor -- worth taking and not worth
# advertising. The digest is unchanged at 5709b12b0517c8c5, which is the part
# that is not a matter of degree.

# 2026-08-18, the layer asks for the sequence. A block's queries, keys and
# values are three matrices read against one normalized input with nothing
# between them, and they now go to a device together: one upload of that
# input, one command buffer, one submission, one fence. It is the first thing
# in the engine to record a sequence, and the first device figure in this
# line of work that moves.
#
#   seven tokens, device, before  0.570 s wall, 0.468 s generating
#   seven tokens, device, after   0.497 s wall, 0.399 s generating
#
# Both at a load under 1.50, same model, same prompt, median of three. Fifteen
# per cent off the generating time for removing two submissions and two fence
# waits a layer -- which is worth saying plainly, because the arithmetic did
# not change at all. The digest is the check that it did not: 5709b12b0517c8c5
# before and after, which is what three products sent together have to
# produce if they are the same three products.

# 2026-08-24, three ways of making the device's product faster, none of which
# does. Nothing here changed and no figure below moves; this entry exists so
# that the next attempt starts after these three rather than at them.
#
# The 110-token prompt on the device reads 1.937 s as the shader stands.
#
#   a row's blocks divided between the lanes, with a workgroup slot per row
#   and a tree reduction at the end -- every shape that fits:
#     64 lanes to a row   4.300 s        16 lanes   1.994 s
#      8 lanes            1.834-2.205 s   4 lanes   2.181 s
#
#   the quants read a word at a time rather than a byte at a time, alternated
#   against the byte-wide decode three rounds each, on generation because the
#   prompt did not move at all:
#     word   3.695, 3.622, 3.381 s        byte   3.744, 3.616, 4.595 s
#     the prompt: 2.018 s word against 1.937 byte
#
# The two bracket each other, which is this file's usual way of saying they
# are the same figure. Fourteen passes over 1.09 GiB in 1.95 s is 7.8 GB/s on
