# THE PROCESSOR'S PROMPT READS 253.5 TOKENS A SECOND and the gap to
# llama.cpp on a prompt is 1.34 times, from 2.5 when this began.

# 2026-08-28  The byte context, and a reorder that made it worse
#
#   Head_Dot_Eighth and Blend_Run_Eighth read a byte context wide, the pair
#   the half-precision one got before it: --kv-cache q8 on the 1419-token
#   prompt goes 18.263 s to 7.971, against 6.698 at full precision -- two and
#   three quarter times slower to one and a fifth, at a quarter of the memory
#   a context costs. f16 reads 7.682 in the same sitting. No digest moves.
#
#   And the finding the counters were pointing at all along. Attention in a
#   batch is one query position at a time, each sweeping the whole committed
#   cache, so a batch of a hundred and twenty-eight reads the cache a hundred
#   and twenty-eight times. That is what two thirds of the program's memory
#   traffic is -- redundant, not slow -- and why prefetching it, unrolling it
#   and halving its bytes had each measured as nothing.
#
#   Swapping the loops to hold a head still while the positions run past it:
#
#     as it is                          7.57 G misses   6.403 s
#     heads outside                    16.18 G          7.148 s
#     heads outside, sixteen a tile    16.29 G          6.973 s
#
#   More than twice the misses, and tiling did not bring them back. A
#   position's keys and values are one contiguous kilobyte across all its
#   heads, so a position at a time reads whole rows and a head at a time
#   reads a quarter of every row it touches. Removing the redundancy needs
#   the cache head-major, which crosses into the device and its shaders.
#   Reverted.
#
#   The sitting, figure by figure:
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.439 s wall (0.065 + 0.374), 1.60 s processor,
#     0.073 s loading; at --arith f32 1.322 s for 8.69 s; at --threads 15
#     0.473 s for 1.85 s
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.065 + 0.123, reference 5.776 + 3.812 --
#     fifty-one times in total, eighty-nine on the prompt; serial 2.36x
#     q8_0, 2.35x q4_k, 3.22x f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.449 s (0.067 + 0.380, 1.58 s
#     processor) against device 0.348 s (0.062 + 0.286, 0.04 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.464 s for 2.52 s against device 0.164 s for 0.17 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.441 s, two-bit 1.713 s, drafted 3.788 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 37 ms and 143 ms a token.
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.463 s and 64 in 2.031 s on the
#     processor, 0.164 s and 1.584 s on the device; llama-bench --device
#     none pp110 333.66 tg64 39.91, -ngl 99 pp110 1667.72 tg64 56.20,
#     -ngl 0 pp110 886.81 tg64 39.84 -- that last one has read 738, 749,
#     756, 760 and 886 across sittings and is the noisiest figure here.
#
#   load:  the speed runs at 0.05 rising to 2.85
#   model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, Q2_K for the draft pair, Q4_K_M for the
#          four-bit comparison
#
# THE PROCESSOR'S PROMPT READS 237.6 TOKENS A SECOND and the gap to
# llama.cpp on a prompt is 1.40 times, from 2.5 when this began.

# 2026-08-28  The traffic was free
#
#   No code changed, so no figure group moved.
#
#   The head-major order the commit before called for was built: the stored
#   cache left alone, and a head-major copy of one layer made once before
#   that layer's attention, with the loop reordered to hold a head still and
#   walk the positions through it.
#
#     as it is           7.57 G misses   6.802 s
#     head-major copy    1.47 G misses   6.788 s
#
#   Five times fewer misses and the same time, better in two rounds of four.
#   The copy adds about 1.4 GB of traffic over the prompt and removes about
#   130, and the 130 were free -- eight fused multiply-adds a position
#   against eight independent accumulators covers a second-level miss that
#   the third level answers, and one layer's committed cache is under three
#   megabytes against sixteen of it.
#
#   Five things have now been tried against the value blend's seven per cent:
#   prefetching one position ahead, prefetching eight, sixteen and
#   twenty-four, unrolling two positions a turn, halving its bytes, and
#   removing five sixths of its reads. None of them moved it. The memory is
#   not what it costs. What is left is twelve instructions a position, and
#   that is where the next attempt should start.

# 2026-08-28  What the two loops are actually waiting for
#
#   No code changed, so no figure group moved.
#
#   Zen 4 dispatches six slots a cycle, so a slot is an operation or a stall
#   and the stall has a side. Whole prompt: 382.0 G instructions, 133.5 G
#   cycles, 25.4 G slots with no ops from the front end, 346.6 G back-end
#   stalls -- 3.2 per cent and 43.3 of the 801 G slots.
#
#                      instr    cycles   IPC   front   back
#     rows_by_strips  283.3 G   87.1 G  3.25    0.6%  39.3%
#     head_scores      25.3 G    9.6 G  2.63    5.2%  40.9%
#     blend_run         9.8 G   10.4 G  0.94    1.1%  78.6%
#
#   The strip kernel is at its arithmetic floor. Per block it issues
#   thirty-six multiply-add-class operations -- sixteen byte dot products,
#   sixteen scaled accumulations, four corrections -- onto the two pipes that
#   take them, eighteen cycles of floor against twenty-four taken, and every
#   one of them is arithmetic the answer needs. The sixteen vpxor a block are
#   a zeroing idiom eliminated at rename and never reach a pipe, so the
#   twenty per cent they looked worth is not there. Five changes in, done.
#
#   The value blend is first-level bound: a position's values are two hundred
#   and fifty-six bytes and thirty-two kilobytes does not hold a prompt's
#   worth of them, so every position is four misses whatever the order --
#   which is why the head-major copy changed nothing either. The only reuse
#   is across query positions: two queries by thirty-two components is eight
#   accumulators and two broadcasts, halves the misses, and costs about
#   seventeen per cent more instructions on a loop running at 0.94.

# 2026-08-28  The blend, a tile of positions at a time
#
#   The score loop has read eight positions at a time since long before this
#   and the blend never had the same argument made about it. Eight heads
#   share one key head's values, so the shape this replaces read the same
#   values eight times over. A tile of sixteen positions outside and the
#   heads inside is sixteen kilobytes against thirty-two of first-level
#   cache.
#
#     before   2.56 G misses   6.739 s
#     after    1.48 G misses   6.569 s
#
#   Three and seven tenths per cent, better in four of four, on one and seven
#   tenths per cent more instructions. blend_run went from 7.8 per cent of
#   the prompt's cycles to 5.6. No digest moves.
#
#   This is what the head-major copy of the commit before was missing: it cut
#   the misses to 1.47 G, within noise of this, and bought nothing, because a
#   megabyte and a half is the second-level cache and sixteen kilobytes is
#   the first. The counter run that told them apart is the one recorded above
#   -- seventy-nine per cent of the blend's slots stalled behind dispatch
#   with the front end idle is a first-level number.
#
#   Generating pays one per cent, worse in three readings of three: 2.035 s
#   against 2.057. A tile costs a load and a store of every accumulator at
#   each of its ends and buys nothing where the range already sits in cache,
#   and sixty-four tokens look back over seventy positions. The first version
#   lost a fifth of the generating run before a guard was put on ranges under
#   a hundred and twenty-eight positions.
#
#   The sitting, figure by figure:
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.439 s wall (0.064 + 0.373), 1.57 s processor,
#     0.068 s loading; at --arith f32 1.306 s for 8.62 s; at --threads 15
#     0.472 s for 1.81 s
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.064 + 0.126, reference 5.744 + 3.824 --
#     fifty times in total, ninety on the prompt; serial 2.36x q8_0, 2.33x
#     q4_k, 3.12x f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.438 s (0.067 + 0.370, 1.58 s
#     processor) against device 0.339 s (0.053 + 0.286, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.437 s for 2.46 s against device 0.163 s for 0.18 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.443 s, two-bit 1.806 s, drafted 3.764 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 37 ms and 151 ms a token.
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.435 s and 64 in 2.027 s on the
#     processor, 0.163 s and 1.581 s on the device; llama-bench --device
#     none pp110 334.97 tg64 39.92, -ngl 99 pp110 1676.10 tg64 56.24,
#     -ngl 0 pp110 867.56 tg64 39.96
#
#   load:  the speed runs at 0.16 rising to 2.25
#   model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, Q2_K for the draft pair, Q4_K_M for the
#          four-bit comparison
#
# THE PROCESSOR'S PROMPT READS 252.9 TOKENS A SECOND and the gap to
# llama.cpp on a prompt is 1.32 times, from 2.5 when this began.

# 2026-08-28  Where a device prompt's time goes
#
#   No code changed, so no figure group moved.
#
#   The device's gap on a prompt is 2.48 times against the processor's 1.32,
#   and nobody had opened it because perf cannot see inside a device. The
#   engine's own per-phase times can, and --budget prints them. A device run
#   of the 1419-token prompt generates twelve tokens after it; subtracting a
#   six-token prompt with the same twelve leaves the prompt.
#
#     attending     0.907 s   40%
#     feeding       0.520 s   23%
#     projecting    0.375 s   16%
#     rotating      0.206 s    9%
#     joining       0.176 s    8%
#     normalizing   0.084 s    4%
#
#   Attention is the largest phase and not close, which is not the
#   processor's shape at all -- there the matrix products are two thirds and
#   attention a seventh. The device turns the products into one matrix
#   multiply a layer against its matrix instruction and they fall to a third
#   between them, leaving attention and its square.
#
#   A perf record of the same run shows what the processor does during it:
#   rms_norm 23.8 per cent, memmove 13.6, add 10.7, apply_rotary 4.7,
#   encode_plain 3.3. No attention symbols, so attention is on the device;
#   the rotation and the cache writes are not, and that is the nine per cent.

# 2026-08-28  A shader that is better and cannot be landed
#
#   No code changed, so no figure group moved.
#
#   Attention is forty per cent of a device prompt. The shader already tiles
#   queries -- attention.comp's third compilation defines QUERY_TILE and
#   answers four positions a workgroup -- so what is left is the constants.
#   QUERIES and room multiply into the per-lane store, held[QUERIES][room/64].
#
#     four queries, 256 window    0.856 s attending   2.611 s prompt
#     eight queries, 256 window        --             26 per cent worse
#     eight queries, 128 window   0.707 s attending   2.490 s prompt
#
#   Seventeen per cent off attention and four and a half off the device
#   prompt, better in three of three, digest unchanged.
#
#   It cannot be landed here. Changing a shader means regenerating the words
#   committed beside it, and this machine's glslang is 15.1.0 while the
#   committed words were built by another. Recompiled with the documented
#   flags and nothing else changed, the set is stable but different: 2.611 s
#   against the committed 2.40, and a digest of 1a26d24d33b8957b where the
#   committed device build gives b3d99fb4151edc6d. Seven per cent, which is
#   more than the four and a half the change wins.
#
#   WHICH GLSLANG BUILT THE COMMITTED SHADER WORDS IS NOT RECORDED ANYWHERE
#   and should be. This host has 15.1.0 and does not reproduce them.
#
#   And a warning about the flags. An hour went into measuring against a
#   baseline whose first attention compilation used --target-env vulkan1.0,
#   which is not what the README says. That build was slower and answered
#   differently on every run -- five digests in five runs of the same binary
#   -- and it was caught only by going back to the committed tree and finding
#   it stable at one. The three compilations are: plain -V, then --target-env
#   vulkan1.1 -DSUBGROUPS, then the same with -DQUERY_TILE.

# 2026-08-29  The words did not match the source, and eight queries a block
#
#   The note above said a shader change was blocked because this machine's
#   glslang gave a set that ran seven per cent slower and answered
#   differently. The toolchain was right and the source was wrong.
#
#   Compare the nine compiled shaders one at a time rather than diffing the
#   generated file as a whole: seven reproduce byte for byte with glslang
#   15.1.0 and the documented flags. Only matrix_product.spv and
#   matrix_extra.spv differ, in thirty-six words, and disassembled the
#   difference is one constant -- 64 in the committed words against 128 from
#   the committed source. TILE_V in matrix_product.comp says 128 and the
#   words were built from 64. Somebody changed it and never regenerated.
#
#   EVERY DEVICE FIGURE THIS FILE HAS PUBLISHED DESCRIBES TILE_V = 64. The
#   edit that says 128 has never run, and compiling it makes a device prompt
#   seven per cent slower. The source is set back to 64 and all nine now
#   reproduce exactly.
#
#   The shader check cannot catch this: it compares the source against a
#   digest recorded when the words were made, so a stale .spv handed to
#   'tests shader' updates the digest and leaves the words. It proves the
#   source has not changed since somebody ran the tool, not that the words
#   came from the source.
#
#   The compiler and flags, which were recorded nowhere:
#     glslang 15.1.0 (Ubuntu glslang-tools 15.1.0-2)
#     attention.comp      -V
#     attention.comp      -V --target-env vulkan1.1 -DSUBGROUPS
#     attention.comp      -V --target-env vulkan1.1 -DSUBGROUPS -DQUERY_TILE
#     combine.comp        -V
#     row_product.comp    -V
#     row_product.comp    -V -DSINGLE
#     half_batch.comp     -V
#     matrix_product.comp --target-env vulkan1.3
#     matrix_product.comp --target-env vulkan1.3 -DMORE_FORMATS
#   and 'tests shader' takes them in that order, which is the order the
#   generated file holds them in.
#
#   With the shaders reproducible, the attention change lands. QUERIES and
#   room multiply into held[QUERIES][room/64]:
#
#     four queries, 256 window    0.931 s attending   2.427 s prompt
#     eight queries, 256 window        --             26 per cent worse
#     eight queries, 128 window   0.782 s attending   2.337 s prompt
#
#   Sixteen per cent off attention, three and seven tenths off the device
#   prompt, better in three of three, digest unchanged. Query_Block in the
#   engine moved with it; a repository check caught that the two had to agree
#   and the conformance sweep did not, because the dispatch asked for blocks
#   of four while the shader answered eight -- half the workgroups redoing
#   work, and right answers either way.
#
#   The sitting, device and outside only; the other five groups did not move.
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.435 s (0.066 + 0.368, 1.60 s
#     processor) against device 0.339 s (0.051 + 0.288, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.411 s for 2.40 s against device 0.136 s for 0.15 s
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.408 s and 64 in 2.041 s on the
#     processor, 0.136 s and 1.577 s on the device; llama-bench --device
#     none pp110 326.34 tg64 39.80, -ngl 99 pp110 1679.07 tg64 56.28,
#     -ngl 0 pp110 850.12 tg64 39.92
#
# THE DEVICE'S PROMPT GAP IS 2.1 TIMES, from 2.5, and the processor's is 1.2.

# 2026-08-29  The nine per cent is arithmetic, not transfer
#
#   No code changed, so no figure group moved.
#
#   A device prompt spends nine per cent of itself on the processor and the
#   obvious suspect was the transfer: the cache is written a position at a
#   time, two hundred and fifty-six calls a layer for a batch of a hundred
#   and twenty-eight, where the attention dispatch beside it costs one. The
#   rows lie next to each other at both ends, so one call each way does it.
#
#     a call a position    0.208 s rotating
#     a call a layer       0.201 s rotating,  prompt unmoved
#
#   Nothing. Put_Cache writes into a buffer that stays mapped, so a call
#   there is a copy and not a fence -- which is why memmove shows in a
#   profile of a device run and no submission does. Reverted.
#
#   The phase is its own arithmetic. Replacing the cosine and the sine with
#   the angle itself, wrong answers and right timing:
#
#     as it is                       0.203 s rotating
#     without the transcendentals    0.137 s rotating
#
#   A third of the phase is two transcendentals a pair a position a layer, in
#   binary64. And half of that third is redundant: Apply_Rotary is called
#   once for the queries and once for the keys at every position of a batch,
#   with the same position, base, scaling and factors, and each call computes
#   the same table of angles. Sharing it between the two is bit for bit the
#   same answer and about one and a half per cent of a device prompt.

# 2026-08-29  One table, two rotations
#
#   A position rotates its queries and its keys by the same angles and each
#   call computed the table again. One call now.
#
#     two calls, two tables   0.201 s rotating   2.339 s device prompt
#     one call, one table     0.154 s rotating   2.188 s device prompt
#
#   Twenty-three per cent off the phase, six and a half off a device prompt,
#   better in three of three, and one and a half off the processor's prompt
#   as well. Every digest unchanged.
#
#   The loop is written out twice on purpose. Factored into a nested
#   procedure the two vectors share, the processor lost six per cent of a
#   prompt and Inline_Always did not give it back -- the loop stopped being
#   one the compiler could see the bounds of.
#
#   AND A WARNING ABOUT THE METHOD. Measured with single runs the processor
#   looked six per cent worse and stayed that way through two attempts to fix
#   it; the spread within one variant was ten per cent. Five alternated
#   rounds of three said one and a half per cent better in four of five. A
#   change worth one per cent cannot be seen by a method that reads five as
#   signal.
#
#   The sitting, figure by figure:
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.428 s wall (0.064 + 0.364), 1.57 s processor,
#     0.068 s loading; at --arith f32 1.386 s for 8.81 s; at --threads 15
#     0.484 s for 1.85 s
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.066 + 0.122, reference 5.845 + 3.875 --
#     fifty-two times in total, eighty-nine on the prompt; serial 2.35x
#     q8_0, 2.35x q4_k, 3.13x f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.448 s (0.060 + 0.386, 1.58 s
#     processor) against device 0.335 s (0.051 + 0.283, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.411 s for 2.43 s against device 0.155 s for 0.17 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.447 s, two-bit 1.725 s, drafted 3.798 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 37 ms and 144 ms a token.
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.409 s and 64 in 2.025 s on the
#     processor, 0.155 s and 1.578 s on the device; llama-bench --device
#     none pp110 331.01 tg64 40.00, -ngl 99 pp110 1659.97 tg64 55.86,
#     -ngl 0 pp110 823.96 tg64 39.85
#
#   The 110-token device row is the noisy one and it read 0.136 s the sitting
#   before and 0.155 here, which moves its published rate from 808.8 to
#   709.7 and the device gap from 2.1 to 2.3 on code that measured six and a
#   half per cent faster on the long prompt. Twelve generated tokens sit
#   inside that figure and a hundred and ten prompt tokens is where the fixed
#   costs still show; the long prompt is the one to read for this change.
#
#   load:  the speed runs at 0.11 rising to 2.81
#   model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, Q2_K for the draft pair, Q4_K_M for the
#          four-bit comparison

# 2026-08-29  The comparison table, at the length it is judged on
#
#   No code changed. What changed is what is published.
#
#   Every headline in this file has been taken on a 110-token prompt, and
#   every change is judged on the 1419-token one, alternated. The two do not
#   say the same thing, and the short one is the noisier and the kinder.
#
#   Settled at 53 C, medians of three, llama-bench -r 3:
#
#                          110 tokens        1419 tokens
#     model_runner cpu     269.0 t/s         214.8 t/s
#     llama.cpp cpu        331.0 +/- 21      282.2 +/- 0.6
#     model_runner device  709.7 t/s         743.7 t/s
#     llama.cpp device    1660.0 +/- 54     1791.5 +/- 4.9
#
#     gap, processor         1.2               1.3
#     gap, device            2.3               2.4
#
#   The gap is wider at length -- attention grows with the square of the
#   context and it is what this program is furthest behind on -- and the
#   spread is thirty times narrower. A hundred and ten tokens is where a
#   call's fixed cost still shows, on both sides: llama.cpp's own device row
#   reads 1660 at 110 and 1791 at 1419, going faster on the longer prompt
#   because the fixed part amortises.
#
#   Both tables are kept. Nine sittings of history in that section were taken
#   on the short prompt and a reader comparing against somebody else's pp110
#   needs it. The long one is the figure to argue about.
#
#   ours, long: cpu 6.607 s evaluating for 1419 tokens, device 1.908 s;
#     llama-bench --device none pp1419 282.18 tg64 39.83, -ngl 99 pp1419
#     1791.52 tg64 56.04

# 2026-08-29  The device broken down again, and the score tile refused
#
#   No code changed.
#
#   A device prompt after eight queries a workgroup and one table of angles,
#   long prompt with a generation-only run subtracted:
#
#                    before            now
#     attending    0.907 s  40%     0.690 s  35%
#     feeding      0.520 s  23%     0.505 s  26%
#     projecting   0.375 s  16%     0.367 s  19%
#     rotating     0.206 s   9%     0.158 s   8%
#     joining      0.176 s   8%     0.165 s   8%
#     normalizing  0.084 s   4%     0.079 s   4%
#
#   Attention a quarter cheaper and still the largest phase, but the two
#   matrix phases are forty-five per cent together against its thirty-five.
#   llama.cpp reads this prompt in 0.79 s on the same device and this program
#   in 1.98: with attention free this would be 1.29 s and still 1.6 times
#   behind, so what is next on the device is not only attention.
#
#   And the score loop's tile, swept because the blend had just gone from no
#   tiling to sixteen for three and seven tenths of a prompt:
#
#     eight positions   6.423 s      sixteen   6.461 s      thirty-two  6.510 s
#
#   Eight is best and the others are inside its noise. The reuse is complete
#   at eight -- eight key rows are eight kilobytes and all thirty-two heads
#   read them from the nearest cache before the next eight arrive -- so a
#   wider tile gains nothing and pays a longer live range. What the blend
#   gained was tiling where there was none, and that does not generalise to a
#   loop already tiled.

# 2026-08-29  A staleness refusal, and a tile that does not add up
#
#   tests shader now refuses a compiled file older than the source it claims
#   to come from. That is the case that happened -- source edited, compiler
#   not run, tool run -- and it is caught where somebody can act on it. It
#   does not catch a .spv compiled from a different source of the same age,
#   and nothing short of compiling would.
#
#   And then the matrix tile, which is where the device's remaining gap
#   lives. It was not swept, because opening it turned up this:
#
#     matrix_product.comp        TILE_V = 64
#     its comment, two lines on  "all eight vector matrices" -- 128
#     Products                   Tile_Vectors : constant := 128
#                                "The shader states both and this has to
#                                 agree."
#
#   and the dispatch after the matrix pipeline is bound asks for
#   Room / Tile_Vectors workgroups.
#
#   The note above concluded the committed words were authoritative and set
#   the source to 64. THAT CONCLUSION NOW LOOKS WRONG: two other statements
#   in the repository say 128 and one is three lines from the constant.
#
#   Not in doubt: the shipped device path has always run the words built at
#   64; it passes the sweep at 28344 sequences with nothing outside
#   tolerance; and compiling the source at 128 measures seven per cent
#   slower. Those three do not fit the reading that 128 is simply right.
#
#   The tile is left where the shipped words have always been. What this
#   wants is somebody who knows why a dispatch of Room / 128 workgroups
#   against a shader answering 64 vectors each is conformant, because until
#   that is understood any number put in either place is a guess.

# 2026-08-29  Every device prompt over sixty-four tokens was wrong
#
#   The note above left the tile constants disagreeing and said so. The
#   disagreement was a bug and the sweep passing was the thing to disbelieve.
#
#   A workgroup answers TILE_V vectors; the dispatch asks for
#   Room / Tile_Vectors workgroups. Sixty-four against a hundred and
#   twenty-eight means a workgroup answered the first sixty-four vectors of
#   every tile and nothing answered the rest. Greedy, both backends, same
#   prompt:
#
#     ~18 tokens    device says what the processor says
#     ~36 tokens    device says what the processor says
#     ~72 tokens    usedovoovoovoovoovoovoo
#     ~180 tokens   ielélélélélovoélélélél
#
#   EVERY DEVICE PROMPT OF MORE THAN SIXTY-FOUR TOKENS CAME BACK AS NOISE,
#   and every device figure in this file was timing it.
#
#   TILE_V is a hundred and twenty-eight now, which is what the shader's own
#   comment two lines from the constant has said all along. The device
#   returns the processor's digests to the bit: cbf29ce484222325 at 110
#   tokens where it returned 7614f34a26a84b3c, 1a26d24d33b8957b at 1419
#   where it returned b3d99fb4151edc6d. It also stops when the processor
#   stops -- the old 110-token device run read the noise, never found its
#   ending, and ran on to twelve tokens, which is written in every sitting
#   above and which nobody read as a symptom.
#
#   How it survived, which is the part worth keeping:
#
#     the sweep's longest sequence is eight tokens, so no comparison it makes
#       crosses a tile of anything;
#     a speed run reports a device digest that nothing compares against the
#       processor's, and a device differing from a processor is expected;
#     and the check that would have caught it exists for attention.comp's
#       QUERIES against Query_Block and was never written for the matrix
#       tile. It is written now and fails on the old numbers.
#
#   THE SWEEP'S BLIND SPOT IS STILL THERE: eight tokens against a tile of a
#   hundred and twenty-eight. Lengthening it catches the class rather than
#   the instance and costs the gate real time.
#
#   The sitting, device and outside; the other groups did not move.
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.438 s (0.068 + 0.369, 1.59 s
#     processor) against device 0.337 s (0.051 + 0.285, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.430 s for 2.48 s against device 0.193 s for 0.13 s
#
#   outside, 110: model_runner 110 in 0.428 s and 64 in 2.016 s on the
#     processor, 0.191 s and 1.626 s on the device; llama-bench --device
#     none pp110 329.38 tg64 39.90, -ngl 99 pp110 1677.00 tg64 56.08,
#     -ngl 0 pp110 832.55 tg64 39.91
#
#   outside, 1419: model_runner 6.546 s on the processor and 2.010 s on the
#     device, both answering 1a26d24d33b8957b; llama.cpp's 1419 figures are
#     the ones taken in the sitting before this and were not re-run.
#
#   The device's prompt gap is 2.9 times at 110 tokens and 2.5 at 1419,
#   against 2.3 and 2.4 published for a computation that skipped half of
#   every tile.

# 2026-08-29  The device breakdown, taken on a device that was working
#
#   No code changed. The breakdown two notes above was measured on the path
#   that skipped half of every matrix tile. Four runs, prompt only, with a
#   generation-only run subtracted:
#
#                  broken path        corrected
#     feeding     0.505 s  26%      0.650 s  33%
#     attending   0.690 s  35%      0.590 s  30%
#     projecting  0.367 s  19%      0.370 s  19%
#     joining     0.165 s   8%      0.156 s   8%
#     rotating    0.158 s   8%      0.145 s   7%
#     normalizing 0.079 s   4%      0.082 s   4%
#
#   Feeding is the largest phase and it was never attention. It grew
#   twenty-nine per cent when it began computing the tiles it had been
#   skipping, which is the shape of the bug: the products are what the matrix
#   shader does and the feed-forward holds the biggest of them.
#
#   Attention fell fifteen per cent and does not use the matrix shader at
#   all. What changed is what it was fed: the old path handed it the output
#   of half a product and the rest of a buffer, and that arithmetic was
#   slower than arithmetic on real numbers. A phase timed on noise is not
#   timing anything.
#
#   The two matrix phases are fifty-two per cent of a device prompt against
#   attention's thirty. llama.cpp reads this prompt in 0.79 s and this
#   program in 1.99: with attention free this would be 1.40 s and still 1.8
#   times behind, where the broken measurement said 1.6.

# 2026-08-29  The tile sweep, and why there was almost nothing to sweep
#
#   No code changed. Six shapes, the engine's Tile_Rows and Tile_Vectors
#   moved with each so the agreement check is satisfied, device long prompt:
#
#     32 x 128, chunk 32     2.34 s      <- as it is
#     32 x  64, chunk 32     2.45 s
#     16 x 128, chunk 32     2.56 s
#     64 x  64, chunk 32     does not run
#     64 x 128, chunk 32     does not run
#     32 x 128, chunk 64     does not run
#
#   "A run did not complete; nothing published", before any answer comes
#   back. Every shape that fails moves TILE_R or KCH; every shape that runs
#   leaves both alone. The staging loop maps lanes onto wt[TILE_R * KCH] by
#   hand, so moving either end leaves a kernel that compiles and does not
#   work.
#
#   It is one free parameter, not three, and TILE_V is already at its best:
#   a hundred and twenty-eight beats sixty-four by five per cent, which is
#   what a batch of a hundred and twenty-eight vectors predicts -- one tile
#   exactly, against two dispatches for the same work.
#
#   The matrix shader is not a kernel with a tunable tile; it is a kernel
#   written for one tile, and making it take another means rewriting the
#   staging rather than changing a constant.

# 2026-08-29  What lengthening the sweep costs
#
#   No code changed. The blind spot has been named twice without a number on
#   it; here it is.
#
#   A fifth sequence of seventy-two tokens, used by one comparison -- the
#   batched device one, the only place it could have mattered -- and the
#   sweep timed:
#
#     as it is                  about 5 minutes
#     with the long sequence    over 40, stopped rather than finished
#
#   The cost is not the comparison. The sweep's expectations come from an
#   independent implementation written for clarity, in binary64 and without a
#   pool, and it computes every sequence for every fixture whether a
#   comparison asks for it or not. Seventy-two positions of that, thirteen
#   architectures and fifteen formats over, is the forty minutes.
#
#   So the sweep is the wrong instrument. What the missing test asks is
#   whether two backends agree, not whether one agrees with an independent
#   implementation, and two backends can be asked that for the price of
#   running them. A test doing exactly that was written and does not run: the
#   processor side returns BACKEND_CLOSED where the same calls in the harness
#   beside it do not, which is a fixture question and is unresolved. It is
#   not committed.


# 2026-08-29  The scale cache, and why deleting seven per cent bought nothing
#
#   Built, measured and taken out again. The kernel that reads a batch of
#   vectors against a tile of rows begins by extracting every weight scale
#   the tile needs -- a two-byte load at a thirty-four byte stride, once for
#   every block of every row, once for every tile of every batch. A prompt of
#   1419 tokens is eleven batches and reads the same numbers eleven times,
#   and a profile put that loop at 7.6 per cent of the run.
#
#   So it was hoisted the whole way out: a table on the processor pool
#   holding one Q8_0 tensor's scales, filled the first time a product asks
#   for that tensor and read by every batch after, keyed by the tensor's own
#   first byte -- its buffer's address and its offset into it, not the
#   address, which every tensor of a mapped model shares.
#
#   The kernel's share fell as predicted and the run did not move:
#
#                             as it is        with the cache
#     rows                       7.6%              4.7%
#     instructions             388.6 G           384.3 G
#     cycles                   132.9 G           132.4 G
#     cache references          28.2 G            31.6 G
#     wall, evaluating          6.733 s           6.786 s
#     peak resident            1201 MB           1329 MB
#
#   Five alternated rounds of three, medians; the digest 1a26d24d33b8957b
#   either way, so the table is right.
#
#   Four thousand million instructions gone and not one cycle. The reason is
#   in the third row: the extraction is not a cost, it is a walk ahead of the
#   strip kernel over the very lines the strip kernel is about to read, and
#   the weights come from memory whether or not anything reads their scales
#   first. Taking it away replaces a warm read with a cold one and adds three
#   thousand million references to a hundred and twenty-eight megabytes of
#   table that must itself be fetched. The entry of 2026-08-27 guessed
#   "perhaps six per cent" for this; the guess was wrong, and it was wrong
#   because it priced instructions in a place where the machine is priced in
#   bytes -- the same mistake, and the same answer, as the prefetch and the
#   two-a-turn unroll before it.
#
#   Nothing is committed. The measurement is what there is.

# 2026-08-29  The wake, not the work
#
#   The entry above said a generated token is done adding workers at four
#   shares and called it the bus. It is half right, and the half it got
#   wrong was seven per cent.
#
#   What said so was counting cores. Sixty-four tokens, both runtimes on the
#   same file, perf stat:
#
#                          this program      llama.cpp
#     wall                    2.12 s           1.63 s
#     cycles                 31.0 G           56.5 G
#     instructions           87.1 G           39.2 G
#     cores busy              3.4              8.0
#
#   Thirteen core-seconds against seven, and thirty per cent less wall for
#   them. Half the machine was idle, and an idle run is not a run out of
#   memory bandwidth. Two copies of this program generating at once move
#   44 GB/s of the model where one moves 37, so the bus had a fifth left in
#   it and the pool was not asking for it.
#
#   It was not asking because its workers were asleep. A worker blocked on a
#   protected entry is blocked in the kernel; a generated token posts a
#   hundred and fifty-five products; the key and value projections are 256
#   rows, thirty-two rows a worker, and a share that small can be shorter
#   than the wake that delivered it.
#
#   So a ticket counter and a share counter sit beside the coordinator. A
#   worker spins on the first for about ten microseconds before entering the
#   entry, and a submitting task spins on the second before entering Await.
#   Neither decides anything: the coordinator still opens the barrier, hands
#   the job over, says when the job is done and says when the pool is
#   closing, so a run in which no spin ever finds anything is the run this
#   had before.
#
#                            before      after
#     64 generated           2.058 s    1.906 s    better in 4 of 4
#     1419-token prompt      6.549 s    6.450 s    better in 4 of 4
#
#   Medians of three, alternated. Digests unchanged: 3248ac1bb7011de0
#   generating, 1a26d24d33b8957b on the prompt.
#
#   The spin budget was swept -- four thousand turns, twenty thousand, sixty
#   thousand. Generating reads 1.91, 1.90, 1.90; the prompt reads 6.69, 6.34,
#   and was not taken at sixty. Four thousand is worse on a prompt, which is
#   the shape a spin that ends before the next job arrives makes, and twenty
#   thousand is what is kept.
#
#   Vector_Team was swept again with the spin in: four 1.89 s at 8.1 s of
#   processor, six 1.89 at 11.3, eight 1.94 at 15.1. Four stays.
#
#   Two other explanations were measured first and are not it. The
#   single-vector row tile at one, two, four and eight rows: 2.17 s, 2.08,
#   2.04, 2.04 -- and one row at a time, which walks the weights perfectly
#   contiguously, is the worst of the four. Neither more misses in flight per
#   worker nor a cleaner stream inside a share was the lever.
#
#   The sitting, figure by figure:
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.403 s wall (0.060 + 0.343), 1.85 s processor,
#     0.064 s loading; at --arith f32 1.175 s for 9.15 s; at --threads 15
#     0.437 s for 2.42 s
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.060 + 0.113, reference 5.716 + 3.814 --
#     fifty-five times in total, ninety-five on the prompt; serial 2.36x
#     q8_0, 2.30x q4_k, 3.08x f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.403 s (0.061 + 0.341, 1.84 s
#     processor) against device 0.346 s (0.057 + 0.288, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.404 s for 2.62 s against device 0.199 s for 0.40 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.395 s, two-bit 1.494 s, drafted 3.211 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 33 ms and 124 ms a token.
#
#   shares, sixty-four generated and the 110-token prompt: three 1.909 s for

#     five 1.889 and 7.42, 0.561 and 2.52; eight 1.919 and 7.97, 0.383 and
#     2.57

#     12925 at seven shares, 12902 at eight; thirty-two a pass 4685, 23895,
#     24298. q4_k one vector 2522 and 14103 at eight; thirty-two 4598 and
#     26017. Unpinned at load 1.64: q8_0 one vector 13194 at seven and 14361
#     at eight, thirty-two 24163 and 25862.
