#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.383 s and 64 in 1.861 s on the
#     processor, 0.182 s and 1.608 s on the device; 1419 in 5.627 s and
#     1.997 s. llama-bench --device none pp110 393.54 pp1419 312.37 tg64
#     40.33, -ngl 99 pp110 1664.21 pp1419 1811.60 tg64 56.39, -ngl 0 pp110
#     878.83 pp1419 1330.66 tg64 40.38
#
#   The speed runs were taken as the load rose from 0.24 to 3.6, which is
#   what a sitting of back-to-back medians of three does to a load average,
#   and the part ended at 91 C. The two benchmark runs were taken afterwards
#   on a part let back down to 53 C, because every figure in them is a
#   serial rate or a ratio against one and those are the boost-sensitive
#   ones.
#
#   load:  the speed runs at 0.24 rising to 3.62; the benchmarks at 0.10
#          pinned and 1.64 unpinned
#   model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, Q2_K for the draft pair, Q4_K_M for the
#          four-bit comparison

# 2026-08-29  A prompt that answered differently at four threads
#
#   Not a figure. A fault, found while reading the sitting above: the
#   sixty-four-token digests in the share sweep were not all the same.
#
#     --threads 3   3248ac1bb7011de0
#     --threads 4   3740ed87be385f2d
#     --threads 7   3248ac1bb7011de0
#
#   Same file, same seed, temperature zero. Present in the commit before the
#   wake change as well, so it is not that; the first token that differs is
#   the forty-third, and --batch-size 1 removes it.
#
#   A batch is computed a tile of rows at a time, and a tile of an odd size
#   takes a different kernel from a full one -- eight lanes folded one way
#   against four folded another. The rows were cut into shares without
#   regard to the tile, so every share ended in a short tile and where they
#   fell followed the share count. A few units in the last place, made while
#   reading a six-token prompt, carried in the key cache until a token whose
#   top two logits were close enough for it to decide.
#
#   A share boundary now falls on a multiple of the row tile. At the worker
#   count the program chooses for itself the partition is bit-identical to
#   what it was -- 2048, 5632, 256 and 32000 rows all divide by eight into
#   eight shares -- so nothing published moves, and --threads 2 through 15
#   all answer 3248ac1bb7011de0.
#
#   Alternated three rounds of three each way, because the partition is
#   less even for share counts that are not divisors: the long prompt reads
#   6.25 s against 6.33 and the medians cross when the order is reversed,
#   which is level. The figures were taken again in one sitting regardless,
#   because the sources behind six groups moved.
#
#   The test that should have caught it compares a parallel product against
#   a serial one at every worker count, against a binary32 weight and one
#   vector -- the one shape the integer tile kernel is never asked for. The
#   new one is a hundred rows of Q8_0 against a batch of eight, every
#   element compared bit for bit at every share count from one to eight; it
#   fails on the old partition at two workers, on element 99.
#
#   It asserts nothing while a pool is in scope. A raised assertion skips
#   Close and the frame then waits for workers nobody has told to finish:
#   the first version of the test hung for ten minutes where it meant to
#   fail. The test beside it had the same shape and now does not.
#
#   The sitting, figure by figure:
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.399 s wall (0.059 + 0.337), 1.83 s processor,
#     0.061 s loading; at --arith f32 1.094 s for 8.61 s; at --threads 15
#     0.409 s for 2.20 s
#
#     Re-taken settled at 50 C after the rest of the sitting, because the
#     first reading of the sitting was 0.467 s where the same command read
#     0.408 and 0.402 later in it -- three readings of one command in one
#     sitting, and the outlier was the one the section publishes. Taken
#     again it read 0.399 and 0.401 back to back.
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.054 + 0.115, reference 5.609 + 3.716 --
#     fifty-six times in total, a hundred and four on the prompt; serial
#     2.37x q8_0, 2.30x q4_k, 3.09x f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.408 s (0.057 + 0.351, 1.86 s
#     processor) against device 0.358 s (0.065 + 0.292, 0.04 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.430 s for 2.71 s against device 0.178 s for 0.36 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.402 s, two-bit 1.479 s, drafted 3.147 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 34 ms and 123 ms a token.
#
#   shares, sixty-four generated and the 110-token prompt: three 1.864 s for
#     5.77 s and 0.769 s for 2.18 s; four 1.763 and 7.20, 0.545 and 2.00;
#     five 1.821 and 7.48, 0.556 and 2.51; eight 1.856 and 7.90, 0.387 and
#     2.60. Every one of the eight digests the same, which is the point of
#     the entry above.
#
#   scaling, pinned and settled at load 0.59: q8_0 one vector 2636 serial,
#     13181 at seven shares, 14537 at eight; thirty-two a pass 4806, 24509,
#     26918. q4_k one vector 2622 and 14527 at eight; thirty-two 4830 and
#     26988. Unpinned at load 1.98: q8_0 one vector 13326 at seven and 14578
#     at eight, thirty-two 23320 and 26215.
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.375 s and 64 in 1.866 s on the
#     processor, 0.179 s and 1.589 s on the device; 1419 in 5.533 s and
#     1.930 s. llama-bench --device none pp110 393.25 pp1419 311.06 tg64
#     40.41, -ngl 99 pp110 1674.69 pp1419 1805.61 tg64 56.35, -ngl 0 pp110
#     885.55 pp1419 1346.58 tg64 40.40
#
#   load:  the speed runs at 0.18 rising to 3.61; the benchmarks at 0.59
#          pinned and 1.98 unpinned, from a part at 50 C
#   model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, Q2_K for the draft pair, Q4_K_M for the
#          four-bit comparison

# 2026-08-29  The tile made tunable, swept, and put back
#
#   The matrix shader's staging was rewritten so that TILE_R, TILE_V and KCH
#   are numbers rather than assumptions, the shapes it could not reach were
#   swept, and the rewrite was taken out again. Three things were learned
#   and one of them is a check.
#
#   The staging was sixty-four invocations decoding thirty-two rows of a
#   thirty-two column step, half a row each, with the invocation number cut
#   in two to say which row and which half. The rewrite deals sixteen-value
#   units round the workgroup instead: a unit is half a thirty-two element
#   block, which is the largest piece every format here decodes the same
#   way, and the units are dealt round however many invocations there are.
#   A workgroup of more than one subgroup was made possible at the same
#   time, each subgroup carrying its own slice of the vectors.
#
#   Every shape then ran, where the sweep of 2026-08-29 found three of six
#   producing a kernel that compiled and answered nothing. The long device
#   prompt, medians of three:
#
#     32 x 128, step 32, one subgroup      1.935 s   <- as it is
#     64 x 128, step 32                    2.058
#     32 x 128, step 64                    2.062
#     64 x  64, step 32                    2.148
#     16 x 128, step 32                    2.198
#     32 x 128, step 128                   2.122
#     64 x 128, step 64                    2.213
#     32 x 128, step 32, two subgroups     2.130
#     32 x 128, step 32, four subgroups    2.461
#     32 x 256, step 32, batch 256         3.207
#     32 x 256, step 32, two, batch 256    2.803
#
#   Every one of the eleven answered 1a26d24d33b8957b. The shape that was
#   already there is the best of them, and it is not close: the nearest
#   alternative is six per cent behind.
#
#   Raising the vector tile above a hundred and twenty-eight is worse even
#   with the batch raised to match, which was the one combination the old
#   sweep could not ask. So the weights are not what this shader is waiting
#   for: reading them half as often costs sixty per cent. Nor is it
#   occupancy -- more subgroups sharing a tile is worse at every count. What
#   is left is the register pressure of the accumulators against the cost of
#   the decode, and thirty-two by a hundred and twenty-eight is where those
#   two cross.
#
#   The rewrite itself costs one and a half per cent of a device prompt --
#   1.99 s against 1.965, better in three of eleven alternated rounds, in
#   three forms: a strided loop, a counted loop, and a counted loop with the
#   step arithmetic guarded by constants so the kept shape folds back to a
#   straight line. All three cost about the same, so it is the loop and not
#   the arithmetic in it.
#
#   Generality that costs one and a half per cent and unlocks nothing is not
#   kept. What is kept is a repository check that TILE_R and KCH are
#   thirty-two, which is what the staging is written for, so that the next
#   person to turn one of those two gets a failure rather than a kernel that
#   runs and answers nothing.

# 2026-08-29  The blend is not waiting for its loads
#
#   Four measurements against the value blend, which has been five per cent
#   of a prompt and unexplained since the counters were first read. Nothing
#   is kept; what is kept is why.
#
#   The tile, swept again now that the wake change has moved everything
#   around it. Long prompt, medians of three:
#
#     8 positions    6.161 s
#     16             6.230      <- as it is
#     32             6.222
#     64             6.304
#
#   Flat. Eight is a per cent under sixteen and that is the noise floor.
#
#   Where the first-level misses are, by symbol, counting
#   l2_cache_req_stat.dc_access_in_l2 on the long prompt:
#
#     rows_by_strips    83.4%
#     head_scores        6.2%
#     blend_run          5.2%
#     rows               2.1%
#
#   The blend's share of the misses is its share of the cycles, near enough:
#   5.2 against 5.0. It is not missing disproportionately, which the tile of
#   sixteen positions is what fixed.
#
#   So the loads themselves were tried. Blend_Run_Twice takes two heads of
#   one key head's group -- they read the same values and weight them
#   differently -- loads a position's values into registers once and
#   multiplies them into both, which is half the loads for a fifth more
#   instructions. On a bench reading from the second-level cache it is 39867
#   Me/s against two single blends' 29788, **one and a third times faster**.
#
#   In the engine it is worth nothing. blend_run_twice takes 4.92 per cent
#   of the prompt where blend_run took 4.95, and the wall is level: 6.388 s
#   against 6.309, worse in the median of seven alternated rounds. The bench
#   measured the wrong regime -- in a tile of sixteen positions with the
#   heads inside, every head after the first reads values the first left in
#   the first-level cache, and there the load ports are not what the loop is
#   waiting for.
#
#   And the pair is not free to try. Two heads of sixty-four components is
#   sixteen accumulators, and sixteen is every register the base encoding
#   can name -- with none left for the values, which is the whole point. So
#   the component run must halve, from sixty-four to thirty-two, and a head
#   becomes two passes. That alone, with the pairing switched off, measures
#   6.454 s against 6.309: **two and a third per cent, worse in three of
#   three**, paid in twice the calls and twice the tile bookkeeping.
#
#   Which prices the whole family. Any two-way pairing of this blend --
#   across heads, as here, or across query positions, as the entry of
#   2026-08-28 proposed -- needs sixteen accumulators, so it needs the
#   narrow run, so it starts two and a third per cent behind. Query pairing
#   would halve the first-level misses where head pairing only halves the
#   loads, and halving those misses is what the tile did for three and
#   seven tenths per cent; but it has to find that twice over before it is
#   level.
#
#   The register file is the constraint, not the cache and not the loads.

# 2026-08-29  A tile of thirty-two rows
#
#   The strip kernel is sixty-two per cent of a prompt and this file had
#   called it finished: thirty-six multiply-add-class operations a block
#   onto two pipes, eighteen cycles of floor against twenty-four taken, five
#   changes in, done. A third of it was outside that argument.
#
#   At the top of every call it stages the batch's own numbers -- each of
#   the strip's eight vectors, where its scales begin, the scale, and the
#   block total the correction wants. Those are the batch's numbers and not
#   the row's, so they are the same for every tile of rows a share is cut
#   into, and the kernel built them again for each. With a tile of eight
#   rows a share of 2048 is 256 tiles, and a profile put the staging at
#   twelve per cent of the symbol -- a scalar loop of eight instructions in
#   the one kernel whose reason for existing is that it is not scalar.
#
#   The tile is one constant. Long prompt, medians of three:
#
#     Row_Tile   8    6.244 s
#               16    6.206
#               32    5.874     <- kept
#               64    6.059
#              128    6.411
#
#   Five and nine tenths per cent, better in three of three alternated
#   rounds, digest 1a26d24d33b8957b throughout: the same rows multiplied by
#   the same numbers in the same order, four times as many of them for each
#   staging. Generating does not move -- 1.930, 1.916, 1.922, 1.913, 1.941
#   across the same five -- because a generated token asks for a tile of
#   four and always did.
#
#   The curve turns over at sixty-four because the tile's accumulators stop
#   fitting the first-level cache: thirty-two rows by a hundred and
#   twenty-eight vectors is sixteen kilobytes, sixty-four is thirty-two.
#
#   The partition follows the tile since 2026-08-29, and 2048, 5632, 256 and
#   32000 all still divide by thirty-two into eight shares, so no share
#   boundary moves. --threads 2 through 15 still answer 3248ac1bb7011de0.
#
#   Hoisting the rest of it: built and not kept.
#
#   At a tile of thirty-two the staging is still done eight times a share.
#   Staged by the caller for the whole share and handed down a strip at a
#   time, it measures 5.926 s against 5.938 -- level in six alternated
#   rounds, three each way.
#
#   Two shapes were tried and the difference is the part worth keeping.
#   Reading the shared table through one name at a chosen address -- an
#   overlay whose address is picked between the parameter and the local --
#   costs 7.256 s against 6.014, a fifth. An object at an address the
#   compiler cannot reason about is one it cannot prove does not alias
#   Scaling, which the same loop writes, and the loop stops vectorizing.
#   Written out twice, once against the parameter and once against the
#   local, both arms vectorize and the result is level.
#
#   Level because the table it saves building is two kilobytes freshly
#   written into the first-level cache and the table it reads instead is
#   thirty-two kilobytes walked eight strips apart. Building it there is as
#   cheap as fetching it from further away, which is the third time this
#   file has found that shape -- after the row scales a tile re-reads and
#   the values a head re-reads.
#
#   The sitting, figure by figure:
#
#   engine, twelve tokens: 0.399 s wall (0.059 + 0.340), 1.85 s processor,
#     0.067 s loading; at --arith f32 1.137 s for 8.82 s; at --threads 15
#     0.400 s for 2.22 s. Re-taken settled at the end of the sitting, as the
#     entry of 2026-08-29 says the engine trio now is; it read 0.402 first
#     and 0.395 second, so the three readings agree this time.
#
#   reference, four tokens: cpu 0.059 + 0.113, reference 5.579 + 3.753 --
#     fifty-four times in total, ninety-five on the prompt; serial 2.33x
#     q8_0, 2.35x q4_k, 3.11x f32
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.395 s (0.053 + 0.341, 1.79 s
#     processor) against device 0.331 s (0.050 + 0.280, 0.03 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.358 s for 2.38 s against device 0.187 s for 0.39 s
#
#   drafting: eight-bit 0.392 s, two-bit 1.544 s, drafted 3.310 s with 24
#     proposed and 7 accepted -- 33 ms and 129 ms a token.
#
#   shares, sixty-four generated and the 110-token prompt: three 1.845 s for
#     5.73 s and 0.706 s for 2.02 s; four 1.776 and 7.22, 0.514 and 1.88;
#     five 1.837 and 7.54, 0.508 and 2.27; eight 1.873 and 8.01, 0.350 and
#     2.36
#
#   scaling, pinned and settled at load 1.07: q8_0 one vector 2596 serial,
#     12839 at seven shares, 14565 at eight; thirty-two a pass 4759, 23954,
#     26797. q4_k one vector 2625 and 14424 at eight; thirty-two 4790 and
#     24907. Unpinned at load 1.82: q8_0 one vector 12948 at seven and 10098
#     at eight, thirty-two 23307 and 25158.
#
#   kernels, row dot in nanoseconds an element: f32 0.25, bf16 0.31, q4_0
#     0.31, q8_0 0.36, q4_k 0.38, q6_k 0.38, q5_k 0.40, q3_k 0.47, q4_1
#     0.48, iq4xs 0.50, q5_0 0.53, f16 0.54, q5_1 0.57, iq4nl 0.62, q2_k
#     0.69
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.367 s and 64 in 1.869 s on the
#     processor, 0.173 s and 1.596 s on the device; 1419 in 5.266 s and
#     1.971 s. llama-bench --device none pp110 385.34 pp1419 310.24 tg64
#     40.39, -ngl 99 pp110 1653.75 pp1419 1817.11 tg64 56.48, -ngl 0 pp110
#     770.37 pp1419 1368.39 tg64 40.42
#
#   The processor's gap on the long prompt is 1.15 times, from 1.21, which
#   is the narrowest this file has published.
#
#   load:  the speed runs at 0.20 rising to 4.06; the benchmarks at 1.07
#          pinned and 1.82 unpinned
#   model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, Q2_K for the draft pair, Q4_K_M for the
#          four-bit comparison

# 2026-08-29  What a device prompt is not waiting for
#
#   No code changed. Three ablations of the matrix shader, to price the
#   decode before building anything that would delete it.
#
#   The proposal was to upload a model to the device already decoded to half
#   precision, so that the shader would stop unpacking Q8_0 into shared
#   memory on every dispatch -- twelve times over for a 1419-token prompt.
#   It costs two bytes a weight against 1.06, so it is worth knowing what it
#   would buy before it is built.
#
#   Each ablation replaces one thing with a constant. The answers are wrong
#   on purpose; what is measured is the time. Device long prompt, medians of
#   three:
#
#     as it is                                    1.939 s
#     the weights never read or unpacked          1.815    -6.4%
#     the activations never loaded                1.752    -9.6%
#     the weights gone from the tile as well      1.673   -13.7%
#     all three                                   1.720   -11.3%
#
#   The last two bracket each other, so with every operand removed the
#   prompt still takes about 1.70 s of 1.94: **the whole handling of both
#   operands is fourteen per cent of a device prompt**, and the decode alone
#   -- every byte of the weights read and all the arithmetic to unpack them
#   -- is six and a half.
#
#   So the upload is priced out. Deleting the decode cannot buy more than
#   six and a half per cent and would buy less, because an f16 copy still
#   has to be read and is nearly twice the bytes; and it would double what a
#   model costs on the device, which is the resource that decides whether a
#   model runs there at all.
#
#   Where the time is instead, from --budget on the same prompt: feeding
#   40.2 per cent, attending 24.6, projecting 19.6, joining 5.7, rotating
#   5.6, normalizing 2.6, reading out 1.6. The two matrix phases are sixty
#   per cent and the operands are fourteen, so **three quarters of the
#   matrix phase is the instruction itself and the loop around it**.
#
#   Which is the third thing the sweep of 2026-08-29 and these ablations
#   agree on. The weights are not the wall: reading them half as often costs
#   sixty per cent, and not reading them at all buys six. The occupancy is
#   not the wall: more subgroups sharing a tile is worse at every count. The
#   operands are not the wall: fourteen per cent between them. What is left
#   is the rate the part retires cooperative-matrix multiplies at, and
#   nothing tried here moves it.

# 2026-08-29  The floor was mis-counted
#
#   No code changed. The entry of 2026-08-28 said the strip kernel issues
#   thirty-six multiply-add-class operations a block onto two pipes --
#   eighteen cycles of floor against twenty-four taken -- and left a third
#   unaccounted for. Part of that third was the staging the entry above
#   removed. The rest was the count.
#
#   Disassembled rather than described, the block loop is seventy-seven
#   instructions:
#
#     vpxor, zeroing an accumulator or flipping the bias    18
#     vpdpbusd, the byte dot product                        16
#     vcvtdq2ps, the integer sum made a float               16
#     vfmadd231ps, scaled and accumulated                   18
#     loads, pointer arithmetic and the branch               9
#
#   The sixteen conversions were never counted. A dot product here is three
#   operations and not two -- multiply the bytes, convert the sum, scale and
#   add -- because every block carries its own scale and an integer sum
#   cannot be scaled until it is a float. Fifty pipe-bound operations a
#   block, and the floor is twenty-five cycles.
#
#   And the kernel is on it. rows_by_strips retires 243.9 G ops in 76.8 G
#   cycles -- 58.5 per cent of the run's cycles and 71.1 of its retired ops,
#   on a whole of 131.2 G cycles and 343.2 G ops -- which is 3.17 ops a
#   cycle. Seventy-seven ops a block over 243.9 G is 3.17 thousand million
#   block iterations, and 76.8 G cycles over those is 24.2 cycles a block
#   against a floor of twenty-five: under it, because the renamed vpxor
#   retire without occupying a pipe.
#
#   Forty-two per cent of the kernel's cycles retire nothing because an
#   operation is not complete and a quarter of those are loads, which is
#   what a loop at its issue limit looks like from the retire side.
#
#   So there is no third missing. What the count does say is where the cost
#   is: sixteen of the fifty, thirty-two per cent of the kernel's
#   arithmetic, are format conversion rather than multiply-add, and they are
#   there because Q8_0 puts a scale on every thirty-two elements. A wider
#   block would pay fewer of them a weight. This one cannot be made to, and
#   no rewriting of the kernel reaches them.

# 2026-08-29  The score half of attention, and the registers again
#
#   No code changed. head_scores is seven per cent of a prompt and the one
#   attention kernel nothing had been aimed at; its horizontal fold was the
#   suspect and is not the answer.
#
#   One turn of its loop, disassembled, is a hundred and twenty-nine
#   instructions and computes eight positions of one head:
#
#     vfmadd231ps, query against key read where it lies      64
#     vxorps, zeroing an accumulator                          8
#     the fold: 6 vhaddps, 2 vextractf128, 2 vaddps,
#       2 vmulps, 2 stores                                   14
#     pointer advances, the scale broadcast, the branch      43
#
#   Half the body is not the dot product, and the fold is fourteen of a
#   hundred and twenty-nine. The profile agrees: its six vhaddps sample at
#   1.2 to 1.4 per cent each, nine per cent of the symbol between them.
#
#   The counters, on the long prompt:
#
#                        cycles   ops    ops/cyc  ceiling  nothing retired
#     rows_by_strips     76.8 G  243.9 G   3.17     3.08        41.7%
#     head_scores         9.5 G   25.2 G   2.65     3.20        46.7%
#
#   The ceiling is the body's instructions over its pipe-bound ones, times
#   two. rows_by_strips is above its own because the renamed vxorps retire
#   without occupying a pipe; head_scores is at eighty-three per cent of
#   its. Since the strip kernel is proved to be on its floor, forty-seven
#   per cent of cycles retiring nothing is what a balanced loop looks like
#   rather than a symptom.
#
#   What is left is the half of the body that is not arithmetic, and it is
#   structural. Eight positions need eight accumulators and the
#   sixty-four-component query needs eight more: sixteen registers exactly,
#   with nothing left. A block of sixteen positions halves every one of
#   those sixty-five instructions per position -- one fold and one query
#   load for twice the work -- and needs twenty-four registers.
#
#   Which is the third time this session the answer is the register file.
#   The paired blend needed sixteen accumulators and could not have them.
#   The blend's own loop balances loads against multiplies at exactly eight.
#   This needs twenty-four and has sixteen.
#
#   The processor has thirty-two under AVX-512 and the integer kernels use
#   them -- rows_by_strips names ymm16 to ymm25. Model_Runner.Kernels cannot,
#   because it is compiled for baseline x86-64 so the program runs where the
#   wide instructions do not, and its insertions are reached through a
#   run-time flag rather than a second compilation.
#
#   So the unlock is named and not written: a second instantiation of these
#   kernels built for x86-64-v4, chosen by the host check that already
#   chooses between three compilations of the integer product. Attention is
#   twelve and a half per cent of a prompt across its two kernels, both
#   within twenty per cent of their present ceilings, so it could buy a few
#   per cent -- against a third compilation of a unit that is not a generic
#   today.

# 2026-08-29  Device attention, priced, and the rate it runs at
#
#   No code changed. The budget puts attending at 24.6 per cent of a device
#   prompt, second to the two matrix phases, and it had never been taken
#   apart. Three ablations of attention.comp, each replacing one thing with
#   a constant. Device long prompt, medians of three:
#
#     as it is                                    1.911 s
#     the score half, q against k, removed        1.689   -11.6%
#     the blend half, p against v, removed        1.759    -7.9%
#     the subgroup reductions removed             does not run
#
#   The reductions cannot be ablated: without the running maximum a score of
#   minus infinity reaches the exponential and the run refuses, which is the
#   guard doing its job.
#
#   So the two arithmetic halves are 19.5 per cent of a device prompt of the
#   24.6 attending takes, and the softmax, the barriers and the stores are
#   the remaining five. There is nothing hidden in attention: it is its two
#   products.
#
#   And the rate they run at is the finding. Attention is
#
#     pairs x heads x (head_size + value_size) x layers
#       = 1,007,490 x 32 x 128 x 22 = 90.8 G multiply-adds = 181.6 Gflop
#
#   in 19.5 per cent of 1.911 s, which is 0.49 Tflops. The weight product in
#   the same run is 2.94 Tflop in the 59.8 per cent the two matrix phases
#   take, which is 2.57 Tflops. **Attention runs at a fifth of the rate the
#   same part reaches on the same prompt in the same second.**
#
#   The reason is not subtle once it is written down: the weight product
#   goes through VK_KHR_cooperative_matrix and attention does not. Both
#   halves of attention are matrix products -- q against the transpose of k,
#   then the weights against v -- and both are computed a lane at a time in
#   binary32.
#
#   Which prices the next thing rather than doing it. Attention at the
#   matrix product's rate would be about five per cent of a device prompt
#   rather than twenty-five, which is seventeen per cent of the prompt and
#   the largest single number left anywhere in this file. It is also the
#   change llama.cpp already made, which is part of why its device prompt is
#   two and a half times this one.

# 2026-08-29  Attention through the matrix instruction, built and not kept
#
#   The entry above priced attention at the weight product's rate as
#   seventeen per cent of a device prompt, on the ground that the two are
#   both matrix products and only one of them uses the instruction. It was
#   built. It is correct and it is thirteen per cent slower, and why is
#   worth more than the number.
#
#   attention_matrix.comp: a workgroup answers sixteen query positions of
#   one head and walks the cache in tiles, doing the scores as the queries
#   against the transpose of the keys and the blend as the weights against
#   the values, both through coopMatMulAdd at sixteen by sixteen by sixteen.
#   The online softmax is kept -- a running maximum and sum a query, the
#   blend so far rescaled when a tile raises the maximum -- because storing
#   a context length of scores is still what a device cannot afford.
#
#   It is correct, and more than that: the published device digests do not
#   move. 1419 tokens with twelve generated answers 1a26d24d33b8957b and
#   the 110-token prompt answers cbf29ce484222325, which are what the
#   lane-at-a-time kernel answers. Half-precision operands did not move a
#   single token.
#
#   The sweep, device long prompt, medians of three, against 1.911 s as it
#   is:
#
#     room 128, tile 16 positions              2.735 s
#     room 128, tile 32                        3.215
#     room 128, tile 64                        4.718
#     room 64,  tile 16                        2.307
#     room 64,  tile 32                        2.649
#     room 64,  tile 64                        2.917
#     room 64,  tile 16, softmax spread        2.157   <- best
#     room 64,  tile 32, softmax spread        2.473
#     room 64,  tile 64, softmax spread        2.703
#
#   Three shapes were tried and every one of them runs the wrong way: more
#   for the instruction to chew on is worse, not better. That is the answer.
#
#   The instruction takes its operands from shared memory, and shared memory
#   is what bounds how many workgroups a compute unit will hold.
#   attention.comp uses two kilobytes a workgroup and reads a key and a
#   value straight from the cache into the multiply-add; this one must stage
#   them, and at its best shape uses nine and a half. Widening the room from
#   the head's sixty-four to the hundred and twenty-eight the caller allows
#   costs half a second on its own -- the first three rows against the
#   second three -- which is occupancy and nothing else.
#
#   The arithmetic did exactly what it was supposed to. A tile of sixteen
#   positions is eight cooperative-matrix multiplies where the old kernel
#   issues five hundred and twelve multiply-adds a lane for the same
#   thirty-two thousand products: sixty-four times fewer instructions. The
#   arithmetic stopped being the cost and the staging became it.
#
#   What would be needed to win, in the order they are worth trying: stage
#   the keys and the values together and lose a barrier a tile; keep the
#   blend in accumulators rather than in shared memory, which needs a way to
#   rescale an accumulator by a per-row factor -- a diagonal multiply, or a
#   store and reload on the rare tile where a maximum moves; and answer
#   thirty-two queries a workgroup rather than sixteen, to halve the staging
#   a query. Every one of those trades against the shared memory that is
#   already what binds it.
#
#   The kernel and the plumbing are four hundred and eleven lines of GLSL
#   and about eighty of Ada, and are not committed. The seventeen per cent
#   the entry above priced is not available this way.

# 2026-08-29  The registers were never the wall
#
#   No code changed. Three entries running ended on the same sentence --
#   sixteen registers is what these kernels have and what blocks the next
#   step, and the thirty-two AVX-512 offers are out of reach because
#   Model_Runner.Kernels is compiled for baseline x86-64. The unlock was
#   named as a second compilation of the unit.
#
#   It is two lines:
#
#     procedure Probe_Wide (Sums : in out Real_Array);
#     pragma Machine_Attribute (Probe_Wide, "target", "avx512f");
#
#   and the baseline-compiled unit emits
#
#     c5050: 62 e1 7c 28 10 07    vmovups (%rdi),%ymm16
#     c5056: 62 e1 7c 28 11 07    vmovups %ymm16,(%rdi)
#
#   An EVEX prefix and the seventeenth register. The attribute is per
#   subprogram, so a kernel that names it is reached through the same
#   run-time flag its insertion already hides behind and a machine without
#   the instructions never enters it. Model_Runner.Platform.Byte_Products is
#   the question already asked for the integer kernels' third compilation.
#
#   And having asked, neither thing it was wanted for is worth doing.
#
#   The score kernel's sixteen-position block: the entry of this date said a
#   block of sixteen would halve the sixty-five instructions of the loop
#   that are not the dot product. That is wrong and this corrects it. Eight
#   of those zero an accumulator and fourteen fold the accumulators down,
#   and both scale with the accumulators: sixteen positions need sixteen
#   zeroing exclusive-ors and a fold of twelve vhaddps rather than six. Four
#   instructions a call are fixed -- the scale broadcast, the counter, the
#   branch -- so doubling the block saves four per sixteen positions out of
#   ninety-eight per eight. Two per cent of the kernel, a tenth of a per
#   cent of a prompt.
#
#   The paired blend at sixty-four components: the sixteen registers forced
#   thirty-two components and the narrowing cost two and a third per cent,
#   which the upper registers would give back -- but the pairing itself
#   measured level in situ, 4.92 per cent of the prompt against 4.95,
#   because that loop is not load-bound. Level plus 2.3 is still level.
#
#   So the wall three sittings kept arriving at was never the register file.
#   Both attention kernels are bound by the two multiply-add pipes with a
#   fold that is fifteen per cent of the loop by count and structural --
#   cutting it needs positions in the lanes rather than components, which
#   needs the keys component-major, which is the head-major order recorded
#   as buying nothing. More registers move neither.
#
#   What is kept is the two lines. The next thing that wants the upper
#   sixteen can have them without restructuring a compilation unit.

# 2026-08-29  Three more rearrangements of the matrix shader, all level
#
#   No code changed. After the tile sweep, the operand ablations and the
#   attention kernel, three things were left that could be rearranged
#   without changing what the shader computes. None of them moves it.
#
#   The subgroup width. This part's matrix instruction is documented at
#   wave32 and the shader asks for a workgroup of sixty-four, so it was
#   compiled with a workgroup of thirty-two as well -- the staging dealt
#   round however many lanes there are. 1.926 s against 1.917: level.
#
#   The barriers. A workgroup of one subgroup executes in lockstep, so the
#   two barriers a step were replaced with memoryBarrierShared alone:
#   1.907 s against 1.936, one and a half per cent and inside the noise --
#   and not correct in general, because a device whose subgroups are
#   thirty-two wide makes that workgroup two of them and the shader is
#   compiled once for every device. Not pursued for a per cent of noise.
#
#   Software pipelining, which is the one with a real argument behind it.
#   The loop decodes a step, waits, multiplies it and waits again, so the
#   unpack and the instruction are end to end and neither starts until the
#   other has finished. Two buffers instead of one, the turn multiplying the
#   step already decoded while it decodes the next, one barrier a turn
#   rather than two:
#
#     as it is   1.971 s
#     pipelined  1.960 s
#
#   Six alternated rounds of three, three each way, better in four of six.
#   Six tenths of a per cent, which is nothing, and the digest does not
#   move either way so the restructure is right -- it simply does not buy
#   anything. Two kilobytes more of shared memory for six tenths of a per
#   cent is not a trade worth making.
#
#   Which closes the shader. Every rearrangement of it has now been
#   measured -- nine tile shapes, three subgroup counts, the batch against
#   the vector tile, the decode ablated, both operands ablated, the wave
#   width, the barriers and the pipelining -- and the only one that ever
#   mattered was the tile it already had. The two and a half times is not in
#   the shader's structure.

# 2026-08-29  A generated token on the device is sixty-seven submissions
#
#   No code changed. The device generates at 1.41 times llama.cpp and had
#   had no work this sitting. Where its token goes, from --budget: feeding
#   63.1 per cent, projecting 16.9, attending 12.6, reading out 4.8,
#   normalizing 1.2, rotating 0.9, joining 0.5.
#
#   The products are pure streaming, which two ablations of
#   row_product.comp settle. Sixty-four generated tokens, medians of three:
#
#     as it is                                  1.549 s
#     the activation never read                 1.528     -1.4%
#     the weights never read                    0.011
#
#   So the weights are ninety-nine per cent of the row product and the
#   activation is nothing. A token reads 1.09 GiB and the products take
#   about eighty-five per cent of 24.4 ms, which is 55.5 GB/s -- against
#   llama.cpp's whole token of 17.7 ms, which cannot be under 61.6 GB/s even
#   with no overhead at all. Eleven per cent apart on the stream itself, and
#   that is the half of the gap nothing here can reach.
#
#   The other half is attending, and it is not arithmetic. Its cost against
#   context:
#
#     context about 40      3.6 ms a token
#     context about 1450   13.9 ms a token
#
#   Thirty-six times the work for three and nine tenths times the time, so
#   fitting a line gives 0.0073 ms a position and **3.3 ms a token that does
#   not depend on the context at all** -- thirteen and a half per cent of a
#   generated token spent before any position is read.
#
#   What that fixed cost is: a counter on vkQueueSubmit says a run of six
#   prompt tokens and sixty-four generated makes 4421 submissions, and eight
#   generated makes 669, so a generated token is **sixty-seven submissions**
#   -- three a layer. Every one of them is a submit and a wait on a fence,
#   which this file has measured at eighty-three microseconds before it
#   computes anything. Sixty-seven of those is 5.6 ms of a 24.6 ms token:
#   **twenty-three per cent**.
#
#   Which is the largest single cost identified anywhere on the device, and
#   no shader touches it. Everything a token does between the sampler at one
#   end and the sampler at the other is device work -- the norms, the
#   rotation, the attention, the blends and the joins are all dispatches --
#   so a layer needs no round trip in principle and a token needs one. Three
#   a layer is what the engine does, not what the device asks for.
#
#   Not built. Cutting to one a layer would be about fifteen per cent of a
#   generated token; cutting to one a token, twenty-two.

# 2026-08-29  The engine asks the fence before it waits for one
#
#   The entry above counts sixty-seven submissions a generated token, three
#   a layer, and prices them at twenty-three per cent. The three a layer are
#   structural -- the host normalizes, rotates and joins between the
#   products and holds the activations, so no two of the device's sequences
#   are adjacent. What is not structural is the waiting.
#
#   vkWaitForFences blocks in the driver and what a short dispatch pays
#   there is the wake-up rather than the device's work, which is the worker
#   pool's own finding of this sitting. The engine now asks
#   vkGetFenceStatus up to a thousand times before it waits, and waits as it
#   did when the asking finds nothing.
#
#                                    before    after
#     64 generated on the device     1.667 s   1.583 s   better in 6 of 6
#     1419-token prompt, device      1.975 s   1.886 s   better in 6 of 6
#
#   Six alternated rounds of three, three each way. Five per cent generating
#   and four and a half on a prompt; digests 448c2ed68ec342ee and
#   1a26d24d33b8957b either way. The device's gap goes 2.52 to 2.32 on a
#   prompt and 1.41 to 1.34 generating.
#
#   The budget is not free to choose. vkGetFenceStatus goes into the driver
#   -- it is not a pause -- so a spin long enough to cover a prompt's
#   dispatches costs more than it saves:
#
#     spin      64 generated    1419 prompt
#      100      1.482 s         1.960 s
#     1000      1.475           1.897
#     8000      1.471           2.390
#
#   A thousand covers a generated token's dispatches and gives up long
#   before a prompt's. What it costs is host time the device was not using:
#   a sixty-four token run goes from 0.22 s of processor time to 0.48.
#
#   And it changed what a test could see. A standing stop request is noticed
#   between slices of the wait; a spin that covers a whole short dispatch
#   reaches the wait with the fence already signalled, so the request was
#   never seen and the test said so. The spin now asks the same question
#   every sixty-four turns. Its companion assertion -- that the wait went
#   round more than once -- was written against slices, and the engine now
#   waits in two stages: Waited counts both, because a product the asking
#   answered has waited and saying otherwise would have been false.
#
#   The sitting, device and outside only; the other six groups did not move:
#
#   device: six tokens twelve generated, cpu 0.396 s (0.053 + 0.340, 1.78 s
#     processor) against device 0.296 s (0.041 + 0.256, 0.07 s); 110 tokens
#     cpu 0.385 s for 2.44 s against device 0.180 s for 0.35 s
#
#   outside: model_runner 110 in 0.382 s and 64 in 1.862 s on the processor,
#     0.178 s and 1.514 s on the device; 1419 in 5.163 s and 1.808 s.
#     llama-bench --device none pp110 364.70 pp1419 304.08 tg64 40.38,
#     -ngl 99 pp110 1680.90 pp1419 1820.32 tg64 56.48, -ngl 0 pp110 752.66
#     pp1419 1363.47 tg64 40.42
#
#   The device generating row is ahead of llama.cpp's processor row by more
#   than a rounding for the first time: 42.3 against 40.4.
#
#   load:  0.19 rising to 3.93
#   model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-08-29  A layer's second half in one submission: built, right, slower
#
#   The entry above prices a generated token's sixty-seven submissions at
#   twenty-three per cent and names the three a layer as structural: the
#   host normalizes, rotates and joins between the products, so no two of
#   the device's sequences are adjacent. One of the three was removed. It
#   works, it computes the same answers, and it is slower.
#
#   What was built:
#
#     norm.comp, the root-mean-square normalization, one invocation a
#     position with the sum walked in order and accumulated in binary64 --
#     which is what the processor does, so the two agree bit for bit.
#
#     A third unit on combine.comp: two is not a unit but an addition,
#     which is the residual join. Three lines rather than a kernel, on the
#     same layout and the same two arms in and one out.
#
#     Add_Norm and Add_Join on the sequence, and back-references -- Reads,
#     Reads_Two, At_Vector -- so a step may read one it names rather than
#     the one before it. A fused layer needs all three: both arms of the
#     feed-forward read the normalization four steps back, and the second
#     join reads the first.
#
#     Attend_And_Feed: attention, its projection, the join, the
#     normalization, both arms, the combination, the projection down and
#     the second join. Nine steps, one submission, where the engine made
#     two.
#
#   It is right. Twelve generated tokens on the device answer
#   5abff916f9d83ca6 and sixty-four answer 448c2ed68ec342ee, which are what
#   the published figures answer -- so a normalization computed on the
#   device in binary64 agrees with the processor's to the last bit, which is
#   what walking the sum in order was for.
#
#   And it is forty per cent slower: sixty-four generated tokens 0.367 s
#   against 0.256. Two candidates were measured and are not it. The binary64
#   sum is worth eight per cent of the difference -- the same kernel in
#   binary32 reads 0.365 against 0.398 and answers the same tokens. And the
#   barriers are worth nothing: fencing every reader serialized the two arms
#   of the feed-forward, which used to run together, and publishing instead
#   -- one barrier makes every step before it visible, so a second reader of
#   the same result needs none -- gave back four thousandths of a second.
#
#   So a third of a generated token went somewhere the submission count does
#   not explain, and this is written down rather than guessed at. Not
#   committed.
#
#   Three bugs found on the way, each of which cost an hour and none of
#   which the type system could have caught:
#
#     A pipeline created before its request had an entry point and a layout
#     -- the driver takes the module and faults later, with a stack in
#     libvulkan and nothing in this program to look at.
#
#     A barrier emitted only for a step chained to the one before it, where
#     a step that names an earlier one needs it just as much. Without it the
#     sequence answers, and answers wrongly.
#
#     A normalization handed to the weight loader as its Rows by its
#     Columns, which for a step whose Rows and Columns say what it reads and
#     writes is a square: the loader was asked to upload four million values
#     out of an array of two thousand.
